# Impact and cost-effectiveness of doxycycline post-exposure prophylaxis in Australian men who have sex with men

- Document type: peer_review
- Journal: Nature Communications
- DOI: https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-026-68561-7
- Published: 2026-01-21
- Article ID: s41467-026-68561-7

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 1 -->

Peer Review File
Impact and cost-effectiveness of doxycycline post-exposure
prophylaxis in Australian men who have sex with men
Corresponding Author: Professor Lei Zhang
This file contains all reviewer reports in order by version, followed by all author rebuttals in order by version.
 
Version 0: 
Reviewer comments: 
Reviewer #1 
(Remarks to the Author) 
Summary: 
The authors developed an agent-based network model to simulate the epidemics of three bacterial STIs among GBMSM in
Australia, taking into account demographic, behavioural and AMR-related factors. The model was used to examine the
impact of seven doxy-PEP implementation strategies, focusing on different eligibility criteria, compared with the baseline
case without doxy-PEP, from both epidemiological and economic points of view. 
The study showed that all the strategies were cost-effective, and six out of seven were cost-saving, likely due to the low cost
of doxycycline. More interestingly, one strategy focusing on HIV+ and PrEP-using GBMSM with syphilis diagnosis led to
reduced incidence of gonorrhea with doxycycline-related AMR and had the highest benefit-to-cost ratio. 
There are several major and minor concerns about the model and the results. 
Major comments: 
1. The model had a fixed population size of 10000 GBMSM, and no information was found on new individuals introduced
into the system or mortality rates by age. An individual aged 40-49 would stay in the same age category during the
simulation. This assumption did not consider the possible population growth and demographic shift during such a long
simulation period (2012-2034). How could this assumption affect the epidemics of the three STIs and the impact of doxy-
PEP interventions? 
2. There are several assumptions related to HIV/PrEP status may need more explanation/justification: (1) In the model, the
form of regular partnership was determined by serostatus-based sexual mixing, and 82.5% of HIV- GBMSM preferred a
seroconcordant relationship. However, this assumption may not hold in the era of PrEP and U=U (PMID: 33884510) when
PrEP-using GBMSM can behave differently. This could change the structure of the partnership network with a 30% PrEP
coverage rate. (2) HIV prevalence and PrEP coverage were assumed to be constant in the model, which did not reflect the
increasing uptake of PrEP in real settings. How could the expansion of PrEP affect the partnership network and bacterial STI
epidemics? (3) The model should consider different bacterial STI testing frequencies by HIV or PrEP status, since HIV+
GBMSM and GBMSM on PrEP are recommended for more frequent bacterial STI screening, which can affect the epidemics
of the three STIs. 
3. Could the authors provide more details about the initialization of each bacterial STI prevalence? It seems that individuals
with STIs were selected randomly without considering risk level (e.g., annual number of partners), HIV or PrEP status.
However, studies have shown that bacterial STIs were more prevalent among HIV+ or PrEP-using GBMSM (PMID:
36751651, 30973847). A random initialization of bacterial STI prevalence might underestimate the bacterial STI burdens
among HIV+ or PrEP-using MSM and as a result weaken the effect of the scenario that focusing on this subgroup. 
4. Several sensitivity analyses were performed to reflect the uncertainties regarding doxy-PEP implementation. It might be
helpful to perform additional sensitivity analyses to reflect uncertainties of model assumptions: (1) The authors emphasized
the lack of information on resistance emergence per doxy-PEP use. It seems necessary to perform a sensitivity analysis to
show the difference if this parameter is largely changed; (2) The condom coverage was assumed to be 50% condom for
those who sometimes used condoms without any explanation/justification. How could a different condom coverage affect the
model? 
5. While the study highlights the development of AMR as a critical concern, the long-term implications of resistance on
gonorrhea management and doxy-PEP implementation are not fully explored. Could the authors expand the Discussion
section to address how increasing tetracycline resistance might affect future bacterial STI treatment strategies and public
health recommendations? For instance, how would resistance trends impact doxy-PEP’s long-term viability as a bacterial

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 2 -->

STI prevention tool? 
Minor comments: 
METHODS 
1. Line 173: Should “intervention” be removed in “Eight intervention scenarios” since scenario 1 was the base case without
any interventions? 
2. Line 178: Is it possible to consider the scenarios that provides doxy-PEP to HIV+ and PrEP-using GBMSM separately,
since they have different characteristics and the proportion of GBMSM on PrEP is almost three times larger than HIV+
GBMSM. 
3. Line 218: The derivation of BCRs was not given in the text. What is the benefit in BCRs? 
4. Line 222: The model incorporated the preventive effect of doxy-PEP on gonorrhea susceptible to tetracycline, with an
efficacy of 45% (13%-66%) from a meta-analysis of randomized trials. However, the prevalence of tetracycline-resistant
gonorrhea might play a role in the efficacy of doxy-PrEP on gonorrhea prevention in different settings (PMID: 37018493).
Therefore, it is likely to be an underestimation of the impact of doxy-PEP on tetracycline-susceptible gonorrhea, and 66% as
the upper limit for gonorrhea may not reflect the potential efficacy of doxy-PEP on tetracycline-susceptible gonorrhea. 
RESULTS 
5. Line 233: Did Figure 1 show the mean or the median of the simulations based on 100 parameter sets? The same issues
are for the results provided in the text. 
6. Line 258: Even though the incidence of gonorrhea rebounded after 2028, it was expected to reach an equilibrium after
2034, which should be lower than the 2024 level. This indicated a lower gonorrhea burden even though the prevalence of
tetracycline-resistant gonorrhea significantly increased. 
7. Figure 1: It is interesting that under scenario 5, the reduction (% change) in chlamydia incidence was smaller than
syphilis, even though the efficacy of doxy-PEP on chlamydia was higher. Similar results were observed under the other
scenarios too. It is unlikely to be explained by the higher prevalence or the higher proportion of asymptomatic infections of
chlamydia. Did this mean that the epidemic of chlamydia was less concentrated among the targeted subgroups? Could the
authors provide some insights? 
8. Figure 3: Could the authors provide more details about why the number of doxy-PEP prescriptions dropped after the first
year of implementation under scenario 5 (HIV+ or PrEP-using)? The number of individuals eligible should be constant over
the years since the HIV prevalence and PrEP coverage were fixed. And the uptake rate was also fixed at 75%. 
9. Table 2 and Figure 1: It seems that under scenario 2, the majority of bacterial STI reductions was syphilis (approximately
1800 out of 2687 cases), with approximately 1168 (2687/2.3) doxy-PEP prescriptions. This indicated an extremely high re-
infection rate of syphilis within 6 months of diagnosis, which seems unrealistic (PMID: 27991843). Could the authors provide
more insights? 
10. Line 291: Could the authors provide the overall cost under the base case (scenario 1)? 
DISCUSSION 
11. Line 328: It is worth noting that even though scenario 6 was the most economically viable strategy, it led to the smallest
reduction in bacterial STI incidence. 
12. Line 333: Instead of only pointing out the most prioritized strategy, it could be helpful to provide a list of strategies ranking
by priority in case the budget for doxy-PEP implementation is larger and aims for a higher reduction in bacterial STI
incidence. 
13. Line 382: Higher adherence also led to no reduction on the prevalence of tetracycline resistant gonorrhea under
scenario 6 (Figure S33). 
14. Line 402: The model was calibrated using data from 2012-2023 with static sexual behaviour assigned to each individual
at the beginning of the simulation. However, since the beginning of the COVID-19 pandemic, there might be dramatic
changes in sexual behaviours and access to the healthcare system, which could affect the formation of the partnership
network and screening frequencies of STIs. This needs to be listed as a limitation since it was not considered in the model. 
15. Line 405: Is the conclusion focusing on individuals diagnosed with syphilis or HIV+/PrEP-using individuals diagnosed
with syphilis like the authors mentioned in the first paragraph of the Discussion section? 
Supplemental materials 
16. Algorithm 1.3): When initializing the epidemics of STIs, the authors set all the individuals at exposed stages of
corresponding diseases, which may require further considerations since there should be individuals in each stage of
disease progression at the beginning of the simulation. 
17. Section 1.2.2: The term GCPS was not defined until Section 1.2.3. 
18. Section 1.2.6: Inconsistent gamma distributions between the text and Figure S3. 
19. Section 1.2.7: Is the sex frequency of casual partnership for one partner (maximum duration as two weeks) or all the
causal partnerships in the past year? 
20. Section 1.3: What is the meaning of “have at most one regular partner and/or at most one regular partner”? Does this
indicate each individual can only have at most two partners at each time step? 
21. Algorithm 2: Could the authors provide more details on those who were left in the Ra list (e.g., the percentage of those
who were left without partnership formation and the percentage of HIV+ among them)? 
22. Section 1.4: “Figure S5-7” or “Figure S6-8”? 
23. Section 1.4: bacterial STI transmission through oral sex was not considered in the model. How could this simplification
affect the epidemics of the three STIs in the model? 
24. Section 1.4.4: Given that the STIs are still infectious while under treatment, is the assumption of non-infectious under
treatment due to the recommendation that people should avoid sexual activity without protection? 
25. Algorithm 4.2: It seems that the number of condomless sexual acts defined in Section 1.3.3 was the number of sexual
acts with condom use and the number of weekly sexual acts was overcounted in the inequality. 
26. Section 2.2: The authors acknowledged the stochastic nature of the individual-based model. For each parameter set, the
model can generate different results if running multiple times. Did the authors use the mean of simulated results from
multiple simulations based on one parameter set or use the result from one simulation per parameter set with a fixed random

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 3 -->

generator? 
27. Figure S9: The simulated proportion of NG with tetracycline resistance did not agree with the majority of the data points,
and the data showed a faster increase during 2019-2022. A sensitivity analysis with a larger background rate to develop
tetracycline resistance might be helpful. 
28. Section 3: Is one year long enough to achieve equilibrium in the partnership network? 
29. Section 3.2: The implementation of doxy-PEP usually come along with recommendation of frequent bacterial STI testing,
which were not considered in the model. This could underestimate the impact of doxy-PEP strategy on bacterial STI
preventions. 
30. Section 3.2.1: In the model, doxy-PEP was assumed to be completely ineffective against NG with tetracycline resistance.
However, the DOXYVAC trial showed a moderate efficacy against gonorrhea even though all strains of NG were resistant to
tetracycline. Does this indicate some preventive effect of doxy-PEP on NG with tetracycline resistance? 
31. Section 3.2.2: For clarification, if an individual on doxy-PEP infected with a doxycycline-susceptible NG, is the
probability of developing resistance equal to 2K, accounting for the background resistance development rate? 
32. Section 4.1: It seems that the term “categorized based on AMR susceptibility status” was missing in the description of the
outcome of cumulative number of incidental gonorrhea infections. 
33. Figure S11: It seems that the plot with the title “One syphilis diagnosis” should be for the scenario targeting HIV+ or
PrEP-using GBMSM since it was the only scenario which was not cost-saving. 
34. Sections 5.2.7 and 5.2.8: Could the authors elaborate on the results based on different doxy-PEP efficacy? Specifically,
why did the low efficacy of doxy-PEP lead to a smaller incidence of gonorrhea with doxycycline AMR? For example, under
scenario 5 where the number of individuals on doxy-PEP was not affected by the sensitivity analysis, wouldn’t the low
efficacy lead to a higher incidence of gonorrhea and more GBMSM infected with NG can develop doxycycline resistance
due to doxy-PEP? 
35. Section 5.2.9: Figure S44 showed the results of one-way sensitivity analysis instead of Figure S43. In addition, did the
bar plot show the mean BCR using 100 parameter sets or the results based on one parameter set? Is it possible to provide
the uncertainty range based on the 100 parameter sets? 
(Remarks on code availability) 
The authors did not include the full version of the code, so it is not working. 
Reviewer #2 
(Remarks to the Author) 
Key results: 
The manuscript presents an individual-based model of gonorrhea, chlamydia, and syphilis transmission among gay,
bisexual, and other men who have sex with men (GBMSM) in Australia to estimate the impact of seven different definitions of
eligibility for Doxy-PEP. The eligibility criteria are defined through combinations of HIV status, HIV PrEP use, and STI
history. The study estimated multiple outcome measures for each of definition of eligibility, including impacts on the burden
of STIs, antimicrobial resistance, and the cost-effectiveness of Doxy-PEP. The study suggests that the widest eligibility
criteria led to the greatest reduction in STIs, while the simulations with the most restrictive eligibility criteria (namely, users of
HIV PrEP and people living with HIV/AIDS with a syphilis diagnosis) had the greatest benefit-cost ratio while limiting
antimicrobial resistance in N. gonorrhoeae. 
Validity: 
The study draws valid conclusions from the simulated data. However, some of the assumptions are questionable, the
modeling approach would benefit from additional validation and sensitivity analyses, and the results, discussion, and
conclusions should reflect the uncertainty in the parameters. I’m not sure how best to interpret the base case presented in
Figures 1, for example, in which STI rates are projected to be stable. My understanding is that rates of STIs have been
steadily increasing in Australia. Moreover, the authors report that they used 100 parameter sets, but uncertainty intervals are
lacking from the figures and results. This makes it difficult to understand how to interpret the authors’ results and claims. 
Significance: 
The study addresses an important and timely question, by comparing different Doxy-PEP strategies along multiple policy-
relevant dimensions, including incidence of multiple bacterial STIs, drug resistance in N. gonorrhoeae, and economic costs
and benefits. Results from this study aim to inform the implementation of Doxy-PEP in Australia to help reduce bacterial
STIs. However, questions about the underlying model, its parameterization, and the associated uncertainties make it
challenging to interpret the manuscript’s conclusions. 
Data and methodology 
The modeling approach is appropriate to answer the research question, the model parametrization is mostly grounded in the
literature, and the categories of outcomes examined offer a comprehensive picture of both health and economic implications
of various eligibility definitions. However, the study would benefit from (1) additional validation of the calibration procedure,
including calibration to additional targets, and (2) more extensive sensitivity analysis. 
(1) Parameters and calibration: 
The model was calibrated to positivity rates of gonorrhea, chlamydia, and syphilis tests, and the prevalence of decreased
ceftriaxone susceptibility and tetracycline resistance in a sample of isolates from the Melbourne Sexual Health Centre (lines
161ff, supplement pages 20-22). The authors report calibration results by comparing fitted model outputs to the observed
values of the calibration targets in Figure S9. However, the information provided is insufficient to assess how well the model

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 4 -->

reproduces the total number of infections and diagnoses, which is important since one of the key outcome measures in the
study is the number of infections that may be averted by Doxy-PEP. Specifically: 
- How many isolates from Melbourne Health Centre were tested for antimicrobial resistance and how representative are they
of the gonococcal population in Australia? 
- How well does the model capture the incidence and prevalence for chlamydia, gonorrhea, and syphilis in Australia? It
would be important to include additional calibration targets that account for the total volume of infections and/or testing. 
- The authors claim their model ‘aligns well with actual epidemic trends’ (line 392), but the base case of the model seems to
project unchanging incidence of all three STIs. Could the authors please point to data that support stable incidence for these
STIs in Australia? 
- Given the complexity of sexual behavior that is simulated in the model, it would be helpful to compare the number of
partnerships, the distribution of the numbers of regular and casual partners, or other measures of sexual behavior with those
observed empirically, e.g., those reported by the authors in Figure S3. This would help validate the assumptions of sexual
behavior, which may impact how infections are spread across the population and how well different eligibility criteria for
Doxy-PEP alter those transmission patterns. 
- The authors state that they calibrated the background probability of doxycycline resistance emergence based on data from
2015-2022 and cite Table S4 (section 3.2.2. should cite Table S2 not S4). However, those data are perplexing. From 2015-
18, the proportion with tetracycline AMR is steady around 8%, and then it jumps to around 20% from 2019-21, and then to
30% in 2022. Is it clear what’s driving this change from 2019-22, why it only started in 2019, and whether this driver is
expected to continue unabated? 
- Table S5 reports the doxy-PEP efficacy for preventing gonorrhea at 45%, with a range for sensitivity analysis from 13-66.
However, is the success of doxy-PEP in preventing gonorrhea reflective of the prevalence of resistant strains, rather than an
“intrinsic” likelihood of success? 
(2) Sensitivity analyses 
The study includes extensive sensitivity analysis of parameters related to Doxy-PEP, including varying the length of the
prescription, uptake and adherence in the eligible population, efficacy, and cost parameters (see lines 220-227). However,
the study did not conduct sensitivity analysis on key parameters of the natural history of each STI, sexual behavior, or those
governing the emergence of resistance. Here are some examples of parameters that should be included in such a sensitivity
analysis: 
- For example, the reported values for the proportion of gonococcal infections that are symptomatic varies widely in the
literature. The value of 0.3 in this study seems reasonable but should be varied in sensitivity analyses, as symptom status
impacts diagnosis rates and these in turn impact Doxy-PEP eligibility. 
- Also, as the authors state (e.g., lines 191-192), the probability of doxycycline resistance emerging with the use of Doxy-
PEP is poorly understood. To account for this uncertainty, it would be helpful to explore smaller or larger values than the
0.05% fixed in the analysis. 
- Similarly, a rate of 10% treatment failures for strains with reduced ceftriaxone susceptibility was defined through calibration.
Only very few treatment failures to ceftriaxone have been reported in the literature, so it is unclear which MICs are
associated with treatment failure and at which frequency. Sensitivity analyses should explore whether dynamics change if
this the treatment failure proportion is higher or lower. 
- Another parameter that would benefit from revisiting and from sensitivity analysis is the time until spontaneous recovery,
which was defined to be 33 weeks (see supplement). Recent evidence suggests this is far shorter (see Barbee, 2021 and
Barbee, 2022, natural history studies for both rectal and pharyngeal gonorrhea). 
- Assumptions around sexual behavior are known to play an important role in estimates of the impact of different
interventions produced by individual-based models (see Althaus et al, 2012). As mentioned above, it would be helpful to
understand how well the model reproduces empirically observed partnership structures, and in sensitivity analyses, explore
how changes in the assumed sexual behavior alter the outcomes of different Doxy-PEP eligibility criteria. For example, if
contacts and infections are more (less) concentrated in a subset of the population, do strategies with narrower (wider)
eligibility criteria become more cost-effective? It would also be useful to conduct sensitivity analyses on sexual behavior
parameters, such as the extent of condom use in the population. 
References: 
- Althaus CL, Turner KM, Schmid BV, Heijne JC, Kretzschmar M, Low N. Transmission of Chlamydia trachomatis through
sexual partnerships: a comparison between three individual-based models and empirical data. J R Soc Interface.
2012;9(66):136-146. doi:10.1098/rsif.2011.0131 
- Barbee LA, Soge OO, Khosropour CM, et al. The Duration of Pharyngeal Gonorrhea: A Natural History Study. Clin Infect
Dis. 2021;73(4):575-582. doi:10.1093/cid/ciab071 
- Barbee LA, Khosropour CM, Soge OO, et al. The Natural History of Rectal Gonococcal and Chlamydial Infections: The
ExGen Study. Clin Infect Dis. 2022 May 3;74(9):1549-1556. doi: 10.1093/cid/ciab680. 
Analytical approach 
The study would benefit from some improvements in the analytical approach, including (1) the statistical analysis and
definition of outcomes and (2) the visualization and presentation of the results. 
(1) 
Analysis of outcomes 
The sets of eligibility criteria that the authors compare are relevant and established in the literature and each strategy is
assessed along several dimensions of outcome measures. Health impacts are assessed primarily by the number of averted
infections. However, as strategies differ in the size of the eligible population, it would be helpful to include a measure of

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 5 -->

infections averted normalized by the number of prescriptions. 
In lines 263ff, the authors describe the number of gonorrhea infections with reduced susceptibility to ceftriaxone and
resistance to doxycycline. However, the number of infections alone might not be sufficient to evaluate changes in AMR,
since the strategies produce different numbers of total infections. Including the proportion of infections with decreased
susceptibility or resistance might help better understand the resistance implications of each strategy. 
I would also recommend extending the analysis to explain which characteristics of each strategy are driving the outcomes
(e.g., reduced infections but rising resistance). For example, is it better to prescribe Doxy-PEP broadly or try different
methods of targeting those most likely to a) take prescribed Doxy-PEP and b) be exposed to gonorrhea? Is there a
discernible relationship between the number of individuals who are eligible for Doxy-PEP and the selection for resistance?
For example, in lines 333ff, the authors write: “Our model outcomes suggest that doxy-PEP strategies targeting individuals
diagnosed with syphilis should be prioritised over other strategies, as they provide the best balance between reducing STI
incidence, minimising AMR development, and achieving strong economic returns on investment”. Is this because the
strategy targets a small number of individuals or because it reduces syphilis transmission, specifically? Identifying the
driving forces behind the strategy rankings may help draw more generalizable conclusions from the analysis beyond a
recommendation for a single strategy. 
Finally, I would be interested to see more interpretation of multiple outcomes simultaneously. Currently, the outcomes are
presented as individual analyses and strategy performance is not assessed along all outcome measures jointly (beyond a
subset of outcomes presented in Table 2 and a couple sentences on ideal strategies in lines 299-304 but without much
reference to data). The text would benefit from an exploration of pairs or sets of outcomes, such as the trade-off between
averting infections and reducing antibiotic use. This could also be achieved visually in the form of a scatterplot of strategies
with, say, the number of pills used and the number/percentage of infections averted. 
(2) Visualization and comparison of strategies 
In reading the text, I found it difficult to keep track of the performance of all seven strategies according to different outcome
measures. The manuscript would benefit from a table comparing all the strategies along all outcome dimensions. Table 2
captures some of the outcomes, though with an emphasis on the cost-effectiveness analysis. It would be helpful to have a
similar table with one or two metrics for all outcomes at the beginning of the results section to easily compare the different
strategies. 
I also found some of the visualizations difficult to interpret and provided some comments for each figure below. 
Detailed comments 
- Lines 80-81. From my reading, those studies don’t “underestimate the broader population impact…of doxy-PEP strategies
in reducing multiple STIs,” because the studies don’t evaluate multiple STIs. I suggest rephrasing to accurately reflect that
these studies simply didn’t investigate the impact of doxy-PEP on multiple STIs. 
Methods: 
- It is unclear to me how many simulations were ultimately run to generate the data in the main analysis. In lines 167-170, it
is indicated that 100 parameter combinations were used in the main analysis, but the text does not mention how often each
parameter combination was run and the figures do not report uncertainty intervals. 
- Lines 157-159 describe emergence of resistance but leave out the process by which resistance emerges with the use of
Doxy-PEP. 
When first reading this, I thought doxycycline resistance emerged independently of doxycycline use and this
point was only clarified after reading the supplement. 
- Please clarify in the main text the rate of treatment failures of infections with decreased ceftriaxone susceptibility. In the
supplement, it is stated that treatment failure occurs in just under 10% of cases and that this value was determined by model
calibration. Additionally, is this consistent with empirical data? 
- Line 207f: “Based on previous clinical trials, we assumed doxy-PEP was prescribed as two doses (400 mg) per week,
totalling 52 doses for the six-month prescription duration.” I believe doxyPEP was not prescribed as two doses a week, but
rather for use as needed. The average use was four doses a month in the Luetkemeyer et al. trial. Do the authors account for
additional use of the remaining doses of Doxy-PEP after the 6 months have passed? Do you account for refills of Doxy-PEP
if the prescribed pills are used up more quickly? 
- How is Doxy-PEP use distributed among age groups? 
- Why did you decide to implement random mixing of casual partners (see supplement, algorithm 2)? Does assortative
mixing (e.g., by preferred number of casual partnerships) change the STI transmission patterns? 
- Since modeling by sex act: Did you account for changes in sexual behavior if symptomatically infected with an STI? 
- Is the condom use proportion applied to both regular and casual partnerships? Or is condom use more/less likely in one of
the two partnership types? 
- How do you handle co-infections? Can patients be co-infected with multiple STIs? Would testing for a symptomatic
chlamydia infection also detect an asymptomatic gonorrhea infection? 
- My understanding is that any partner has at most one regular and one casual partner at any time point. Could you please
clarify whether your model allows for greater numbers of simultaneous partnerships? 
- Supplement, page 14 states: “As our study excluded the “no anal intercourse” scenario, we calculated the average

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 6 -->

proportions for the remaining choices and adjusted them to sum to 100% for each partnership type”. Why did you exclude
non-anal sex acts? Does this alter the assumptions you make, for example, about the proportion of infections that are
symptomatic? As the model does not explicitly account for anatomic sites of infection, I don’t understand why you would
need to restrict sexual behavior by site. 
Results: 
- Impacts on incident STIs: The strategies are compared by the number of each STI averted under each strategy (lines 233ff
and Figure 1). Unsurprisingly, the results indicate that strategies with wider eligibility criteria lead to greater absolute
reductions in STIs. In table 2, the authors introduce a new measure of STI reduction that is normalized for the number of
Doxy-PEP prescriptions (new infections averted per doxy-PEP prescription). This is arguably the more interesting outcome,
as it enables a fairer comparison of strategies, and I would recommend mentioning these results in the epidemiological
impacts section, not only in the cost-effectiveness analysis. 
- The base scenario projects 27,801 STIs among 10k individuals in 10 years. i.e., roughly 0.3 cases per person-year. How
does that compare to observed/estimated incidence in Australia? 
- How are the projected infections distributed among the population and how does this distribution compare to real-world
data? This level of validation is important since the study compares strategies that target specific groups. Especially since
the strategy with widest Doxy-PEP use had the highest impact. What if infections are more concentrated in the real-world
population than in the simulation? 
- Lines 233-251. I would recommend presenting results in order of effectiveness of strategies or including a summary table. It
was a bit confusing having to rank the numbers while reading the text. 
- Lines 256-258 (and also Line 321): I enjoyed seeing the performance of the strategies in the context of WHO targets,
though these targets don’t need to be achieved by a single intervention. Instead of saying that “only the strategy prescribing
doxy-PEP to all HIV+ or PrEP-using GBMSM (scenario 5) could achieve the WHO targets”, the authors could mention the
percentage of the targeted reductions that can be achieved by Doxy-PEP alone. 
- In Figure 2, the ordering of strategies is inverted for panels A and B versus panels C and D. Could the authors elaborate on
the tradeoff between selecting for doxycycline-sensitive or doxycycline-resistant strains? 
- Antibiotic use results: Lines 277ff: “In all strategies, the annual doxy-PEP prescriptions and doxycycline pills used by
participants after condomless sexual acts increased rapidly in the first one to two years, then decreased slightly and
stabilised.” Is this driven by the fact that STI diagnoses decline with the use of Doxy-PEP and therefore fewer individuals are
prescribed Doxy-PEP at the point of diagnosis? Would we expect patients to want to continue using Doxy-PEP if it is
working for them? Could the author elaborate on which assumptions are driving the reduction in prescriptions and how those
compare with empirically observed uptake patterns of Doxy-PEP? 
- Sensitivity analyses (lines 305ff and Figures S12-S43): The sensitivity analyses are a reproduction of the main text figures
for variations in some parameters. Comparing the sensitivity analysis figures requires a lot of effort from the reader. The goal
of these sensitivity analyses, as I understand it, is to convey to readers how much each outcome changes with variation in
key parameters, and how much different strategies are impacted by that variation. This might be better achieved through a
summary of the one-way sensitivity analyses, for example in a table or a heatmap of how a single outcome measure (e.g.,
cumulative infections) changes for each strategy with variation in the parameters of the sensitivity analysis (e.g., shorter
prescription). 
- Why was sensitivity analysis of benefit-cost analysis restricted to a single strategy? See: “Focusing on the strategy with the
highest BCR under initial parameter settings—providing doxy-PEP to HIV-positive or PrEP-using individuals diagnosed with
syphilis (scenario 6)—we conducted a one-way sensitivity analysis on the BCRs (Figure S44).” (lines 310-312) 
Figure 1: 
- Brighter colors would improve legibility. 
- As mentioned above, an outcome measure that normalizes for the number of Doxy-PEP prescriptions would help place
these results in context. Could the authors include such a measure in Figure 1 (e.g., the new infections averted per Doxy-
PEP prescription)? 
Figure 2: 
- As mentioned above, Figure 2 could be improved by representing resistance proportions rather than absolute numbers. For
example, by changing the y-axis in panels A-D and/or adding the resistance proportions to the stacked bar plot in panel E.
Another option would be to replace panels A-D with Figure S10 from the supplement. 
- Please use consistent acronyms for the strains, e.g., either ceftriaxone DS or CRO DS. 
Discussion: 
- The paper is missing a discussion of the analysis relative to Traeger et al., 2023. What are the differences or similarities in
estimates of antibiotic use or reduction in infections, in which strategies are optimal, etc. 
- If the goal is to examine strategies targeting syphilis reduction (as in the Australian consensus statement), did the authors
consider strategies where partners of patients with syphilis are prescribed Doxy-PEP? (lines 335ff) 
- Lines 358ff: The recommendation to “maintain the current screening frequency” seems out of place and is not supported by
any of the data in this study. 
- Lines 396ff: “Third, because of limited utility data, we applied lifetime QALY losses per STI rather than stage-specific
losses. Incorporating stage-specific data in future studies could yield more precise QALY estimates.” Could the authors
please expand on this limitation? It seems that with the individual-based model, it should be possible to incorporate disease
stages.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 7 -->

Reference: 
Traeger MW, Mayer KH, Krakower DS, Gitin S, Jenness SM, Marcus JL. Potential Impact of Doxycycline Post-exposure
Prophylaxis Prescribing Strategies on Incidence of Bacterial Sexually Transmitted Infections. Clin Infect Dis 2023; : ciad488.
Minor and editorial comments: 
- Line 295: typo “avertin” instead of “averting” 
- The supplement would benefit from revision, as it is repetitive and hard to follow. For example, on pages 16ff, the models
are described in vague terms and then later the parameter values are introduced. It would be easier if the parameters were
introduced together with the models (or if the model description did not reference vague parameters). For example, mixing
based on seroconcordance is vaguely explained on page 4 (“The formation of regular partnerships adhered to their
requirements for seroconcordance.”) and then later explained again in more detail on page 8. 
- Figure S5: What do you mean by “ratio of condomless sex”?

Version 1: 
Reviewer comments: 
Reviewer #1 
(Remarks to the Author) 
I have reviewed the revised manuscript and the response document. The authors have made structural changes to their
model and they have addressed most of my comments/concerns. However, I still have some minor comments listed below. 
Minor comments: 
1. Please review the manuscript and Supplemental materials for missing/repeated definitions of acronyms. For example,
PrEP was not defined in the Abstract section and GCPS was defined twice in the Supplemental materials. Please also
check the definitions of ANCP, SCC, SSD, CI and ACCESS. 
2. Please review the manuscript and Supplemental materials for label consistency. For example, Supplementary Fig. 16 and
Fig. 1 were used in the main text, but Figure 16 and Figure 1 were used in the figure headings. As another example,
scenario 1 was used in line 325 but Scenario 3 was used in line 328. Please also check the use of Table 1/2 and Table
S3/S4 in the Supplemental materials. 
Manuscript 
3. In the abstract, it is worth noting the large proportion of new gonorrhea cases with HL TetR in the base case, followed by
the differences between the base case and the intervention strategies. 
4. Line 136: It may be worth pointing out the projected incidence rate/proportion of gonorrhea resistant to both drugs under
different strategies, which potentially affects the treatment of gonorrhea (PMID: 36364980). It is interesting to see the
incidence of gonorrhea resistant to both drugs under Scenarios 3 and 4 was similar to the one under Scenario 6 which had
the highest number of doxy-PEP prescriptions. 
5. Multi-criteria evaluation: The ranking for each single indicator makes sense but the ranking for combinations of indicators
seems ambiguous (Figure 4). For example, to prioritize maximal STI reduction and minimal increase in NG with HL TetR,
Scenarios 3 (two STI in 6m), 4 (two STI in 12m) and 6 (PrEP) have the same score of 6 if summing individual indicator ranks
with equal weight. However, Scenario 3 ranks higher than the other two in Figure 4. Similarly, to prioritize maximal STI
reduction and maximal BCR, both Scenario 2 (syphilis) and 3 (two STI in 6m) have the lowest score of 5 but Scenario 2
ranks higher in Figure 4. Supplementary Figures 18–19 seem to better depict the order of implementation between

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 8 -->

strategies: based on the efficiency, Scenario 2 should be prioritized, followed by Scenario 4 if aiming for further STI
reduction, or Scenario 6 if aiming for further syphilis reduction. In addition, is it possible to implement the efficient frontier
analysis to balance the reduction in STIs and the increase in NG with HL TetR/both resistances? 
6. Line 203: Decrease the cumulative incidence of HL TetR gonorrhea or HL TetR only gonorrhea? 
7. Line 229-242: fully addressed 
8. Line 329: The descriptions of Scenarios 5 and 6 are not clear to me. Did the strategies target all HIV+/PrEP-using
GBMSM, or those attending sexual health clinics for doxy-PEP, or doxy-PEP is provided to whoever attends sexual health
clinics? Based on the testing frequencies shown in Supplementary Figure 12, HIV+/PrEP-using individuals attend sexual
clinics at least once per year for asymptomatic screening, which is not clear in the main text. It may be clearer to describe the
scenarios as “…targets all HIV+/PrEP-using GBMSM. Doxy-PEP is offered at sexual health clinics when they seek care for
symptomatic infections or attend for routine screening”. Please make similar changes to Table 1 and throughout the
manuscript/Supplemental materials. 
9. Line 337: Did the authors mean gonorrhea without HL TetR? 
10. Figure 1: Add the incidence rates of chlamydia and syphilis in 2020 in Panels (B) and (C) for consistency. 
11. Figure 3: The title of Panel (D) overlaps with the panel number. 
Supplemental materials 
12. Figure 3: Based on the title, Panel A should show observed annual HIV prevalence as blue points with 95% CIs.
However, I could not see the 95% CIs in Panel A. Are they too small to be observed? 
13. Figure 8: Figure 8 seems like a table instead of a plot. 
14. Line 545: Please add the rationale for assuming that individuals become non-infectious immediately upon commencing
treatment. 
15. Line 554: The first half of the inequality seems to focus on the transmission due to condomed sex acts, but the event
probability did not include the protection efficacy of condom, which showed up in the second half of the inequality focusing
on condomless acts. 
16. Line 594: Is it possible to add the web link of the Australian national statistical databases? 
17. Line 648: Process sexual maturation or Process immigration? 
18. Figure 13: Based on the Population Entries and Exists panel, the annual entries were always larger (over 500) than exits
(<500). How did those model outputs result in the negative net population change in 2020? Some additional explanation can
be helpful. 
19. Figures 14–15: Could the authors use the same upper limit for the y-axis across all panels to better reflect changes in
age-stratified populations over time? 
20. Figure 16: Please add definitions of CT and TP in the figure heading. 
21. Table 2: The explanation for Con_(reg-not PrEP) was the same as the one for Con_(reg-on PrEP). 
22. Line 727: It is worth mentioning that the infection statuses were initialized during a 52-week burn-in period. 
23. Line 809: It seems that κ should be κ_2. 
24. Figure 17: Why did the authors use the coverage of doxy-PEP as the x-axis instead of the number of doxy-PEP
prescriptions, given that the denominator of each coverage varied based on the number of eligible individuals for each
strategy? 
25. Figures 20–23: Would it be possible to use different colors for the bars indicating changes due to increases/decreases in
parameters? In addition, in the figure headings, the authors state that the precise details for all parameter variations are
provided in Table 2. However, Table 2 only includes the base-case values prior to variation, with no vavariation methods. 
(Remarks on code availability) 
The authors provided a README file and we were able to run the code and reproduce the results. 
Reviewer #2 
(Remarks to the Author) 
We thank the authors for their extensive revisions and efforts to respond to the many comments on this manuscript. There
are still a few outstanding issues, including some that have arisen in response to the authors’ revised methods. 
Re Comment 3: 
We thank the authors for adopting our suggestion to calibrate to incidence, but we still have concerns about the calibration
procedure. We were surprised to see the test positivity data dropped from the calibration targets. Why did the authors decide
to calibrate only to incidence, not to both incidence and test positivity as in the prior version? These two data types together
would allow calibration both to care seeking behavior (testing behavior) and transmission (incidence). 
We are perplexed by why the authors ran each simulation just once for each parameter combination. As the authors mention
in the supplement, line 699, individual-based models are “highly stochastic”. That is why multiple simulation iterations are
critical to reduce stochastic noise and effectively compare the 10,000 combinations of input parameters. 
The calibration process needs more description in the manuscript. In the supplement lines 703ff, the authors write: “We
generated 10,000 unique parameter sets by sampling values for each uncertain parameter from its predefined (typically
uniform) distribution.” How were these parameter sets sampled? Did the authors sample the parameter ranges randomly?
Using Latin Hypercube Sampling or a similar strategy could cover the parameter space more efficiently and would free up
computational resources to run the simulation multiple times for each parameter combination. 
Re Comment 6: 
The explanations for stabilizing STI incidence raise further questions. In their response to reviewers, the authors write:

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 9 -->

“Given that the HIV-positive population has historically had a higher burden of these STIs, a reduction in this population size
can contribute to a moderation in overall STI incidence.” Do PLWH have higher STI prevalence because of HIV or because
of behavior? What is causing the decline in HIV? If it is behavior, then we would expect STIs to decline as well, but if it’s
PrEP or ART, then we wouldn’t expect that to impact STIs. 
The authors also write: “Furthermore, the model includes a significant scale-up of PrEP coverage among HIV-negative
GBMSM (Figure R6B), coupled with higher STI testing frequencies among PrEP users (Figure R7). Increased testing leads
to earlier detection and treatment of STIs, which can reduce onward transmission and consequently temper the rise in
incidence or even lead to declines for some infections. It is also plausible that after a period of sustained increase, the
transmission dynamics for certain STIs within specific sexual networks may approach a form of natural equilibrium or
“bottleneck”, especially when influenced by evolving prevention and testing behaviours like those associated with PrEP
uptake.” It is unclear whether these are hypotheses the authors are proposing to explain a real-world stabilization of STI
incidence or whether the authors observe these patterns in their model, i.e., increased STI testing and PrEP coverage. Test
volume, transmission “bottlenecks”, and PrEP coverage could certainly be verified in the model outputs. These should then
be compared to real-world data if used to justify their calibration fit. 
Re Comment 7: 
Our comment has been addressed, assuming figures R16 and R17 include empirical data. This is not completely clear in the
figures. Could the authors more clearly indicate which values in figures R16 and R17 refer to empirical data and from which
data sources they stem? 
Re Comments 9-15 (Sensitivity Analysis): 
Primary comment (required): 
We appreciate that the authors extended the sensitivity analyses to additional parameters. For each parameter, they
considered a range between 0.5 and 1.5 times the value in the main analysis. However, this results in very different effective
parameter ranges. For example, condom use is varied from 0.25 to 0.75 (0.5 +/-50%, while the probability that tetracycline
resistance emerges upon Doxy-PEP use varies from 0.000675 to 0.002025 (0.00135 +/- 50%). Given that both these
parameters are technically bound by 0 and 1, very different ranges of the parameter space are considered. Furthermore, in
the last version of the model, the resistance emergence parameter was calibrated to a value of 0.05, which is not even
included in the range of values considered in the sensitivity analysis. Ranges for parameters such as the treatment failure
rate are similarly narrow. The authors write in their response to reviewers: “For instance, a study (2014, PMID: 25371490)
investigated the persistence of NG DNA following standard treatment. They found that while overall DNA persistence at day
14 post-treatment was around 8% for both pharyngeal and rectal infections, for a small subgroup of isolates (n=10) with
elevated MICs for both ceftriaxone (≥0.06 mg/L) and azithromycin (≥0.5 mg/L), DNA persistence was notably higher at 30%.
This 30% persistence in a specific, potentially harder-to-treat subgroup (though based on a small sample) is considerably
higher than our model’s calibrated TF rate of 7.6% for strains with ceftriaxone DS (where ceftriaxone DS is defined by
ceftriaxone MIC >0.06 mg/L, without a concurrent azithromycin resistance criterion for the TF probability itself). This
comparison suggests our calibrated TF rate is within a plausible range, potentially on the conservative side relative to
specific high-risk scenarios reported in literature.” However, the range of values they consider in the sensitivity analysis is
0.038 to 0.114 (0.076 +/- 50%). If they base their value on the 2014 study, why not cover the full range of values in that study
in the sensitivity analysis, including a 30% treatment failure rate? 
In the main text and in the reviewer response, the authors compare the impact of variations in different parameters, which is
misleading, given the varying ranges of parameter values. For example, in the main text, lines 243ff: “Our comprehensive
one-way sensitivity analyses highlighted that the assumed condom use frequency among individuals who “sometimes” use
condoms (varied 25%–75%; base case: 50%) was the most influential parameter across the four key outcomes.” Also in the
main text, lines 170ff: “While the results were generally robust to variations in most parameters, the assumed condom use
frequency among individuals who “sometimes” use condoms (varied 25%–75%) was the most influential factor on all four
key outcomes.” However, our sense is that the current sensitivity analysis does not allow for this kind of comparison, for the
reasons described above. 
Finally, the authors interpret the impact of changes in parameter values using qualitative terminology, such as describing
effects as “modest”, “moderate”, or “substantial” or stating that results are “robust” to variation in parameters. For example, in
their response to reviewers, the authors write: “Our findings indicate that changing this doxy-PEP-induced resistance
probability within the tested range had a generally modest impact on overall STI reductions and benefit-cost ratios. As
expected, it had a more discernible, though still moderate, influence on the projected increase in HL TetR gonorrhoea
(Figure R10).” We recommend providing either definitions for these terms or, preferably, quantitative results, such as the
difference in the number of infections averted. 
Taking these points together, we are concerned that the sensitivity analyses do not sufficiently support the claims made in
the manuscript. We suggest that the authors consider more realistic plausible ranges for the parameters in the sensitivity
analysis and change the interpretation of the sensitivity analysis. For example, stating which parameter value level is
required to reduce/increase the number of infections averted by some percentage. 
Secondary comments: 
The authors did not include the parameter governing natural clearance of gonococcal infections in the one-way sensitivity

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 10 -->

analysis, arguing that the recent estimates for the duration of infection from Lindley Barbee’s studies (median 9 week
duration of rectal infections and 16 weeks for pharyngeal infections) fall within their gamma distribution centered around 33
weeks (with 95% CI of 16.5-55.2 weeks). They write: “Because our model already incorporates this significant variability by
sampling from this comprehensive distribution for each infection, a separate one-way sensitivity analysis on this parameter
was not performed.” However, the studies by Barbee showed that the *median* duration of infection is shorter for pharyngeal
and rectal infections, not that short infections can occur in the tail of the distribution of durations. Shifting the gamma
distribution in their model to consider distributions centered on shorter median durations may impact the analysis. It is
therefore perplexing that the authors did not include this parameter in sensitivity analyses. 
In the response to reviewers, the authors write: “Regarding the concentration of infections: As a proxy to explore sensitivity to
risk concentration, we varied the initial STI prevalence in the high-risk subgroups (HIV-positive and PrEP-using individuals),
multiplying the calibrated base-case values by 0.5 and 1.5. We found that variations in these initial subgroup prevalences
had a generally modest impact on overall STI and syphilis reduction.” The starting prevalence of STIs should not have much
impact on how concentrated infections are in the population after the burn-in period. Instead, the authors might consider
changing the heterogeneity in contact rates in the population. How concentrated infection are should also be directly
measured, for example by counting the number of people infected and the number of infections or calculating gini
coefficients. Distribution of infections should be examined by groups targeted in the eligibility criteria and compared with
real-world data. 
Re Comment 19: 
The scatterplot of the number of tetracycline resistant infections for varying Doxy-PEP uptake levels is helpful. 
The authors do not explain how the efficient frontier was calculated. 
The “multi-criteria evaluation framework” is helpful. However, the four single-outcome measures should be separated both
visually and in the interpretation from the multi-criteria outcome measures. How were the combinations determined? Some
combinations of outcomes are missing. This should be explained more clearly in the text. 
In the response to reviewers, the authors write: “Its effectiveness thus stems from efficiently targeting high-transmission
networks with a relatively modest number of prescriptions.” Did they verify this statement quantitatively in their model? For
example, which proportion of persons with high partner change rates receive a Doxy-PEP prescription under the syphilis
strategy relative to other strategies? 
Re Comment 22: 
It is highly unusual to run only a single simulation run for each of 100 parameter combinations, given the stochastic noise
associated with individual-based models. Please consider running more iterations for each parameter combination to better
capture the range of possible scenarios, including variation in contact networks, STI transmission, emergence of resistance,
etc. 
Also, the labels on figures need to be corrected to read “average” for all outcomes. 
Re Comment 34: 
Please see our response to the prior comments 9-15 mentioning the sensitivity analysis for risk concentration. 
Re Comment 37: 
We recommend that the authors elaborate on the trade-off between minimizing ceftriaxone resistance vs doxycycline
resistance in the main text, i.e., the fact that strategies that optimize ceftriaxone resistance produce higher doxycycline
resistance (and dual resistance). 
Re Comment 42: 
The comment is partially addressed. There is now a sentence mentioning the Traeger et al. paper, but the discussion of the
paper is very limited. 
Re Comment 44: 
We thank the authors for removing the recommendation to “maintain the current screening frequency”. 
Re Comment 45: 
The comment has been addressed. The explanation that there are insufficient data on stage-specific utility values in the
Australian context is convincing. It might be worth stating this reason directly in the text. 
Re Minor Comment 2: 
This comment has not been addressed. The supplement is longer in the revised version than in the prior version (41 pages
now, 30 pages in the prior version) and vague references remain. For example: 
- Lines 756ff: “To simulate the first impact, we applied efficacy values from a meta-analysis of doxy-PEP. For the second

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 11 -->

impact, we adapted a probability   2 (Table 2) derived from model calibration to represent the likelihood of resistance
development with each use of doxy-PEP.” 
o Without mentioning the efficacy values and explaining the parameter, this statement does not help explain the model. 
- Table 2, parameter “HIV positive rate” 
o What does this mean? If it is a rate, then over what time frame and population (e.g., annual decrease in HIV positivity?) Or
is it the proportion of the population that is HIV positive? 
- Lines 596ff and 606ff are highly repetitive: “The model’s ability to reflect these demographic shifts has been validated by
comparing its population size and age structure outputs against expected national trends. The model’s ability to accurately
reflect these demographic shifts, including the number of population flows, net changes, total population size, and age
specific population numbers and proportions, has been validated by comparing its outputs against expected national trends,
as illustrated in Supplementary Figures 13–15.” (…) “Similarly, STI status (for gonorrhoea, chlamydia, and syphilis, including
relevant gonococcal antimicrobial susceptibility profiles for immigrants) is assigned based on the current time-varying
prevalence and stage distribution of each infection within the model for immigrants, while newly maturing individuals are
initialized as susceptible. Other critical behavioural attributes, such as preferred partnership types, annual number of casual
partners (ANCP), age-specific sexual activity frequencies, and condom use preferences, are assigned using established
model protocols.” 
- Line 456f: “For each infection, we identified partnerships where individual i was in the susceptible stage and the other
individual j was in an infectious stage, and they had scheduled sexual acts within this cycle (week).” 
o The authors don’t explain which compartments for each STI are infectious and thus contribute to the force of infection. 
- Lines 452f in section 1.4.1 “Overview of gonorrhoea model”: “For model calibration, we utilized antimicrobial susceptibility
data, defining ceftriaxone DS and HL TetR with minimum inhibitory concentrations (MICs) exceeding 0.06 mg/L and 8 mg/L,
respectively. These thresholds were adopted in our model to define the antimicrobial susceptibility profiles of the infections.” 
o This reference to the calibration procedure seems out of place in a section describing the compartmental model structure
and does not provide the required information to understand the calibration process, as it does not describe the methods
used, data sources, calibration parameters etc. It raises more questions and does not refer the reader to the calibration
section of the supplement. 
- Lines 365, section 1.3.3 “Schedule sexual acts”: this section describes the parameters twice in short succession, first as
factors 1, 2, and 3, then with the mathematical expressions. 
The lack of clarity in the methods description limits reproducibility of the model and transparency of the study. The
supplement would still benefit from considerable revision to make the methods description more concise and more clearly
structured.

Version 2: 
Reviewer comments: 
Reviewer #1 
(Remarks to the Author) 
The authors have made extensive efforts to address my comments on the manuscript and the supplementary materials for
the second time. Their responses are generally thorough, and the added explanations and edits have strengthened the
overall quality of the work. Only a few minor issues remain to be addressed, primarily related to formatting. 
1. Re Minor Comment 1: The authors have reviewed the use of acronyms and have corrected most of them. However, there
are a few issues remaining: (1) SCC was only defined in Supplementary Figure 4, but not in the text of the supplementary
materials; (2) CI was not defined in Section 1.2.3 of the supplementary materials as the authors mentioned; (3) Full terms
were used after acronyms were defined (e.g., infections averted instead of IA was used in the Discussion section, and
Neisseria gonorrhoeae was used in the Supplementary materials). In addition, is it appropriate to use confidence interval,
which is commonly used to estimate an unknown statistical parameter, to represent the uncertainties of simulated results
(PMID: 35255860)? 
2. Multiple revisions were not marked in red in the manuscript or the supplementary materials. For example, the second
sentence that was added to the manuscript based on Minor Comment 4, and the revisions in Supplementary Table 3 based
on Minor Comment 21. 
3. Re Minor Comment 6: Based on the revised Figure 2, it is unclear whether the trade-off effect between the increased
gonorrhea infections with HL TetR and the decreased infections with Ceftriaxone DS would sustain beyond 2034, since the

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 12 -->

incidence rates of infections with both resistances were much higher than those with only Ceftriaxone DS by 2034. In
addition, the CIs for the decreased infections with Ceftriaxone DS seem comparable across all intervention strategies by
2034. It is important to emphasize the temporality of the results, or choose a different outcome for comparison. 
4. Re Minor Comment 11: In the revised Figure 3, “(D)” still slightly overlaps with “Two”. 
5. Re Minor Comment 24: The authors clarified that doxy-PEP coverage was defined as the proportion of all GBMSM using
doxy-PEP, rather than as the proportion among eligible individuals. However, inconsistent descriptions as doxy-PEP
coverage percentage among eligible individuals can still be found in the titles of Figure 1 and Supplementary Figure 16.
