Response to the comments from Reviewer 1 
Summary: 
The authors developed an agent -based network model to simulate the epidemics of three bacterial STIs 
among GBMSM in Australia, taking into account demographic, behavioural and AMR -related factors. The 
model was used to examine the impact of seven doxy -PEP implementation strategies, focusing on different 
eligibility criteria, compared with the baseline case without doxy -PEP, from both epidemiological and 
economic points of view. 
Response: 
Thank you for your thoughtful review of our manuscript. We have carefully considered the points raised 
and have addressed them in our revisions. 
In our point-by-point response below, the key points are formatted in bold. Any new or revised text that 
has been added to the manuscript or supplementary materials is quoted and highlighted in red for clarity. We 
believe these revisions, guided by your valuable feedback, significantly strengthen the manuscript. 
Major comment 1: 
The model had a fixed population size of 10000 GBMSM, and no information was found on new 
individuals introduced into the system or mortality rates by age. An individual aged 40 -49 would stay in the 
same age category during the simulation. This assumption did not consider the possible population growth 
and demographic shift during such a long simulation period (2012-2034). How could this assumption affect 
the epidemics of the three STIs and the impact of doxy-PEP interventions?  
Response: 
We sincerely appreciate the reviewer for identifying the limitations of our initial model, which assumed 
a fixed population size of 10,000 GBMSM and did not account for new individuals, age-specific mortality, or 
individual aging. We acknowledge that these assumptions could affect the simulation of STI epidemics and 
the evaluation of doxy-PEP interventions over the extended period of 2012-2034. 
To address this critical point, we have significantly enhanced our model by incorporating dynamic 
population processes . The revised model now simulates natural population turnover through several 
mechanisms. We model individuals entering the population upon reaching sexual maturity (age 15, 
transitioning from age 14) and exiting due to aging (age 65, transitioning from age 64). We also incorporate 
age-specific mortality rates to reflect natural population decline. Recognizing Aust ralia’s migration patterns, 
we have further integrated age -specific immigration and emigration. These five demographic flows, which 
collectively define our population dynamics, are illustrated in Figure R1. 
 
Figure R1. Schematic diagram of population inflow and outflow incorporated into the model.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 15 -->

To implement these updates, we utilized age -specific data from Australia’s national statistical 
databases (https://dataexplorer.abs.gov.au/). Specifically, we collected data on the male population size 
(aged 15-64, in 5-year groups), mortality rates, and immigration/emigration numbers between 2012 and 2022. 
Our population age structure  processing involved several steps. First, we initialized the  age of our 10,000 
agents based on the age -specific male population proportions in 2012, using a gamma distribution  
 1.71,40 ,15 64X Gamma X 
. Second, we calculated the rates of GBMSM newly entering the model 
due to sexual maturity based on the annual num ber of males aged 14 between 2012 and 2022. Third, we 
calculated the rates of GBMSM entering or exiting the model due to immigration and emigration based on 
age-specific annual numbers between 2012 and 2022. Finally, we applied the collected age-specific mortality 
rates to simulate deaths within the model. Separately, individuals also leave the model upon reaching the age 
of 65, which represents the upper limit of our defined sexually active age range. 
To validate the accuracy of these demographic updates, we compared the model ’s population 
dynamics with expected population size. We calculated the expected population size between 2013 and 2023 
by multiplying the ratios of the age -specific population size in each year (relative to 2012) with our initial 
population of 10,000. The population flows and the comparison between the model outputs and the expected 
population numbers and proportions are presented in Figures R2-R4. These comparisons demonstrate that the 
model outputs exhibit similar ma gnitude and trends to the collected national data between 2012 and 2023.  
Due to the lack of official data for projecting population structure changes beyond 2023, and acknowledging 
the inherent unpredictability of future demographic shifts, we have revised  our approach for modelling 
population dynamics from 2024 onwards. Instead of using only the 2023 parameters, we now calculate the 
average annual demographic change parameters based on the observed data from the 2012-2023 period. These 
averaged parameters are then applied to model the population dynamics for the year 2024 (to reach the 
population state at the beginning of 2025). Subsequently, for the 10-year Doxy-PEP intervention period (2025-
2034), these demographic parameters, derived from the 2012-2023 average, are held constant. 
 
Figure R2. Number of population flows, changes, and the comparison of total population size.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 16 -->

Figure R3. Comparison of age-specific population numbers. 
 
Figure R4. Comparison of age-specific population proportions. 
Beyond age, we assigned additional relevant characteristics to individuals newly entering the model 
through sexual maturity or immigration.  First, HIV status was determined based on the time -varying 
proportion of HIV+ individuals in the model for immigrant s, while all individuals entering due to sexual 
maturity were assigned as HIV-. Second, among new HIV- individuals, PrEP use status was assigned according 
to the time-varying proportion of PrEP users within the HIV  negative population in the model. Additionally, 
the infection status (positive or negative) for syphilis, gonorrhoea (including antimicrobial susceptibility), and 
chlamydia was assigned based on the time-varying prevalence of each infection within the model. The specific 
infection stage (e.g., exposed, asymptomatic, symptomatic) was determined using the methodology previously 
detailed in the supplementary materials. Furthermore, other behavioural features, such as the annual number

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 17 -->

of casual partners, age-specific frequency of sex acts, and condom use during anal intercourse, were assigned 
based on the process previously described in the supplementary materials. 
Through the processes outlined above, we have aimed to incorporate realistic population structure updates 
into our model to reflect real -world demographic changes as accurately as possible. We believe that these 
measures have addressed the reviewer’s concerns regarding the update of the age structure in our long -term 
model simulation. 
We mentioned the demographic update in the manuscript as follows: 
“The model features an evolving sexual partnership network formed by an initial population of 10,000 
individuals, which is subject to demographic flows including sexual maturation, immigration, aging out, 
mortality, and emigration.” 
We also included these information to the supplementary as following text and algorithm: 
“1.5 Population dynamics and annual update 
To enhance the realism of long-term epidemic simulations and address limitations of a fixed population, 
our individual -based model incorporates dynamic population processes. The model, which initiates with 
10,000 individuals, simulates natural population turnover through several key demographic mechanisms that 
occur annually. These include the entry of individuals into the sexually active population (defined as ages 15-
64) via sexual maturation (at age 15) and age -specific immigration, as well as th e exit of individuals due to 
aging out (upon reaching age 65), age -specific mortality, and age -specific emigration. The rates for these 
demographic flows are derived from Australian national statistical databases, covering data on male 
population size by a ge group, mortality rates, and immigration/emigration numbers, primarily from 2012 to 
2022. The model’s ability to reflect these demographic shifts has been validated by comparing its population 
size and age structure outputs against expected national trends. The model’s ability to accurately reflect these 
demographic shifts, including the number of population flows, net changes, total population size, and age -
specific population numbers and proportions, has been validated by comparing its outputs against e xpected 
national trends, as illustrated in Supplementary Figures 13–15. 
Individuals newly entering the simulated population, whether through sexual maturation or immigration, 
are assigned a comprehensive set of characteristics to integrate them into the on going epidemic and network 
dynamics. For individuals maturing into the population (turning 15), HIV status is initialized as negative, 
while for immigrants, it is determined by the current HIV prevalence within the model. PrEP status for new 
HIV-negative individuals is assigned based on current PrEP coverage rates in the model ’s HIV-negative 
population. Similarly, STI status (for gonorrhoea, chlamydia, and syphilis, including relevant gonococcal 
antimicrobial susceptibility profiles for immigrants) is assigned based on the current time-varying prevalence 
and stage distribution of each infection within the model for immigrants, while newly maturing individuals 
are initialized as susceptible. Other critical behavioural attributes, such as preferred partnership types, annual 
number of casual partners (ANCP), age-specific sexual activity frequencies, and condom use preferences, are 
assigned using established model protocols. Due to the unavailability of official data for projecting detailed 
demographic changes beyond 2023, average annual demographic parameters derived from the 2012 -2023 
period are used for the year 2024 and then held constant for the 10-year doxy-PEP intervention period (2025-
2034). The detailed step-by-step process for this annual population update is described in Algorithm 5. 
Algorithm 5. Population update (Performed annually) 
 
1. Age all individuals: Increment age of all individuals by one year. 
2. Process age-outs (exit): 
a) Identify individuals now aged 65.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 18 -->

b) For each individual aging out: 
i. Dissolve all their existing regular and casual partnerships. 
ii. Remove the individual from the population. 
3. Process mortality (exit): 
a) For each individual, determine if mortality occurs based on their current age and age -specific 
mortality rates. 
b) For each individual who dies: 
i. Dissolve all their existing regular and casual partnerships. 
ii. Remove the individual from the population. 
4. Process emigration (exit): 
a) Identify individuals emigrating based on their current age and age-specific emigration rates. 
b) For each individual emigrating: 
i. Dissolve all their existing regular and casual partnerships. 
ii. Remove the individual from the population. 
5. Update partnership availability for remaining partners: 
a) Identify individuals whose regular or casual partner exited the population (due to steps 2, 3, or 
4). 
b) Return these individuals to the appropriate availability lists (Ra or Ca) if they still meet criteria 
for seeking new partnerships. 
6. Process sexual maturation (new entries): 
a) Determine the number of new 15-year-old individuals to add based on national demographic data. 
b) For each new 15-year-old individual: 
i. Assign HIV status (HIV-negative). 
ii. Assign PrEP status (based on current PrEP coverage among HIV-negative individuals in the 
model). 
iii. Assign STI status (susceptible to all STIs). 
iv. Assign other behavioural and demographic features according to established model 
distributions. 
7. Process sexual maturation (new entries): 
a) Determine the number and age distribution o f new immigrating individuals based on national 
demographic data. 
b) For each new immigrating individual: 
i. Assign age. 
ii. Assign HIV status (based on current HIV prevalence in the model population). 
iii. If HIV-negative, assign PrEP status (based on cur rent PrEP coverage among HIV-negative 
individuals in the model).

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 19 -->

iv. Assign STI status for gonorrhoea, chlamydia, and syphilis, including gonococcal AMR 
profiles (based on current STI prevalence and stage/AMR distributions in the model population). 
v. Assign other behavioural and demographic features as per step 6.b.iv.” 
 
Major comment 2: 
There are several assumptions related to HIV/PrEP status may need more explanation/justification: (1) In 
the model, the form of regular partnership was determined by serostatus-based sexual mixing, and 82.5% of 
HIV- GBMSM preferred a seroconcordant relationship. However, this assumption may not hold in the era of 
PrEP and U=U (PMID: 33884510) when PrEP-using GBMSM can behave differently. This could change the 
structure of the partnership network with a 30% PrEP coverage rate. (2) HIV prevalence and PrEP coverage 
were assumed to be constant in the model, which did not reflect the increasing uptake of PrEP in real settings. 
How could the expansion of PrEP affect the partnership network and bacterial STI epidemics? (3) The model 
should consider different bacterial STI testing frequencies by HIV or PrEP status, since HIV+ GBMSM and 
GBMSM on PrEP are recommended for more frequent bacterial STI screening, which can affect the epidemics 
of the three STIs. 
Response: 
We acknowledge that more nuanced modelling of HIV/PrEP status would enhance simulation realism. 
Below, we address each concern raised: 
Firstly, our previous model considered differing serosorting preferences only between HIV+ and HIV- 
individuals, without distinguishing between PrEP users and non-users. Based on the study provided by the 
reviewer (PMID: 33884510), we have incorporated more nuanced serosorting preferences . Figure R5 
summarizes the proportions of individuals who report requiring or not requiring known seroconcordance for 
their partners. These proportions now differ among HIV+, PrEP -using, and non -PrEP-using individuals. 
Individuals requiring seroconcordance can only form partnerships with others of ma tching serostatus . We 
revised the supplementary as follows: 
“Based on data adapted from published study, we set the proportions of individuals requiring 
seroconcordant regular partners as follows: 41.5% for HIV -positive individuals, 79.7% for HIV -negative 
PrEP-using individuals, and 89.9% for HIV-negative PrEP non-using individuals.” 
 
Figure R5. Summary of the elements and process related to the serosorting in the formation of regular 
partnerships.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 20 -->

Secondly, we now incorporate decreasing HIV prevalence and increasing PrEP coverage over time, 
informed by empirical data  from the GBQ+ Community Periodic Surveys (GCPS) study . GCPS study is an 
annual community -based survey among the GBMSM population in Australia  
(https://www.unsw.edu.au/research/csrh/our-projects/gbq-community-periodic-surveys). Data from the GCPS 
study show that the proportion of participants living with HIV significantly decreased over time (blue points 
in Figure R6A). As mentioned in our response to Major Comment 1 , we assign HIV status to immigrants 
based on the time -varying proportion of people living with HIV observed in the data, while newly sexually 
mature individuals are assumed to be HIV  negative. Interestingly, with these settings and an initial 
prevalence of 9.5% (estimated by linea r regression of the real data), the trend of HIV prevalence 
emerging from our simulation (red points in Figure R6A ) closely resembles the observed decline. 
Therefore, we have opted not to impose an explicit external trend for HIV prevalence, instead allowi ng the 
model’s demographic and epidemiological dynamics to drive the decreasing prevalence over time , which is 
aligned with the trend in reality . We simulated the inclusion of new PrEP users according to a logistic 
curve fitted to the real data (Figure R6B), which shows PrEP coverage increasing following an S -shaped 
trajectory. After excluding two potential outlier data points in 2021 -2022 potentially influenced by COVID -
19, the logistic function provided a good fit to the data, with an R -squared (R2) value higher than 0.8 and a 
Root Mean Square Error (RMSE) less than 10. Using this function, we initialize PrEP coverage at 0.2% in the 
model and then simulate increasing coverage by annually enrolling additional eligible individuals onto PrEP 
to reach the targ et coverage predicted by the fitted curve. For this enrolment process, new PrEP users are 
sampled from HIV- PrEP non -users based on probabilities weighted by their Annual Number of Casual 
Partners (ANCP). Specifically, the probability for each eligible ind ividual is calculated by dividing their 
individual ANCP by the total ANCP across all eligible individuals in the model. We revised the supplementary 
as follows: 
“In this study, individuals’ HIV status was categorized as either positive or negative, excluding unknown 
statuses. At the simulation’s start in 2012, the initial proportion of individuals living with HIV was set to 9.5%, 
a value derived from linear regression of GCPS data spanning 2014-2023(Supplementary Figure 3, Panel A). 
While HIV transmission i tself is not explicitly modelled, the overall HIV prevalence within the simulated 
population changes dynamically over the simulation period. This dynamic prevalence is influenced by the 
initial rate, the characteristics assigned to individuals entering the  population (newly sexually mature 
individuals are initialized as HIV-negative, while immigrants are assigned an HIV status based on the time -
varying proportion of people living with HIV in the model at their time of entry), and demographic exits 
(Algorithm 5). This approach allows the model to reproduce observed trends, such as the declining proportion 
of GBMSM living with HIV in Australia over recent years. The model continues to account for differences in 
condom use, the annual number of casual partners,  and serostatus-based sexual mixing preferences between 
HIV-positive and HIV-negative individuals.” 
“PrEP coverage among HIV -negative individuals is not treated as a constant characteristic but instead 
changes dynamically over the simulation period to reflect observed uptake patterns in Australia. We modelled 
this changing coverage by fitting a logistic f unction to national Gay Community Periodic Surveys (GCPS) 
data on PrEP use from 2014 to 2023 (see Supplementary Figure 3, Panel B). This results in an initial PrEP 
coverage of approximately 0.2% among HIV -negative individuals in 2012, which then increases annually 
according to the fitted logistic curve, reaching approximately 32.1% by 2024 and stabilizing around 32.3% by 
2034. In the model, this increasing coverage is achieved by annually transitioning a proportion of HIV -
negative PrEP non-users to PrEP user status, with individuals selected for this transition based on probabilities 
weighted by their Annual Number of Casual Partners (ANCP). New HIV -negative individuals entering the 
population (either through sexual maturation or immigration) are also assigned PrEP status based on this time-
varying coverage rate for the respective year. The model continues to account for differences in condom use

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 21 -->

and the annual number of casual partners between PrEP users and non-users among HIV-negative individuals.” 
 
Figure R6. Simulation and calibration of the changing HIV prevalence and PrEP coverage over time.  
Panel (A) shows the observed annual HIV prevalence among Australian GBMSM from the Gay Community 
Periodic Surveys (GCPS) for 2012-2023 (blue points with 95% CIs) and the corresponding mean HIV average 
prevalence emerging from our model simulations (red points). The  model was initialized with 9.5% HIV 
prevalence in 2012, and the subsequent trend is driven by the model's dynamic population turnover rather than 
an imposed external trend. Panel (B) shows the observed annual PrEP coverage among HIV-negative GBMSM 
from the GCPS for 2012 -2023 (blue points). The dashed line represents the logistic curve fitted  to the solid 
data points, which is used to model the PrEP uptake trend in our simulation. The two hollow data points (for 
2021-2022) were excluded from the curve fitting process as potential outliers likely influenced by the COVID-
19 pandemic. The latest national data shown for both panels is from 2023. 
Thirdly, we agree that assigning individuals ’ STI screening frequencies without considering their HIV 
status and PrEP use is not appropriate. Thus, we have now assigned different STI screening frequencies for 
HIV+, PrEP-using, and non-PrEP-using individuals based on empirical data (Figure R7). We collected 
annual per capita STI testing frequencies between 2012 and 202 3 from the Australian Collaboration for 
Coordinated Enhanced Sentinel Surveillance (ACCESS) system. These data were specific to different 
populations (HIV+, PrEP-using, and non-PrEP-using individuals) and showed a decrease during the COVID-
19 period across all groups. Upon re-examination of these data, we observed that testing frequencies for HIV+ 
individuals and HIV- PrEP non-users both remained relatively stable over the observed period. Therefore, for 
these two groups, we calculated their respective average annual testing frequencies (approximately 203.9 
tests/100 person-years for HIV + individuals and approximately 116.4 tests/100 person-years for PrEP non -
users) and have applied thes e as constant rates throughout the simulation period.  In contrast, testing 
frequencies for HIV- PrEP users demonstrated a clear increasing trend over time. Consequently, we have fitted

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 22 -->

a logistic curve to the ACCESS data specifically for PrEP users. The values derived from this fitted curve are 
used to represent the changing STI testing frequencies for PrEP users in the model.  This revised approach 
more accurately captures the distinct STI testing patterns observed for each of these key populations.  We 
revised the supplementary as follows: 
“To accurately reflect real -world healthcare engagement, our model incorporates differential annual 
frequencies for asymptomatic STI testing, stratified by three key GBMSM subgroups: HIV -positive 
individuals, HIV -negative PrEP-using individuals, and HIV -negative PrEP non -using individuals. The 
underlying data for these testing frequencies were sourced from the Australian Collaboration for Coordinated 
Enhanced Sentinel Surveillance (ACCESS) system, covering the period from 2012 to 2023. These empirically 
derived annual per capita STI testing rates inform the scheduling of asymptomatic screening events for 
individuals within our simulation. 
The specific modelling approach for determining annual testing frequencies varies by subgroup to capture 
observed trends, as illustrated in Figure 10. For HIV-positive individuals and for HIV -negative PrEP non -
users, observed testing frequencies remained relatively stable across the 2012-2023 period; therefore, for these 
two groups, we calculated and applied their respective average annual testing frequencies (approximately 
203.9 tests per 100 person-years for HIV-positive individuals and approximately 116.4 tests per 100 person -
years for HIV -negative PrEP non -users) as constant rates th roughout the simulation. In contrast, testing 
frequencies for HIV-negative PrEP users demonstrated a clear increasing trend over time; consequently, for 
this subgroup, we fitted a logistic curve to the ACCESS data, and the values derived from this fitted c urve 
represent the dynamically changing annual STI testing frequencies for PrEP users in the model.” 
 
Figure R7. Simulation and calibration of the changing STI testing rates over time. 
Major comment 3: 
Could the authors provide more details about the initialization of each bacterial STI prevalence? It seems 
that individuals with STIs were selected randomly without considering risk level (e.g., annual number of 
partners), HIV or PrEP status. However, studies have shown that bacterial STIs were more prevalen t among 
HIV+ or PrEP -using GBMSM (PMID: 36751651, 30973847). A random initialization of bacterial STI 
prevalence might underestimate the bacterial STI burdens among HIV+ or PrEP -using MSM and as a result 
weaken the effect of the scenario that focusing on this subgroup.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 23 -->

Response: 
We appreciate the reviewer’s comment regarding the initialization of bacterial STI prevalence. We agree 
that randomly assigning STIs uniformly across the population would not reflect the well-established disparities 
in STI burden am ong different groups of GBMSM, particularly among HIV+ and PrEP -using individuals, 
who have higher STI prevalence or incidence (as highlighted by PMIDs 36751651 and 30973847). 
To address this, we refined our model to set distinct initial prevalences for gonorrhoea, chlamydia, and 
syphilis across three key GBMSM subgroups: HIV -positive, HIV -negative PrEP -using, and HIV -
negative PrEP non-using individuals. For each of these three groups, we independently set and calibrated 
distinct initial prevalences for gonorrhoea, chlamydia, and syphilis at the start of the simulation in 2012. This 
resulted in a total of nine specific initial STI prevalence parameters that were estimated during model 
calibration to best reflect early epidemic conditions within each subgroup. Following this stratified approach, 
initial infections for each STI were assigned by randomly selecting individuals within each of the three 
population groups according to their respective nine calibrated initial prevalences. This method ensures th at 
the initial distribution of STIs at the start of the simulation appropriately reflects the differing burdens among 
these key GBMSM subgroups, as highlighted by the reviewer. We revised the supplementary as follows: 
“After running the model for one year to achieve an equilibrium in the partnership network (as detailed 
in Algorithm 1), we independently initialized each STI. This was performed using a stratified approach, where 
individuals were randomly selected to have an initial infection based on nine di stinct, calibrated initial 
prevalence parameters—one for each of the three STIs across our three defined GBMSM subgroups (HIV -
positive, HIV -negative PrEP -using, and HIV -negative PrEP non -using). The values for these initial 
prevalences are detailed in Supplementary Table 2.” 
Our model incorporates the annual number of casual partners (ANCP) as a key individual attribute 
recognized to influence STI risk. While the initial assignment of STIs is stratified by HIV and PrEP status 
using the nine group-specific calibrated prevalences, we acknowledge that ANCP could theoretically provide 
a further layer for refining this initial seeding of infections. However, to maintain modelling simplicity at the 
initialization stage, ANCP was not used as an additional, direct stratification factor for determining which 
individuals initially have an STI. Instead, the crucial role of ANCP in determining an individual’s STI risk is 
captured dynamically throughout the simulation: higher ANCP values directly translate to increased sexual 
contact rates within the model, thereby naturally influencing an individual ’s likelihood of acquiring 
and transmitting STIs as the simulated epidemics progress over time. 
Major comment 4: 
Several sensitivity analyses were performed to reflect the unc ertainties regarding doxy -PEP 
implementation. It might be helpful to perform additional sensitivity analyses to reflect uncertainties of model 
assumptions: (1) The authors emphasized the lack of information on resistance emergence per doxy-PEP use. 
It seems necessary to perform a sensitivity analysis to show the difference if this parameter is largely changed; 
(2) The condom coverage was assumed to be 50% condom for those who sometimes used condoms without 
any explanation/justification. How could a different condom coverage affect the model? 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for highlighting the importance of exploring the impact of key model assumptions 
through additional sensitivity analyses. We have now conducted these analyses to assess the influence of 
uncertainties regarding: (1) the rate of resistance emergence associated with doxy-PEP use, and (2) the 
assumed condom use frequency among individuals reporting “sometimes” using condoms. The impact 
of these variations was assessed on four key outcomes (used for ranking the doxy-PEP strategies in the revised 
paper): (i) overall reduction in new STIs, (ii) reduction in new syphilis cases, (iii) change in the incidence of

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 24 -->

gonorrhoea with high -level tetracycline resistance, and (iv) benefit -cost ratios. The detailed results of these 
one-way sensitivity analyses for each doxy-PEP strategy are presented in our new Figures R8-R11. 
(1) Sensitivity analysis: resistance emergence per doxy-PEP use 
We acknowledge the significant uncertainty surrounding the potential for AMR development with 
widespread doxy-PEP use. To address this, we performed a sensitivity analysis on a key related parameter: the 
per-use probability that a doxycycline -susceptible N. gonorrhoeae infection develops high -level resistance 
following a single doxy-PEP dose. Lacking direct empirical data for this per -exposure event, we explored a 
range by varying this probability from 0.5 to 1.5 times our base-case estimate. 
Our analyses, as shown in Figures R8-R11, indicate that varying the per-doxy-PEP exposure probability 
of resistance emergence within this tested range (0.5 to 1.5 times the base-case value) had a relatively modest 
impact on the four key outcomes across the different doxy -PEP strategies. Even for t he specific outcome of 
“change in incidence of gonorrhoea with (high-level tetracycline resistance) HL TetR” (Figure R10), altering 
this doxy-PEP-induced resistance probability within this range did not appear to be a dominant driver of this 
outcome compared to other parameters. While the potential for AMR remains a critical consideration for long-
term doxy-PEP implementation and requires ongoing surveillance, these findings suggest that, within the 
explored range of assumptions, the projected short -to-medium term epidemiological and economic 
outcomes of the doxy -PEP strategies are relatively robust to moderate variations in the per -exposure 
risk of resistance emergence. 
(2) Sensitivity analysis: condom coverage for “sometimes” users 
In our base-case model, for individuals who reported “sometimes” using condoms, we assumed they used 
condoms in 50% of their sexual acts. This midpoint value was chosen due to the lack of more detailed 
behavioural data on the precise frequency of use withi n this specific group. We have added the following 
clarification to the Supplementary Materials: 
“We categorize these preferences into two types: (1) Always use condoms, applying the protection of 
condom in 100% of sexual acts, and (2) Sometimes use condoms, applying the protection of condom in 50% 
of sexual acts as a midpoint value in the absence of more granular behavioural data.” 
To address concerns that actual condom use among those who do not always use condoms may differ 
from our base -case assumption,  we conducted sensitivity analyses varying this probability to a lower 
bound (25%) and an upper bound (75%) of condom use for this group. 
According to Figures R8-R11, variations in the assumed condom use percentage among “sometimes” 
users emerged as the most influential factor affecting all four key outcomes across all doxy-PEP strategies. 
Specifically, assuming a lower condom use of 25% among this group —implying higher baseline STI 
incidence—consistently led to the highest projected reductions in new STI s and new syphilis cases achieved 
by doxy-PEP strategies, as there was a larger burden of infection to avert (e.g., illustrated in Figure R8 and 
R9). Conversely, assuming 75% condom use resulted in the lowest projected absolute reductions in these STIs. 
Regarding the change in incidence of gonorrhoea with HL TetR (e.g., Figure R10 ), a similar degree of 
sensitivity was observed. The 25% condom use scenario among “sometimes” users, which implies 
consequently potentially more doxy-PEP use, corresponded to the largest increase in N. gonorrhoeae with HL 
TetR. In contrast, the 75% condom use scenario was associated with the smallest increase (or, depending on 
the specific doxy-PEP strategy, the largest decrease) in N. gonorrhoeae with HL TetR. Furthermore, the BCRs 
were also highly sensitive to this parameter (e.g., Figure R11). The assumption of 75% condom use  among 
“sometimes” users, by leading to a lower baseline STI burden and thus smaller potential medical cost offsets 
from doxy-PEP, could result in BCRs falling below 1 for some doxy-PEP strategies, indicating they would not 
be cost-saving under that particular condom use assumption.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 25 -->

These results suggest that the overall effectiveness and cost -effectiveness of doxy-PEP are significantly 
influenced by background condom use patterns, particularly among individuals who do not consistently use 
condoms. Doxy-PEP interventions are likely to be more impactful and potentially more cost-effective in 
settings or populations where baseline condom use among this “sometimes” user group is at the lower 
end of plausible estimates. 
 
Figure R8. One-way sensitivity analyses of different doxy-PEP strategies on the total reduction in new 
STIs (2025-2034) compared with the base case (no doxy-PEP). Each panel represents a distinct doxy-PEP 
strategy. The horizontal bars illustrate the range of projected total STIs averted when individual model 
parameters are varied one at a time across their plausible lower and upper bounds. The vertical grey dashed 
line in each panel indicates the mean total STIs averted for that strategy using base -case parameter settings. 
The vertical red dashed line represents zero STIs averted (i.e., no change from the base case).  The error bars 
represent the lower or upper limit of the 95% CIs. a The ranges for doxy-PEP efficacies were varied as 13–
66% for gonorrhoea, 56–92% for chlamydia, and 64–86% for syphilis. b-f For other specified model parameters 
in the figure panels and not having explicit ranges defined on the y-axis, their plausible lower and upper bounds 
were generally derived by multiplying their respective base-case values by 0.5 and 1.5; precise details for all 
parameter variations are provided in Supplementary Table 2 . Doxy -PEP=doxycycline post -exposure

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 26 -->

prophylaxis. PrEP=pre -exposure prophylaxis. STIs=sexually transmitted infections.  HL TetR=high -level 
tetracycline resistance (used as a proxy for doxycycline resistance).

Each panel represents a distinct doxy-PEP strategy. The horizontal bars illustrate the range of projected total

bounds. The vertical grey dashed line in each panel indicates the me an total STIs averted for that strategy

benefit-cost ratio. The error bars represent the lower or upper limit of the 95% CIs. a The ranges for doxy-PEP

PEP=doxycycline post -exposure prophylaxis. PrEP=pre -exposure prophylaxis. STIs=s exually transmitted 
infections. HL TetR=high-level tetracycline resistance (used as a proxy for doxycycline resistance). 
These additional sensitivity analyses enhance the robustness of our findings by systematically exploring 
key uncertainties in model assumptions. The results from these newly conducted sensitivity analyses, 
including Figures R8-R11, have been incorporated into our revised paper. Furthermore, we have specifically 
highlighted the substantial impact of the assumed percentage of condom use among individuals who

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 29 -->

“sometimes” use condoms in the revised Discussion section of the manuscript, as follows: 
“Our comprehensive one -way sensitivity analyses highlighted that the assumed condom use frequency 
among individuals who “sometimes” use condoms (varied 25%–75%; base case: 50%) was the most 
influential parameter across the four key outcomes. Specifically, higher condom use in this group substantially 
diminished the projected impact of doxy -PEP strategies—reducing both the number of STIs averted and the 
increase in HL TetR gonorrhoea —and critically, caused the BCRs for broader strategies targeti ng all “HIV 
positive attendees ” or “PrEP-using attendees ” to fall below 1, rendering them not cost -saving under this 
assumption. This suggests that while deploying doxy -PEP in subpopulations or regions with lower baseline 
condom use among these individuals  might yield greater epidemiological impact and economic benefits, it 
could concurrently carry a higher risk of accelerating the prevalence of HL TetR gonorrhoea.” 
We included the detailed description of the one-way sensitivity analysis in our supplementary as following 
text: 
“5.2 One-way sensitivity analyses 
To assess the robustness of our model’s projections and to identify key parameters influencing the 
epidemiological and economic outcomes of the evaluated doxy-PEP strategies, we conducted extensive one-
way sensitivity analyses. In these analyses, individual model parameters were varied one at a time across 
plausible ranges, while all other parameters were held at their base-case values (as detailed in 
Supplementary Table 2, unless otherwise specified). The impact of these variations was assessed on four 
primary outcomes: the cumulative reduction in overall new STIs, the cumulative reduction in new syphilis 
cases, the cumulative change in new gonorrhoea infections with HL TetR, and the BCRs over the 10-year 
intervention period (2025-2034). The results of these one-way sensitivity analyses for each of the five doxy-
PEP strategies are presented as tornado plots in Supplementary Figures 20–23. 
The key parameters varied in these sensitivity analyses, along with their tested ranges, included: 
(1) Doxy-PEP intervention parameters: 
A. Prescription duration: varied from 3 months (13 weeks) to 12 months (52 weeks), would also 
lead to the prescribed doxy-PEP range from 26 to 104 doses (base case: 6 months). 
B. Uptake rate among eligible individuals: 50% to 100%, (base case: 75%). 
C. Adherence to taking doxy-PEP post-exposure: 60% to 100%, (base case: 80%). 
D. Pathogen-specific efficacies of doxy-PEP: 
a) Gonorrhoea: 13% to 66% (base case: 45%). 
b) Chlamydia: 56% to 92% (base case: 81%). 
c) Syphilis: 64% to 86% (base case: 77%).

Major comment 5: 
While the study highlights the development of AMR as a critical concern, the long -term implications of 
resistance on gonorrhea management and doxy-PEP implementation are not fully explored. Could the authors 
expand the Discussion section to address how increasing tet racycline resistance might affect future bacterial 
STI treatment strategies and public health recommendations? For instance, how would resistance trends 
impact doxy-PEP’s long-term viability as a bacterial STI prevention tool? 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this critical suggestion to expand our Discussion section to more fully explore 
the long-term implications of AMR on gonorrhoea management and the future viability of doxy-PEP. We agree 
that this is a crucial aspect for contextualizing our findings and for informing public health recommendations. 
In response to your comment, we have expanded a paragraph in the Discussion section to present our 
opinions about this topic as follows: 
“Despite its benefits in decreasing incident STIs, our study indicates that doxy -PEP may exacerbate the 
burden of gonorrhoea with HL TetR, affecting its long-term sustainability. … Broader AMR concerns include 
potential co-selection of ceftriaxone resistance, and resistance in bystanders like commensal Neisseria species. 
Effective use therefore requires targeted implementation in key populations (such as GBMSM at risk of 
syphilis), and robust AMR surveillance for pathogens including N. gonorrhoeae and commen sal Neisseria 
species. Ultimately, balancing doxy -PEP’s benefits (syphilis/chlamydia) against its diminishing utility for 
gonorrhoea and wider AMR risks underscores the need for concurrent investment in alternative prevention 
like vaccines and rapid resistance diagnostics as part of an integrated STI prevention strategy.” 
Minor comment 1: 
Line 173: Should “intervention” be removed in “Eight intervention scenarios” since scenario 1 was the 
base case without any interventions? 
Minor comment 2: 
Line 178: Is it  possible to consider the scenarios that provides doxy -PEP to HIV+ and PrEP -using 
GBMSM separately, since they have different characteristics and the proportion of GBMSM on PrEP is almost 
three times larger than HIV+ GBMSM.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 31 -->

Response to comments 1 and 2: 
We thank the reviewer for their careful reading and helpful suggestions regarding the description of our 
study scenarios. 
We agree with the reviewer’s comment that the original phrasing was incorrect as Scenario 1 is the base 
case without any intervention. The word “intervention” has been removed while introducing the scenarios. 
We also agree with the reviewer ’s valuable suggestion to consider HIV + and PrEP-using GBMSM as 
separate groups for doxy-PEP eligibility due to their distinct characteristics. In response, we have revised our 
doxy-PEP intervention scenarios. We now have separate scenarios targeting all HIV+ GBMSM (new 
Scenario 5) and all PrEP -using GBMSM (new Scenario 6), regardless of their recent STI history. To 
maintain a focused set of prima ry eligibility types and to reduce complexity, we have removed the previous 
scenarios that combined “HIV+ or PrEP -using” status with additional STI/syphilis diagnostic criteria.  This 
restructuring results in a new set of 1 base case plus 5 doxy-PEP intervention scenarios.  
Correspondingly, the manuscript main text and Table 1 (introduction of all scenarios) have been updated 
accordingly to our response to above two comments, now stating: 
“Six scenarios (Table 1) were considered: a base case without doxy-PEP (scenario 1) and five doxy-PEP 
implementation strategies (scenarios 2–6) based on potential eligibility options proposed according to the 
current Australian consensus statement. Scenario 2 focuses on GBMSM with syphilis diagnosis, while 
Scenarios 3 and 4 target GBMSM with two STIs’ diagnoses within the past six and twelve months, respectively. 
Scenario 5 targets all HIV+ GBMSM attending sexual health clinics for doxy -PEP, while Scenario 6 targets 
all PrEP-using GBMSM attending sexual health clinics for doxy-PEP, both regardless of STI status.”

In essence, the economic benefit of implementing doxy-PEP in each scenario is defined as the sum of the 
discounted annual differences in direct medical costs between the base case and the intervention scenario over 
the 2025-2034 period. The total cost of intervention is the incremental cost of implementing the doxy -PEP 
strategy in each scenario. 
Therefore, the BCR specifically represents the ratio of averted direct medical costs (benefit) to the cost of

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 32 -->

the doxy-PEP intervention. A BCR greater than 1 indicates that the monetary benefits (in terms of averted 
medical costs) of implementing doxy-PEP outweigh the costs of the intervention.” 
Minor comment 4: 
Line 222: The model incorporated the preventive effect of doxy -PEP on gonorrhea  susceptible to 
tetracycline, with an efficacy of 45% (13% -66%) from a meta -analysis of randomized trials. However, the 
prevalence of tetracycline -resistant gonorrhea might play a role in the efficacy of doxy -PrEP on gonorrhea 
prevention in different setti ngs (PMID: 37018493). Therefore, it is likely to be an underestimation of the 
impact of doxy-PEP on tetracycline-susceptible gonorrhea, and 66% as the upper limit for gonorrhea may not 
reflect the potential efficacy of doxy-PEP on tetracycline-susceptible gonorrhea. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for their comment regarding the potential influence of tetracycline resistance 
prevalence on doxy-PEP efficacy against N. gonorrhoeae. 
Our model explicitly stratifies N. gonorrhoeae  infections based on doxycycline  susceptibility (MIC 
threshold: 8 mg/L), with 0% efficacy assumed for infections with high-level resistance (MIC > 8 mg/L). The 
45% efficacy estimate (13%-66%) is applied only to infections with MIC ≤ 8 mg/L (called susceptible in our 
study). We offer the following rationale for our parameterisation: 
1. Evidence of breakthrough infections: The DoxyPEP trial showed 61.5% of breakthrough gonorrhoea 
infections in the doxy -PEP group involved susceptible strains (MIC < 2 mg/L), indicating imperfect 
prevention well below our 8 mg/L threshold (PMID: 37018493). 
2. Pharmacokinetic limitations: Data suggests rectal doxycycline concentrations after a 200 mg dose 
may be insufficient to reliably inhibit strains with MICs > 2 mg/L (PMID: 38428259). 
3. Methodological robustness: While another modelling study used the same 8 mg/L threshold but a 
higher point estimate (62%, range 20-100%) (PMID: 39374606), our estimate from the meta-analysis 
might be more robust (PMID: 39122208). 
While we acknowledge this approach may be conservative,  it reflects the current evidence until 
more granular efficacy data by MIC levels becomes available.  We hope this explanation clarifies our 
rationale. 
Minor comment 5: 
Line 233: Did Figure 1 show the mean or the median of the simulations based on 100 parameter sets? The 
same issues are for the results provided in the text. 
Response: 
Thank you for pointing out the need for clarification. Figure 1 shows the mean of the simulation results 
based on the 100 parameter sets. To address this for all figures and the text, we have: 
1. Added a statement in the Methods section to explicitly clarify this information: 
“Key model outputs derived from simulations using these 100 parameter sets are reported as mean 
values with 95% confidence intervals (CIs), based on one simulation run per parameter set.” 
2. Revised the captions for all relevant figures to specify that mean values were presented (e.g., the 
caption for Figure 1): 
“Figure 1. Projected impact of various doxy-PEP strategies on STI incidence…All results shown are 
means (with or without 95% CI) calculated from simulations based on 100 parameter sets.”

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 33 -->

Minor comment 6: 
Line 258: Even though the incidence of gonorrhea  rebounded after 2028, it was expected to reach an 
equilibrium after 2034, which should be lower than the 2024 level. This indicated a lower gonorrhea burden 
even though the prevalence of tetracycline-resistant gonorrhea significantly increased. 
Response: 
Thank you for your comment regarding the statement on the projected long -term gonorrhoea dynamics. 
To explain why our model projects this outcome – where gonorrhoea incidence, despite a significant rebound 
due to increased resistance, remains below the bas e case and settles below the 2024 level “in this scenario” 
(referring to the most inclusive strategy, Scenario 5) – the key underlying factors incorporated into our model 
are: 
(1) Partial effectiveness of doxy-PEP: Our model assumes doxy-PEP is ineffective against gonorrhoea 
infections from isolates with high tetracycline/doxycycline MICs (>8 mg/L). However, even as overall 
“doxycycline-resistant” infections increase in this scenario, a significant portion of gonorrhoea cases 
continue to arise from isolates with lower MICs (≤8 mg/L). Because doxy -PEP remains effective 
against these more susceptible strains, it retains overall partial effectiveness at the population level. 
This sustained partial protection is critical: it keeps transmission below that of the b ase case (no 
intervention) and enables incidence to settle below the 2024 level. 
(2) Fewer sources of infection: By continually preventing a proportion of new infections, doxy-PEP 
effectively reduces the overall prevalence of gonorrhoea compared to the base ca se. With fewer 
infectious individuals, the community’s ‘force of infection’ (overall transmission potential) is lessened. 
This sustained dampening effect on transmission enables the gonorrhoea incidence rate to stabilize at 
the observed lower long-term level. 
We have revised the manuscript text to more clearly and concisely describe the incidence levels following 
the observed rebound, reads: 
“However, while doxy -PEP initially reduced gonorrhoea incidence, projected 2030 levels under all 
strategies surpassed the 2020 baseline (black dashed line in Fig. 1A), typically rebounding within two to three 
years of implementation due to increasing doxycycline resistance; nevertheless, incidence remained below the 
projected base-case (no doxy-PEP) level.” 
We believe this revised sentence more directly states the finding, and the detailed explanation provided 
above might clarify the rationale for this model projection. 
Minor comment 7: 
Figure 1: It is interesting that under scenario 5, the reduction (% change) in chlamyd ia incidence was 
smaller than syphilis, even though the efficacy of doxy -PEP on chlamydia was higher. Similar results were 
observed under the other scenarios too. It is unlikely to be explained by the higher prevalence or the higher 
proportion of asymptomatic infections of chlamydia. Did this mean that the epidemic of chlamydia was less 
concentrated among the targeted subgroups? Could the authors provide some insights? 
Response: 
We thank you for this insightful observation. You are correct that across the various intervention scenarios, 
chlamydia incidence consistently shows a smaller percentage reduction than syphilis, despite the higher point-
estimate for doxy-PEP efficacy against chlamydia used in our model (81% vs. 77% for syphilis). 
To provide a clearer quantitative insight into this observation, we have summarized the relevant 
percentage decreases in incidence for syphilis and chlamydia, and the ratio of these decreases, across

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 34 -->

various intervention scenarios from our model (Table R1). It is worth noting that the following values, in 
response to reviewers’ concerns, the following table was calculated based on the results of the last version, 
but the outcomes and explanation are similar in current version. 
Table R1. Percentage decrease in syphilis and chlamydia incidence, and ratio of decreases, by 
doxy-PEP scenario. 
Scenarios Syphilis Incidence 
Decrease (%) 
Chlamydia Incidence 
Decrease (%) Ratio 
Diagnosed syphilis 52.94 5.74 9.23 
Two STI diagnoses in 6m 44.12 18.03 2.45 
Two STI diagnoses in 12m 58.82 34.43 1.71 
HIV+ or PrEP-using 85.29 64.75 1.32 
HIV+ or PrEP-using, one syphilis diagnoses  44.12 4.10 10.77 
HIV+ or PrEP-using, two STI diagnoses in 6m 35.29 13.11 2.69 
HIV+ or PrEP-using, two STI diagnoses in 12m 52.94 25.41 2.08 
We believe this observation, as detailed in the table, can be attributed to two primary and interacting factors: 
1. Higher transmission probability of syphilis: According to the parameter estimation, syphilis’s per-
exposure transmission probability in our model (28.3%) is approximately 1.52 times that of chlamydia 
(18.6%). An effective intervention will more proportionally impact an infection that transmits more 
efficiently per contact, as each averted syphilis transmission prevents a higher -probability event. 
Accordingly, in broader intervention scenarios, the percentage decrease in syphilis incidence was 
typically 1.3 to 2.7 times greater than for chlamydia. This range generally aligns with or exceeds the 
transmissibility ratio, highlighting the influence of syphilis’s higher transmissibility. 
2. Differential epidemic concentration and targeting strategy: Your hypothesis that the chlamydia 
epidemic might be less concentrated in targeted subgroups is strongly supported by findings from 
syphilis-specific targeting scenarios. In these cases, the ratio of percentage decrease (syphilis vs. 
chlamydia) rose dramatically to over 9 (e.g., 9.23 and 10.77). This indicates such strategies effectively 
reach core syphilis transmission networks where chlamydia is comparatively less concentrat ed, thus 
its incidence is less impacted relative to syphilis under these focused approaches. 
In summary, syphilis’s higher transmissibility predisposes it to a larger relative impact from doxy-
PEP. This effect is significantly amplified when strategies tar get subgroups where syphilis is highly 
concentrated. We appreciate you prompting this deeper examination into the differential impacts . We hope 
this detailed explanation, supported by the data in Table R1, sufficiently clarifies this important nuance. 
Minor comment 8: 
Figure 3: Could the authors provide more details about why the number of doxy -PEP prescriptions 
dropped after the first year of implementation under scenario 5 (HIV+ or PrEP -using)? The number of 
individuals eligible should be constant over the years since the HIV prevalence and PrEP coverage were fixed. 
And the uptake rate was also fixed at 75%. 
Response: 
We thank you for this insightful observation regarding the doxy -PEP prescription dynamics. To clarify, 
the initial peak -and-drop pattern in prescriptions is a general dynamic driven by the large -scale

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 35 -->

initiation of doxy-PEP among a previously ‘doxy-PEP naïve’ population. In our revised manuscript, the 
doxy-PEP prescription-related outcomes have been moved to the Figure 1 (Panels E, F, and G). 
The ‘doxy-PEP naïve ’ population effect explains the initial peak in prescriptions across most 
strategies. At the start of implementation, the entire eligible population is available to start the intervention, 
leading to a wave of first-time prescriptions. In subsequent years, since a portion of the eligible population is 
already on an active course of doxy -PEP at any given time, the pool of individuals available for a new 
prescription is smaller, causing the total number of annual prescription events to drop from its initial peak 
before it either stabilizes or follows other long-term trends. 
Our revised model reveals two distinct long -term prescription trends depending on the specific 
eligibility criteria of the strategy. For strategies where eligibility is based on recent STI diagnoses, the annual 
prescription numbers show a gradual decrease over time, because  the success of doxy -PEP reduces the 
incidence of the STIs that trigger eligibility. Conversely, for the new, broad strategy targeting all PrEP users, 
we now project a slight upward trend in prescriptions over the longer term. This increase is primarily du e to 
our dynamic population update, wherein new individuals continually enter the PrEP -using cohort and are all 
considered ‘doxy-PEP naïve’, thus replenishing the pool of eligible initiators. We believe this revised approach 
provides a more realistic projection of long-term prescription dynamics.

three STIs combined, respectively, across different doxy -PEP scenarios. Panel (E) shows the mean annual

among eligible individuals. Panel (G) presents the mean cumulative incident STIs (syphilis, chlamydia,

have sex with men. IA=infections averted. PrEP=pre-exposure prophylaxis. PY=person-years. STIs=sexually

Minor comment 9: 
Table 2 and Figure 1: It seems that under scenario 2, the majority of bacterial STI reductions was syphilis 
(approximately 1800 out of 2687 cases), with approximately 1168 (2687/2.3) doxy -PEP prescriptions. This 
indicated an extremely high re-infection rate of syphilis within 6 months of diagnosis, which seems unrealistic 
(PMID: 27991843). Could the authors provide more insights? 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for their observation on the ~1,800 syphilis cases averted in scenario 2 with doxy-
PEP, relative to ~1,184 doxy-PEP prescriptions (our model figure). The reviewer queried  if this implied an 
“extremely high re-infection rate... which seems unrealistic,” citing PMID: 27991843. 
Our individual-based model (IBM) shows doxy-PEP’s benefit extends beyond direct prevention by 
halting multi -generational transmission chains.  Each averted re -infection (which would have occurred 
during the 0.5-year doxy-PEP course and acts as the index case of a prevented chain) can prevent, on average, 
up to ~11 subsequent generations of spread over a ~4.75-year average potential impact period ((10 years - 0.5 
years) / 2). This calculation is based on model parameters for syphilis progression (e.g., infectious period ~20 
weeks, durations of stages, symptomatic proportions, time to diagnosis and treatment). This cumulative, multi-
generational effect is key. 
To quantify the driving parameters for these chains, we analysed the first year of prescribing doxy-PEP 
to individuals with syphilis diagnosis. This period offers a clearer window for estimating initial parameters 
by minimizing confounding from accumulated long -term transmission effects, the precise tracking of which 
over a decade in an IBM is complex. Key insights from this first-year data: 
(1) Direct re-infection prevention: The cited literature (PMID: 27991843) suggests a ~5.5% re-infection 
risk over 6 months (22% over 2 years). Our model shows doxy-PEP directly prevented re-infection in 
~7.14% of users during their 6 -month course (15.2 averted re -infections from 212.8 prescriptions). 
This rate is comparable to the literature, indicating our model’s direct prevention assumption is realistic. 
(2) Transmission potentials: 
1) Averted re-infections in doxy-PEP users, had they occurred, would have each transmitted to, on 
average, ~2.6 new individuals (i.e., caused ~2.6 new infections). 
2) Other syphilis infections in the background population each transmitted to, on average, ~0.9 new 
individuals (i.e., caused ~0.9 new infections). 
3) The higher potential (2.6 vs 0.9) for the doxy-PEP user subgroup likely reflects factors such as

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 37 -->

higher sexual activity levels in this population often targeted for STI prevention. 
Using these first -year parameters to project over the 10 -year intervention period for 1,184 doxy-PEP 
prescriptions (Figure R12): 
(1) Directly averted re-infections: ~84.5 cases (
1184 7.14% ). 
(2) First-generation indirect infections averted (from the 84.6 cases): ~219.7 cases (
84.5 2.6 ). 
(3) Subsequent ~10 generations of indirect infections averted (propagating at 0.9 new infections/ syphilis 
infections): ~1,287.4 cases (
10
n1
219.7 0.9 n

 ). 
(4) Total estimated averted cases: 84.5 + 219.7 + 1287.4 ≈ 1,591.6 cases. 
 
Figure R12. Schematic diagram illustrating the transmission chain. 
This estimate of ~1,59 2 averted infections is comparable to the ~1,800 cases observed in the full IBM 
simulation (scenario 2 vs scenario 1), with differences likely due to simplifying assumptions in this estimation 
versus the IBM’s complexity. 
In summary, our model’s direct re-infection prevention rate is realistic and comparable to literature. 
The substantial number of averted syphilis cases (~1,800) is primarily due to doxy-PEP’s interruption of multi-
generational transmission chains, an effect initiated by preventing infections in individuals who, while having 
a realistic re-infection risk, also have a higher onward transmission potential. We hope this explanation could 
provide sufficient details of this important comment.

Line 291: Could the authors provide the overall cost under the base case (scenario 1)? 
Response: 
Thank you for the suggestion. Following model re -calibration, the new estimated cumulative cost under 
the base case is 21,415 (20,981 to 21,849) thousands Australian dollars. We have added the overall cost under 
the base case to the “Economic evaluations” subsection of the Results section as follows: 
“Between 2025 and 2034, the estimated direct medical cost in the base case was $21,415 thousand 
($20,981–$21,849 thousand).” 
Minor comment 11 and 12: 
Line 328: It is worth noting that even though scenario 6 was the most economically viable strategy, it led 
to the smallest reduction in bacterial STI incidence. 
Line 333: Instead of only pointing out the most prioritized strategy, it could be helpful to provide a list of 
strategies ranking by priority in case the budget for doxy-PEP implementation is larger and aims for a higher

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 38 -->

reduction in bacterial STI incidence. 
RESPONSE to comments 11 and 12: 
Thank you for both of these insightful comments. We will address them together as they both relate to the 
crucial aspec ts of selecting and prioritizing doxy -PEP strategies based on various outcomes and policy 
considerations.  
Regarding Minor Comment 11, we agree with your observation that an intervention strategy appearing 
most economically viable may not always yield the largest epidemiological impact. This directly relates to 
Comment 12, which highlights the value of providing ranked lists of strategies based on different priorities. 
Exploring such trade-offs is indeed important for informing doxy-PEP implementation. 
To address these points, we developed and applied a structured evaluation framework to our five 
updated doxy-PEP implementation strategies  (Diagnosed syphilis; Two STI diagnoses in 6m; Two STI 
diagnoses in 12m; PLWH attendees; PrEP -using attendees). Each strat egy was evaluated based on its 
performance across four key outcome indicators which fall into three main categories: averted infections 
(specifically, the cumulative reduction in new STIs overall, abbreviated as STI Reduc., and the cumulative 
reduction in newly incident  syphilis, Syphilis Reduc.), antimicrobial resistance (AMR) (specifically, the 
cumulative increase in new gonorrhoea infections with high-level tetracycline resistance, HL TetR, where the 
aim is to minimize this increase), and economic efficiency (specifically, the Benefit-Cost Ratio, BCR, aiming 
to maximize this). 
We then analysed the performance of these doxy -PEP strategies under eleven distinct policy 
prioritization frameworks, as visually depicted by their rankings in our new Figure R 13. These 
frameworks, shown along the x -axis of the figure, assess strategies based on each of the four indicators 
considered singly (four frameworks); combinations of one infection indicator (either overall STI Reduc. or 
Syphilis Reduc.) with one other indic ator (either HL TetR or BCR), yielding four pairwise frameworks; a 
combination of HL TetR & BCR (one framework); and finally, combinations of one infection indicator with 
both HL TetR and BCR, resulting in two three -way frameworks. For frameworks combining  multiple 
indicators, strategies were ranked based on the sum of their individual ranks for the constituent indicators, 
assuming equal weighting, where a lower sum indicates a more favourable overall rank. 
The outcomes of this comprehensive evaluation, pre senting the rank of each doxy -PEP strategy (from 1 
[most favorable] to 5 [least favorable]) under each of these eleven frameworks, are visually summarized in 
Figure R13. This figure directly provides ranked lists of strategies under different policy goals (addressing 
Comment 12) and visually demonstrates the trade-offs by showing how strategy rankings shift when different 
indicators or combinations of indicators are prioritized ( addressing Comment 11). Users of this figure can 
see how rankings change based on the specific policy lens applied. 
We hope that this clearly defined evaluation methodology and its visual presentation in Figure R13 
address both of your important comments. It provides a transparent tool for decision -making, offering 
ranked strategy lists for different priorities and systematically navigating the trade -offs involved. It is 
important to note that these rankings pertain to the specific doxy -PEP implementation strategies evaluated 
within our study; real-world settings might present other alternative strategies for consideration. The revised 
manuscript includes this analysis and the resulting figure. 
We have added the methodology of this ranking frameworks as a separate section to our revised 
manuscript as follows: 
“Evaluation framework of doxy-PEP strategies 
To systematically compare and rank the five doxy -PEP strategies, we used a multi -criteria evaluation

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 39 -->

framework, assessing performance over 10 years (2025 -2034) against four key indicators: two for infection 
reduction—(1) reductions in o verall STIs and (2) reductions in syphilis infection (highlighted due to its 
prioritization in the Australian consensus statement); one for AMR—(3) increased gonorrhoea infections with 
HL TetR, aiming to minimize this; and one for economic efficiency—(4) the BCR, aiming to maximize this. 
Strategies were ranked under eleven policy prioritization frameworks, considering these four indicators 
singly or in pairwise/three -way combinations (e.g., an infection reduction indicator with HL TetR impact 
and/or BCR). F or combined -indicator frameworks, ranks (1=most to 5=least favourable) were based on 
summing individual indicator ranks (assuming equal weighting; lower sum = better performance). These 
rankings (Figure 4) aid decision-making by showing how strategy prioritization varies with policy goals.” 
We also included the ranking figure as Figure 4 in the manuscript, and added related text in our results 
section as follows: 
“Our multi-criteria evaluation framework showed that no single strategy was optimal across all eleven 
policy prioritizations (Fig. 4). The strategy targeting all PrEP -using attendees ranked highest when 
maximizing overall STI and syphilis reduction was the sole priority. Conversely, the strategy targeting 
individuals with a syphilis diagnosis consis tently achieved the highest rankings in frameworks that 
emphasized minimizing the increase in HL TetR gonorrhoea, maximizing the BCR, and all the combined 
prioritizations frameworks.” 
 
Figure R13. Doxy-PEP strategy rankings across different policy prioritization frameworks. This figure 
illustrates the relative ranking (from Rank 1 [most favourable] to Rank 5 [least favourable]) of five distinct 
doxy-PEP implementation strategies across eleven policy prioritization frameworks. The strategies (defined  
in the figure legend and detailed in Table 1 of the main manuscript) were evaluated based on their performance 
against four key indicators: (1) maximal reduction in overall Sexually Transmitted Infections (STI Reduc.); 
(2) maximal reduction in syphilis in cidence (Syphilis Reduc.); (3) optimal outcomes concerning high -level 
tetracycline resistance in N. gonorrhoeae (HL TetR; i.e., minimizing its increase or maximizing its decrease); 
and (4) maximal BCR. The x -axis specifies each framework, showing rankings based on these indicators 
considered singly, or in combinations of two or three indicators. STIs=sexually transmitted infections . 
Reduc.=reductions. HL TetR=high-level tetracycline resistance. BCR=benefit-cost ratio. PrEP=pre-exposure 
prophylaxis. 
Minor comment 13: 
Line 382: Higher adherence also led to no reduction on the prevalence of tetracycline resistant gonorrhea 
under scenario 6 (Figure S33). 
Response:

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 40 -->

Thank you for this observation. You are correct that in Scenario 6 , higher doxy-PEP adherence did not 
reduce, and sometimes increased, tetracycline-resistant gonorrhoea prevalence. 
This outcome aligns with antimicrobial resistance principles:  (1) Higher adherence to doxy -PEP 
means more frequent doxycycline use ; (2) Increased doxycycline use intensifies selection pressure, 
favouring tetracycline -resistant gonorrhoea strains over susceptible ones ; (3) Consequently, even if 
overall gonorrhoea incidence decreases, the proportion of tetracycline-resistant gonorrhoea among remaining 
infections can rise, potentially increasing tetracycline-resistant gonorrhoea prevalence. 
We understand that an expectation for higher adherence to reduce drug resistance might stem from the 
well-documented “bell-shaped” adherence-resistance relationship in other contexts, such as HIV antiretroviral 
therapy. In HIV treatment, intermediate levels of adherence can paradoxically pose the highest risk for 
resistance development (PMID: 16511415). 
However, our model for doxy-PEP assumes a dynamic different from the long-course treatment. For 
short-course, post -exposure prophylaxis like doxy -PEP, we posit that increased use directly intensifies 
selection pressure for resistance, as cumulative antibiotic exposure is considered a key driver. This app roach 
is distinct from the complex pharmacology and virology underpinning the bell-shaped curve in long-term HIV 
treatment. Thus, in our model, while higher adherence is beneficial for preventing susceptible gonorrhoea, the 
associated increase in antibioti c usage can concurrently elevate the proportion, and thus prevalence, of 
tetracycline-resistant gonorrhoea. 
Minor comment 14: 
Line 402: The model was calibrated using data from 2012-2023 with static sexual behaviour assigned to 
each individual at the beginning of the simulation. However, since the beginning of the COVID-19 pandemic, 
there might be dramatic changes in sexual behaviours and access to the healthcare system, which could affect 
the formation of the partnership network and screening frequencies o f STIs. This needs to be listed as a 
limitation since it was not considered in the model. 
Response: 
We thank you for this important comment. We agree that the COVID -19 pandemic had considerable 
impacts on sexual behaviours and healthcare access, which coul d influence STI epidemics. Our model 
addresses these effects in two key ways, while also acknowledging a remaining limitation. 
First, to account for changes in healthcare access, our model incorporates dynamic annual STI testing 
frequencies derived from na tional surveillance data (ACCESS) spanning 2012–2023. As this period 
includes the pandemic years, our modelled screening rates for different GBMSM subgroups reflect the 
observed real-world changes in testing behaviour. 
Second, and most importantly, while o ur model does not explicitly simulate changes in partnership 
dynamics due to the pandemic (a point we now acknowledge as a limitation in the manuscript), it is calibrated 
against observed annual STI incidence and antimicrobial resistance trends from 2012–2022. Because 
these empirical data trends inherently reflect the net epidemiological outcome of all real-world factors during 
that time—including any pandemic-driven shifts in sexual behaviour—our calibration ensures that our model's 
baseline projections are aligned with the actual STI epidemic dynamics observed before, during, and after the 
main pandemic period.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 41 -->

Therefore, while we do not model the specific behavioural mechanisms, the model ’s calibration to real-
world epidemic outcomes that were themselves shaped by the pandemic provides confidence in the validity 
of our baseline against which interventions are assessed. 
In response to this comment, we have now specifically clarified this limitation in our manuscript ’s 
discussion of limitations as follows: 
“This study has several limitations. First, our model’s structure may not capture all nuances of GBMSM 
sexual networks and behaviours. For instance, our model conceptualizes sexual acts in a general manner, 
which precludes modelling of site -specific dynamics like the role of the pharynx in AMR epidemics, and it 
does not explicitly model the direct impacts of the COVID -19 pandemic on partnership dynamics. However, 
the model’s robust calibration to observed annual STI incidence and antimicrobial resistance tr ends supports 
the overall validity of our impact estimations.” 
Minor comment 15: 
Line 405: Is the conclusion focusing on individuals diagnosed with syphilis or HIV+/PrEP -using 
individuals diagnosed with syphilis like the authors mentioned in the first paragraph of the Discussion section? 
Response: 
Thank you for highlighting a potential ambiguity in our previous conclusion regarding individuals 
diagnosed with syphilis. 
To provide clarity on this point in the current version of our manuscript, it is important  to note two key 
aspects of our revised analysis. Firstly, our doxy-PEP implementation strategies have been significantly 
updated (as detailed in our response to Minor Comment 2 and reflected in the updated Table 1). In this revised 
set, scenario 2 (“GBMSM diagnosed with syphilis”) is now the sole strategy that uses a syphilis diagnosis 
as the direct and primary eligibility criterion. Secondly, our recommendations for optimal strategies are 
now based on a comprehensive ranking framework (detailed in our response to Comments 11 and 12). This 
framework evaluates each strategy across three key domains: reduction in new infections, change in AMR, 
and the BCR. 
Therefore, to clarify the conclusion, our revised manuscript now presents the refined conclusion —
informed by these updated strategies and the multi-factor ranking as follows: 
“Targeting individuals diagnosed with syphilis emerges as potentially the most balanced approach when 
considering STI reduction, BCR, and the change in new gonorrhoea infections with HL TetR.” 
Minor comment 16: 
Algorithm 1.3): When initializing the epidemics of STIs, the authors set all the individuals at exposed 
stages of corresponding diseases, which may require further considerations since there should be individuals 
in each stage of disease progression at the beginning of the simulation. 
Response: 
Thank you for your comment regarding the initialization of STI epidemics in Algorithm 1.3.  You are 
correct that we initialize all individuals at the exposed stage for each STI. This initial simplification is made 
due to the lack of precise data to define the STI-specific proportions of individuals across all disease stages at 
the simulation’s start.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 42 -->

To ensure the model reaches a more realistic distribution of disease stages before we begin collecting data 
for analysis, we employ an approach that has been used in other modelling study: a one-year “burn-in” 
period. That is, after the initial seeding of infections, the model runs for this one -year duration. During this 
period, natural model dynamics —including disease transmission, progression through various stages, 
diagnosis, and treatment—occur. This allows the population initially set to the “exposed” stage to naturally 
distribute across the different stages of each STI. Consequently, when we commence recording outcomes after 
this burn-in period, the distribution of individuals across these disease stages is more representative of an 
ongoing epidemic, rather than the simplified starting condition. 
We updated the description of Algorithm 1.3  (now reads the 1.4)  to more clearly state this burn -in 
procedure as follows: 
“Algorithm 1.4 Transmission, progression, and management of STIs without doxy -PEP 
(
2 52 14stageweek  ) 
Note: The first 52 weeks in this period serve as a burn-in period, and outcomes are not documented. 
This allows initialized infections to distribute naturally across disease stages.”
