Minor comment 19: 
Section 1.2.7: Is the sex frequency of casual partnership for one partner (maximum duration as two weeks) 
or all the causal partnerships in the past year? 
Response: 
Thank you for this clarifying question. The “sex frequency of casual partnership ” parameter in 
Section 1.2.7 defines an individual’s expected annual total number of sexual acts with all their casual 
partners over a one-year period. 
When a specific casual partnership forms (which, as you noted, can have a maximum duration of two 
weeks in our model), this individual -specific annual frequency subsequently informs the calculation of the 
expected number of sexual acts within that particular partnership. The process unfolds as follows: 
1) For each of the two individuals in the newly formed partnership, their respective annual casual sex 
frequency is scaled by the duration of that specific partnership. This calculation yields the expected 
number of acts for each individual within that specific timeframe. 
2) The final expected number of sexual acts for that particular partnership is then determined by 
averaging these individually scaled expected acts. 
Therefore, to directly answer your question, the base parameter is an annual total for an individual across 
all their casual partnerships, and this is dynamically applied to each specific, short-term casual partnership as 
it forms. We have also revi sed Algorithms 2 and 3 to explicitly reflect this calculation; for example, the 
relevant description now includes: “…(a) expected sexual acts during this partnership (average of the [duration 
× frequency] products from both individuals) …” 
Minor comment 20: 
Section 1.3: What is the meaning of “have at most one regular partner and/or at most one regular partner”?

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 45 -->

Does this indicate each individual can only have at most two partners at each time step? 
Response: 
Thank you for your careful reading and for identifying this typographical error in Section 1.3 of the 
supplementary material; we sincerely apologize for the confusion it caused. The phrase has been corrected in 
the revised text. 
Your interpretation is exactly right: our model limits individuals to having at most one regular partner 
and, concurrently, at most one casual partner, for a maximum of two simultaneous partners at any given 
time. This is a structural assumption we made to balance model complexity with the available data for 
parameterization, an approach consistent with some previous individual -based models of STI transmission 
(e.g., PMID: 34735428, 34894134). We acknowledge this as a limitation and agree that exploring more 
complex multi-partnership dynamics is a valuable area for future res earch. We add revised the expression of 
our first limitation in the manuscription to reflect it: 
“First, our model’s structure may not capture all nuances of GBMSM sexual networks and behaviours…” 
Minor comment 21: 
Algorithm 2: Could the authors provide more details on those who were left in the Ra list (e.g., the 
percentage of those who were left without partnership formation and the percentage of HIV+ among them)? 
Response: 
Thank you for this insightful question regarding the dynamics of the regular par tnership availability list 
(Ra list) detailed in Algorithm 2. We understand the concern that serosorting preferences, as modelled, might 
lead to some individuals remaining on this list for extended periods, potentially influencing model outcomes 
or realism. 
To directly investigate the composition of this list,  we calculated additional outputs to quantify the 
number and characteristics of individuals waiting to form a regular partnership over time.  We ran the 
model over the period 2012–2034 using 100 distinct parameter sets. At the midpoint of each simulated year, 
we recorded: (1) the total number of individuals present on the Ra list (i.e., those actively seeking but not 
currently in a regular partnership), and (2) the number of HIV+ individuals among them. The average values 
for these two metrics, across the 100 parameter sets, are plotted over time in the new Figure R14. 
The results of this analysis indicate that while the number of individuals on the Ra list at any given 
time is small (Figure R14). On average, approximately 10 to 15 individuals were present on the Ra list at any 
mid-year snapshot. Among these, an average of 5 to 7 were HIV -positive, constituting around 50% of those 
on the list at these time points. This high proportion suggests that serosorting behaviour is indeed a factor that 
contributes to some individuals temporarily remaining on the availability list while searching for a 
seroconcordant regular partner. 
However, it is importan t to emphasize that the composition of the Ra list is highly dynamic, and 
individuals seem not to become permanently unable to form partnerships.  The fluctuations observed in 
the numbers over time (as shown in Figure R14) indicate that individuals generally do not remain on the list 
indefinitely. Instead, they can, and do, form regular partnerships in subsequent time steps as new potential 
partners become available or as they themselves re -enter the availability pool after previous partnerships

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 46 -->

dissolve. This dynamism suggests the model allows for continuous partnership formation rather than creating 
a static group of individuals unable to find partners. 
 
Figure R14. Number of total and HIV+ individuals on the Ra list at mid-year, 2012–2034 (averages 
from 100 parameter sets). 
Minor comment 22: 
Section 1.4: “Figure S5-7” or “Figure S6-8”? 
Response: 
Thank you for identifying this error in the figure reference in Section 1.4. 
The text has been corrected to refer to “Supplementary Figures 9-11”, as extra figures have been inserted 
before this part in the revised version . We have also checked the manuscript for any other similar errors in 
figure citations and have ensured their accuracy throughout. 
Minor comment 23: 
Section 1.4: bacterial STI transmission through oral sex was not considered in the model. How could this 
simplification affect the epidemics of the three STIs in the model? 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for their insightful comment regarding STI transmission routes and for prompting 
us to clarify our modelling approach. The reviewer correctly noted that our model does not explicitly 
parameterize or simulate distinct transmission routes such as oral or anal sex. As we have now clarified in 
our revised supplementary material (e.g., Section 1.3.4), our model instead defines sexual acts in a 
general, non-site-specific manner. We offer these points to explain the impacts of this methodological choice: 
(1) Regarding overall STI incidence estimates: Because we use this general definition of a sexual act, 
our model is calibrated to overall, non-site-specific annual STI incidence rates from national surveillance data 
(ACCESS project). The model’s calibrated per-act transmission probabilities therefore represent a “blended” 
risk that implicitly accounts for the aggregate transmission potential across all relevant sexual act types

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 47 -->

occurring in the population. This ensures the simulated overall STI burden aligns with empirical data , rather 
than underestimating it by omitting a specific route. 
(2) Regarding doxy-PEP intervention effects: Similarly, the doxy-PEP efficacies used in our model are 
derived from a meta-analysis of real-world trials where infections were acquired through various routes. These 
efficacy values are applied to the generally defined sexual acts in our model, reflecting the overall “blended” 
effectiveness observed in trial settings across different types of exposure. 
(3) Regarding AMR dynamics and limitations:  We acknowledge that a limitation of this generalist 
approach is the inability to model site-specific AMR dynamics, such as those that might occur in the pharynx, 
which is a known reservoir for N. gonorrhoeae and may have distinct selection pressures. To be transparent 
about this simplification, we have expanded our manuscript’s limitations section to include this point. 
To address the reviewer ’s point, we have expanded our manuscript ’s limitations section to discuss this 
simplification as follows: 
“This study has several limitations. First, our model’s structure may not capture all nuances of GBMSM 
sexual networks and behaviours. For instance, our model conceptualizes sexual acts in a general manner, 
which precludes modelling of site -specific dynamics like the role of the pharynx in AMR epidemics, and it 
does not explicitly model the direct impacts of the COVID -19 pandemic on partnership dynamics. However, 
the model’s robust calibration to observed annual STI incidence and antimicrobial resistance trends supports 
the overall validity of our impact estimations.” 
Minor comment 24: 
Section 1.4.4: Given that the STIs are still infectious while under treatment, is the assumption of non -
infectious under treatment due to the recommendation that people should avoid sexual activity without 
protection? 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this insightful question regarding our assumption in Section 1.4.4 that 
individuals are non-infectious while under treatment for STIs. The reviewer’s understanding is indeed correct. 
Our model assumes individuals become non-infectious immediately upon commencing treatment. 
One of the primary rationales for this assumption, as the reviewer aptly suggests, is to reflect the strong 
clinical recommendation that individuals undergoing treatment should abstain from sexual activity, or 
at least avoid unprotected sexual contact, until they and their partners are considered cured or non -infectious 
by their healthcare provider (e.g., as per CDC STI Treatment Guidelines, 2021). Adherence to such advice is 
expected to effectively minimize their contribution to onward transmission. 
Furthermore, this approach of modelling individuals as non-infectious upon treatment is a common 
simplification in STI tran smission dynamics modelling.  For instance, published mathematical models 
effectively treat individuals as non-infectious or transitioning to a non-transmitting state following treatment 
for syphilis (PMID: 35474757), gonorrhoea (PMID: 35427491), and chlamydia (PMID: 21352783). 
While we acknowledge that biological infectiousness for some STIs may not cease instantaneously, this 
modelling assumption, supported by expected behavioural changes and established practice in the field, aids 
in model parsimony. It allows us to capture the primary impact of treatment on reducing transmission without

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 48 -->

incorporating the additional complexity and parameter uncertainty associated with modelling partial 
infectiousness and adherence to behavioural recommendations during the treatment window. We hope this 
clarifies the basis for our model assumption.

It is important to clarify that these were errors in the textual description within the manuscript. We have 
thoroughly verified that our underlying simulation code was implemented with the correct logic from 
the outset for all calculations, including the determination of condomless/condomed acts and the calculation 
of transmission expectations and related inequalities. Therefore, these descriptive typos did not affect our

Minor comment 26: 
Section 2.2: The authors acknowledged the stochastic nature of the individual-based model. For each 
parameter set, the model can generate different results if running multiple times. Did the authors use the mean 
of simulated results from multiple simulations based on one parameter set or use the result from one simulation 
per parameter set with a fixed random generator? 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this important question regarding how we addressed the stochastic nature of 
our individual-based model, as acknowledged in Section 2.2. 
To clarify our simulation procedur e: for each of the 100 selected best -fitting parameter sets, we 
performed one simulation run.  We utilized a fixed universal random seed number (set to 123) for all 
simulations. This approach ensures that the specific simulation run for any given parameter set is reproducible. 
The results presented in our manuscript, such as mean values and 95% CIs, are summaries derived from 
the outputs of these 100 individual simulations —one simulation corresponding to each of the 100 distinct 
parameter sets. For instance,  when a point estimate (mean) is reported, it represents the average of the 
outcomes from these 100 runs. Similarly, the 95% CIs reflect the variation across the results from these 100 
different parameter sets, thereby primarily capturing the impact of parameter uncertainty. 
Therefore, in direct response to the reviewer ’s question, we used the result from one simulation per 
parameter set, with a fixed random generator seed to ensure the reproducibility of each individual simulation 
outcome. In the Methods section of our manuscript, we have added this information as follows: 
“Key model outputs derived from simulations using these 100 parameter sets are reported as mean values 
with 95% confidence intervals (CIs), based on one simulation run per parameter set.” 
Minor comment 27: 
Figure S9: The simulated proportion of NG with tetracycline resistance did not agree with the majority of 
the data points, and the data showed a faster increase during 2019 -2022. A sensitivity analysis with a larger 
background rate to develop tetracycline resistance might be helpful. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for their careful assessment of Figure S9 and their valuable suggestion regarding 
a sensitivity analysis on the background rate of tetracycline /doxycycline resistance development in NG. We 
agree with the reviewer ’s observation that our original model  did not fully capture the nuanced trend of 
doxycycline resistance, particularly the steeper increase observed in the data between 2019 and 2022.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 50 -->

We hypothesized that the observed acceleration in doxycycline resistance in NG from 2019 onwards 
could be partly attributable to changes in Australian clinical guidelines for urethritis management. Prior 
to 2018, azithromycin was a common recommendation for urethritis  (PMID: 31752720), particularly those 
caused by Mycoplasma genitalium (MG) (PMID: 26240201) and Chlamydia trachomatis (CT) (PMID: 
26648627). However, due to increasing macrolide resistance in MG, the 2018 (and current) Australian STI 
Management Guidelines began recommending dox ycycline-containing regimens for non -gonococcal 
urethritis (NGU) (see: https://sti.guidelines.org.au/syndromes/urethritis -penile/). We considered that this 
increased use of doxycycline for treating NGU might have exerted greater bystander selection pressure on co-
colonizing NG, potentially accelerating the emergence of doxycycline -resistant strains. This effect could be 
particularly relevant given doxycycline ’s impact on commensal Neisseria species in the oropharynx, which 
can serve as a reservoir for resistance genes. 
Based on this hypothesis and the reviewer ’s insightful comment, the parameter representing the 
“probability of spontaneous (background) development of high -level doxycycline resistance in NG ” is 
now modelled as a piecewise function, with distinct values estimated before and after mid-2018, coinciding 
with the approximate timing of the guideline shift and the observed inflection in resistance data. These two 
period-specific probabilities were estimated during model re -calibration. The updated model fit, which now 
better captures the observed trends in doxycycline-resistant NG, is presented in following Figure R15: 
 
Figure R15. Comparison of the real data points and the corresponding model outputs. The black points 
represent the real data points. The lines and shaded areas represent the mean and 95% CI of the model outputs.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 51 -->

Furthermore, we concurred with the reviewer ’s suggestion to perform a sensitivity analysis on this 
background resistance parameter and have subsequently conducted it.  It is imp ortant to clarify a key 
aspect of our model structure here: as detailed in of our manuscript ( Intervention scenarios and simulation), 
the calibrated weekly probability of spontaneous doxycycline resistance development is also utilized as the 
per-event prob ability that a doxycycline -susceptible NG infection develops high -level resistance upon an 
individual’s use of a doxy -PEP dose. This approach was adopted due to the unavailability of direct data on 
resistance emergence per specific doxy-PEP exposure. Consequently, the sensitivity analysis on the post-mid-
2018 re-calibrated value of this background resistance rate concurrently affects this crucial parameter related 
to doxy-PEP-induced resistance selection. We varied this dual-purpose parameter from 0.5 times to 1.5 
times its re-calibrated base-case estimate. 
Our findings from this sensitivity analysis (resul ts shown in Figures R8-R11) indicate that varying the 
post-mid-2018 background resistance emergence parameter had a generally modest impact on the 
overall reduction in new STIs and new syphilis cases across the different doxy-PEP strategies. However, 
there was a more discernible, albeit still relatively moderate, influence on the projected change in incidence 
of gonorrhoea with HL TetR and on BCRs. Specifically, a higher assumed value for this background resistance 
emergence rate (and thus for the linked pe r-doxy-PEP exposure resistance probability) tended to result in a 
moderately larger projected additional increase in HL TetR NG attributable to doxy -PEP interventions. This 
is plausible as a higher background rate of resistance development would naturally lead to more resistant 
infections, both spontaneously and following doxy -PEP exposure. Furthermore, a higher background 
resistance emergence rate also led to slightly lower (less favourable) BCRs for the doxy -PEP strategies. This 
could be because an increased emergence of HL TetR NG may reduce the overall effectiveness and net benefits 
of doxy -PEP, particularly concerning gonorrhoea control, thereby diminishing the cost -savings or benefit 
component of the BCR. While ongoing surveillance of AMR is crucial, t hese findings suggest our primary 
conclusions regarding the comparative performance of the doxy -PEP strategies are reasonably robust 
to moderate variations in this specific resistance emergence rate , though it does influence the magnitude 
of projected AMR and cost-effectiveness. 
We believe these model refinements and the added sensitivity analysis have strengthened our study and 
more adequately address the observed data trends. We thank the reviewer again for prompting these 
improvements. 
Minor comment 28: 
Section 3: Is one year long enough to achieve equilibrium in the partnership network? 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this important question regarding the sufficiency of our model’s burn-in period 
for achieving partnership network equilibrium. To demonstrate that our designed burn -in period is 
sufficient for the partnership network to reach a stable equilibrium, we have conducted new analyses 
tracking key network metrics over the initial three years of simulation. These analyses, presented below, 
confirm that simulated distributions for both the annual number of casual partners (ANCP) and for partnership 
statuses progressively align with the  expected distributions, providing strong evidence that the network has 
stabilized before the main outcome analysis begins.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 52 -->

(1) Annual Number of Casual Partnerships Formed:  We recorded the number of casual partnerships 
formed annually by individuals in three distinct groups: PLWH, PrEP users, and PrEP non-
users. These data, presented in the Figure R16 (with rows for each population group and columns for 
each of the three years), show that while some deviations from expected annual numbers of casual 
partners (ANCP) were observed in Year 1 (particularly for individuals with lower expected ANCP, 
possibly due to initially ample partner availability), the distributions in Year 2 and especially Year 3 
(which corresponds to 2012, the first year from which we report our main study outcomes) align 
more closely with the target distributions. 
(2) Distribution of Partnership Statuses: We also recorded the partnership status of all individuals at 
the mid-point of each of these first three years. Individuals were categorized into four states: “only 
casual partner”, “only regular partner ”, “both casual and regular partners ”, or “no partner (None)”. 
While our model parameters define target proportions for the first three categories (as detailed in 
Supplementary Section 1.2.2), the “no partner (None)” category accounts for individuals momentarily 
without partners at the time of assessment. The results, shown in the Figure R17, indicate that in Year 
1, the proportion of individuals with “only casual partner” was higher than expected, while those with 
“only regular partner” or “both” were lower. However, these proportions progressively improved in 
Year 2 and Year 3, moving closer to the target distributions, with a consistently low proportion of 
individuals having no partners. 
It is also pertinent to clarify our model’s established pre-analysis period before outcome recording begins 
in 2012 (which is the third year of simulation). As detailed in the Supplementary, this involves a dedicated 
first year for partnership network stabilization, followed by a second year allowing STI s to distribute after 
initialization. The new analyses presented for the first three years (Figures R16 and R17) were conducted to 
specifically illustrate the network’s progression towards stability across this designed two-year burn-in phase 
and into the initial year of outcome recording. 
Considering the computational demands inherent in agent-based models, these new analyses over the first 
three years (Figures R16 and R17), combined with our multi-stage burn-in procedure, provide evidence that 
the partnership network approaches a reasonably stable state before the main o utcome recording 
period begins. We hope this clarifies that the network has had sufficient time to move towards equilibrium.  
We also included the Figures R16 and R17 in the Supplementary as Figure 6 and 2, and added the text in 
corresponding sections as: 
“As the first two years of the model process were serve as a burn-in period for stabilization of partnership 
network and STI, we compare the model outcomes and the expected distribution of the partnership status in 
Supplementary Figure 2.” 
“As the first two years of the model process were serve as a burn-in period for stabilization of partnership 
network and STI, we compare the model outcomes and the expected distribution of the ANCP in 
Supplementary Figure 6.”

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 53 -->

Figure R16 (Supplementary Figure 6). Comparison of expected distribution of annual number of 
casual partners and the actual distribution of annual number of casual partners in the first three 
years of the model process.

the actual distribution of partnership status. 
Minor comment 29: 
Section 3.2: The implementation of doxy -PEP usually come along with recommendation of frequent

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 54 -->

bacterial STI testing, which were not considered in the model. This could underestimate the impact of doxy -
PEP strategy on bacterial STI preventions. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for raising this important consideration regarding the potential synergy between 
doxy-PEP implementation and recommendations for more frequent bacterial STI testing. 
We agree with the reviewer that, in practice, the introduction of doxy-PEP is often accompanied by advice 
for increased STI screening frequency for its users. This approach is indeed consistent with 
recommendations found in clinical guidelines, including the Australian consensus statement on doxy -
PEP (PMID: 38479437). Our current model did not explicitly incorporate concurrent changes in STI testing 
frequency alongside the various doxy-PEP strategies. 
The primary reason for this was to evaluate the specific epidemiological and economic impact 
attributable to different doxy-PEP eligibility strategies themselves. Introducing simultaneous variations in 
STI screening frequency across these scenarios w ould have substantially increased the complexity of the 
model design and the interpretation of doxy-PEP’s direct effects versus the effects of enhanced testing. 
We recognize the value of assessing such combined interventions.  Our research team plans, as a next 
step, to build upon the findings of the current study. Once optimal doxy-PEP implementation strategies are 
identified, we intend to conduct further modelling research to evaluate the population -level impact of 
combining these strategies with various intensified STI testing recommendations for doxy-PEP users. 
Furthermore, in light of the reviewer ’s comment, we have now added a point to the Discussion section. 
This addition explicitly recommends that future research should investigate the combined public health 
benefits and cost-effectiveness of implementing doxy-PEP in conjunction with enhanced STI screening 
protocols tailored for individuals using doxy-PEP: 
“Indeed, the Australian consensus statement underscores the importance of appropriate STI screening for 
doxy-PEP users, and future modelling studies are needed to quantify the population impact of implementing 
doxy-PEP in conjunction with such potentially enhanced testing protocols, an approach not assessed in the 
current work.” 
Consequently, as the reviewer suggests, by not currently modelling this potential increase in testing that 
might accompany real-world doxy-PEP programs, our study may provide a more conservative estimate of the 
overall public health benefit. We believe that evaluating doxy-PEP as a standalone intervention, as done 
here, provides a crucial baseline understanding, which can then inform more complex investigations of 
combined strategies. 
Minor comment 30: 
Section 3.2.1: In the model, doxy -PEP was assumed to be completely ineffectiv e against NG with 
tetracycline resistance. However, the DOXYV AC trial showed a moderate efficacy against gonorrhea even 
though all strains of NG were resistant to tetracycline. Does this indicate some preventive effect of doxy-PEP 
on NG with tetracycline resistance? 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for raising this important point regarding doxy-PEP efficacy against tetracycline-

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 55 -->

resistant N. gonorrhoeae, particularly in light of the DOXYV AC trial findings. Our model’s assumption of 
0% efficacy is specific to strains with high-level resistance (defined as MIC > 8 mg/L), an approach that 
we believe is supported by a careful reading of the DOXYV AC trial data itself, as well as by separate 
clinical and pharmacokinetic evidence. 
While the DOXYV AC trial (PMID: 38797183) showed moderate overall efficacy against 
gonorrhoea, its data also reveal that this protection is significantly diminished against strains with high-
level resistance. The trial’s overall finding occurred in a population where all cultured strains were “resistant,” 
but this was defined using a low breakpoint (MIC > 0.5 mg/L), where some efficacy is plausible. Crucially, 
the trial’s own data supports our high-level resistance assumption: they found a significantly higher proportion 
of breakthrough infec tions involving strains with high -level resistance (MIC > 8 mg/L, consistent with our 
threshold) in the doxy-PEP group compared to the control group (36% vs 13%, p=0.043), indicating reduced 
protection against these highly resistant isolates. 
This interpretation is further supported by both historical clinical data on treatment failure and 
modern pharmacokinetic studies.  An early study showed treatment failure with single -dose doxycycline 
rising to 100% for gonorrhoea with MICs ≥ 4 mg/L (PMID: 4632883). Furthermore, recent pharmacokinetic 
data show that rectal doxycycline concentrations after a standard doxy-PEP dose may be insufficient to inhibit 
strains with high MICs (PMID: 38428259), providing a clear biological rationale for our assumption. 
Therefore, our dichotomous approach—assuming 0% efficacy against high-level resistant (MIC > 
8 mg/L) strains while applying a meta-analysed efficacy for all other strains—is a robust and evidence-
based simplification.  While more granular, MIC -stratified efficacy data would be ideal, the combined 
evidence from multiple sources supports our conservative assumption. We have added this detailed rationale 
to our Supplementary Material to clarify our methodology as follows: 
“Specifically, our model defines high -level doxycycline resistance in NG as MIC > 8 mg/L. Based on 
evidence suggesting poor clinical outcomes against strains with high MICs, and consistent with other 
modelling work, we assumed doxy-PEP is ineffective against these isolates. We explained the reasons for this 
assumption as following paragraphs: 
Although DOXYV AC trial demonstrated moderate efficacy against gonorrhoea. This finding occurred in 
a population where all cultured NG strains were classified as “resistant” to tetracycline. However, this trial 
used a substantially lower resistance breakpoint (MIC>0.5 mg/L) than our model. At this lower threshold, 
observed efficacy is plausible as many strains classified as “resistant” likely remained susceptible to PEP drug 
concentrations. Importantly, DOXYV AC’s data further supports diminishing efficacy with increasing 
resistance. They found a significantly higher proportion of break through infections involving high -level 
resistance (MIC > 8 mg/L, consistent with our threshold) in the doxy-PEP group versus the control group (36% 
vs 13%, p=0.043), indicating reduced protection against highly resistant strains. 
This interpretation aligns with clinical and pharmacokinetic data. An early study showed treatment failure 
with single-dose doxycycline (300 mg) for NG with MICs ≥ 1 mg/L, rising to 100% failure at ≥ 4 mg/L. 
Furthermore, pharmacokinetic data show that rectal doxycycline concentrat ions post -PEP dose (200mg) 
remained below the MIC for tetracycline -resistant NG (defined > 2 mg/L), providing a biological rationale 
for insufficient drug levels to prevent infection by isolates with high-level resistance (MIC > 8 mg/L).

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 56 -->

Therefore, while direct PEP failure thresholds per MIC were not defined in the meta -analyses informing 
our parameters, the combined evidence (DOXYV AC’s high-MIC findings, treatment failure data, PK studies) 
supports our conservative assumption that doxy -PEP is ineffective a gainst NG with MICs > 8 mg/L. This 
approach is also consistent with prior modelling studies.” 
Minor comment 31: 
Section 3.2.2: For clarification, if an individual on doxy-PEP infected with a doxycycline-susceptible NG, 
is the probability of developing resistance equal to 2K, accounting for the background resistance development 
rate? 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this clarifying question. To enhance clarity in the manuscript regarding this 
specific scenario, we have added the following explanation to Section 3.2.2: 
We thank the reviewer for this excellent clarifying question. To answer directly: the probability of 
resistance developing in that week is generally not equal to 2K. Because there are multiple independent 
probabilistic events (one for the background rate, and one for each doxy-PEP dose taken), the total probability 
is calculated as 1 - (1-K)ⁿ, where ‘n’ is the number of opportunities for resistance to emerge. 
The relationship to 2K therefore depends on the number of doxy -PEP doses an alread y-infected 
individual takes in a given week, which is influenced by both their number of condomless sex acts and 
their adherence. 
(1) If no dose is taken:  If an individual has condomless sex but does not take doxy -PEP due to non -
adherence (which occurs with a 20% probability per event in our model), there is only one opportunity 
for resistance to emerge that week: the spontaneous background mutation. The total probability is 
simply K. 
(2) If one dose is taken: There are two opportunities for resistance to emerge (n=2): one from the weekly 
background rate and one from the single doxy-PEP dose. The combined probability is 1 - (1-K)², which 
is slightly less than 2K. This directly addresses the scenario you raised. 
(3) If multiple doses are taken:  The number of opportunitie s increases. For example, if an individual 
uses two doxy-PEP doses in one week, there are three opportunities (n=3; one background + two from 
doxy-PEP), and the total probability becomes 1 - (1-K)³. 
To ensure this concept of multiple “rounds” of resistance development is clear in our manuscript, we have 
added an explanatory note to the Supplementary Material: 
“Thus, an individual infected with a doxycycline -susceptible gonorrhoea strain would experience two 
potential “rounds” of resistance development in a given week: (1) a fixed weekly background mutation, and 
(2) an additional doxy -PEP use-dependent mutation event if doxy -PEP is taken by that infected individual 
during the week.” 
We hope this explanation clarifies how the probability of resistance development relates to 2K under 
different scenarios of doxy-PEP use by an already infected individual.

description of the outcome of cumulative number of incidental gonorrhea infections. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for their meticulous reading and this helpful suggestion to improve the precision

The reviewer is correct. While we do categorize newly incident gonorrhoea infections by their antibiotic 
susceptibility status (as described earlier in Section 4.1) and report annual incidence rates accordingly (listed 
as outcome (2): The annual incidence rates of gonorrhoea infections by antibiotic susceptibility), our original 
description for outcome (4): The cumulative number of incidental gonorrhoea infections from 2025 to 2034 – 
did not explicitly reiterate that this cumulative number is also analysed and presented by AMR susceptibility 
status. 
To ensure clarity and consistency with our other listed outcomes, we have revised Section 4.1 of our 
Supplementary to read: 
“(4) The cumulative number of incidental gonorrhoea infections by antibiotic susceptibility from 2025 to 
2034.” 
We appreciate the reviewer pointing out this oversight, allowing us to enhance the clarity of our 
manuscript.

Figure S11: It seems that the plot with the title “One syphilis diagnosis ” should be for  the scenario 
targeting HIV+ or PrEP-using GBMSM since it was the only scenario which was not cost-saving. 
Response: 
We sincerely thank the reviewer for their meticulous attention to detail and for identifying this error in a 
panel title within Figure S11. As the reviewer rightly deduced by comparing this with our main findings, these 
panels were assigned the wrong title. We apologize for this oversight, and this figure has been corrected. 
Minor comment 34: 
Sections 5.2.7 and 5.2.8: Could the authors elaborate on the results based on different doxy-PEP efficacy? 
Specifically, why did the low efficacy of doxy-PEP lead to a smaller incidence of gonorrhea with doxycycline 
AMR? For example, under scenario 5 where the number of individuals on doxy-PEP was not affected by the 
sensitivity analysis, wouldn ’t the low efficacy lead to a higher incidence of gonorrhea and more GBMSM 
infected with NG can develop doxycycline resistance due to doxy-PEP? 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this insightful question regarding our sensitivity analysis of doxy-PEP efficacy 
and for prompting us to elaborate on this counterintuitive finding. The reviewer rightly noted an expectation 
that lower doxy -PEP efficacy might lead to more op portunities for resistance development. However, our 
model indicates the opposite trend. We believe this phenomenon is best explained by the balance between 
two distinct pathways for the proliferation of resistant gonorrhoea: acquired resistance  (de novo  
resistance developing in a susceptible strain due to doxy -PEP use) and transmitted resistance  
(transmission of a pre-existing resistant strain). We have created a new schematic (Figure R18) based on 
our gonorrhoea model flowchart to illustrate these dynamics.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 58 -->

When doxy-PEP efficacy is higher, it is more effective at preventing infection with or clearing exposures 
to N. gonorrhoeae  without high-level tetracycline resistance (HL TetR) . In our model, this means more 
individuals return to the susceptible (S) state after exposure (annotated as arrow (1) in Figure R18). This 
effective suppression of susceptible strains, however, expands the overall pool of individuals in the population 
who are susceptible to any gonorrhoea infection. This larger susceptible pool, in turn, creates more 
opportunities for the transmission of already circulating strains with HL TetR, as shown by arrow (2) in Figure 
R18. These new infections with resistant strains cannot be prevented by doxy-PEP, and the infected individuals 
have a longer window to transmit them onward. Thus, higher doxy -PEP efficacy primarily drives an 
increase in transmitted resistance. 
Conversely, when doxy-PEP efficacy is lower , fewer suscept ible infections are cleared (arrow (1) is 
smaller). This leads to a higher overall prevalence of gonorrhoea infections, particularly those susceptible to 
doxycycline. The higher number of individuals with breakthrough susceptible infections who are also ta king 
doxy-PEP increases the number of opportunities for de novo, or acquired resistance, to emerge (arrow (3) in 
Figure R18). Thus, lower doxy-PEP efficacy primarily drives an increase in acquired resistance. 
The counterintuitive trend observed by the reviewer arises because, in our model, the increase in 
transmitted resistance  driven by higher efficacy is greater than the increase in acquired resistance 
driven by lower efficacy. This is attributable to the relatively high baseline prevalence of HL TetR gonorrhoea 
in the simulated population; with a substantial existing reservoir of resistant strains, expanding the pool of 
susceptible individuals (the high -efficacy scenario) has a very pronounced effect on their  subsequent 
transmission. This conclusion is further supported by a separate one -way sensitivity analysis, which showed 
that directly varying the per -doxy-PEP-use probability of acquiring resistance had only a modest impact on 
the cumulative number of new HL TetR gonorrhoea infections (as shown in the tornado plots in Figure R10). 
Crucially, despite this observed increase in the incidence of HL TetR gonorrhoea under conditions of 
higher doxy-PEP efficacy, the overall total incidence of gonorrhoea (both susceptible and resistant strains 
combined) was still substantially reduced in these scenarios.  This indicates a net public health benefit in 
terms of total gonorrhoea prevention. We hope this explanation, supported by the schematic in Figure R18, 
clarifies the mechanisms behind this important dynamic.

Minor comment 35: 
Section 5.2.9: Figure S44 showed the results of one -way sensitivity analysis instead of Figure S43. In 
addition, did the bar plot show the mean BCR using 100 parameter sets or the results based on one parameter 
set? Is it possible to provide the uncertainty range based on the 100 parameter sets? 
Response: 
Thank you for your observations regarding Section 5.2.9 and the sensitivity analysis figure. We address 
your points as follows: 
1) Figure numbering: You are correct; the figure cited in Section 5.2.9 should have been S44, not S43, 
in the original manuscript. But due to the new analysis results, we have removed the results of the 
previous bloated and unclear sensitivity analysis including the Figure S44. 
2) Source of bar plot data: The bar plot in the original figure did indeed show the mean benefit-cost 
ratios (BCRs) calculated from the 100 best-fitting parameter sets. 
3) Uncertainty range: Following your valuable suggestion, we have now incorporated error bars  
representing the 95% confidence intervals (CIs) for each bar in the Figures R8-11. 
To elaborate on how these error bars are presented: In a one-way sensitivity analysis, varying a parameter 
typically results in a range of outcome va lues (e.g., a lower and a higher BCR). To reflect the uncertainty 
associated with this range, for the lower bound observed due to parameter variation, we have presented its 95% 
CI lower bound. Conversely, for the upper bound, we have presented its 95% CI upper bound. This approach 
is intended to visually highlight the extent of uncertainty primarily in the direction of the parameter’s induced 
effect on the outcome range. 
The Figures R8-11 now provides a clearer depiction of both the mean effect of these parameter variations 
on BCRs and their associated uncertainty. We appreciate you prompting this enhancement to our sensitivity 
analysis presentation, and have included these figures as Supplementary Figure 20-23.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 61 -->

Response to the comments from Reviewer 2 
Comment 1 (Key results): 
The manuscript presents an individual -based model of gonorrhea, chlamydia, and syphilis transmission 
among gay, bisexual, and other men who have sex with men (GBMSM) in Australia to estimate the impact of 
seven different definitions of eligibility for Doxy -PEP. The eligibility criteria are defined through 
combinations of HIV status, HIV PrEP use, and STI history. The study estimated multiple outcome measures 
for each of definition of eligibility, including impacts on the burden of STIs, antimicrobial resistance, and the 
cost-effectiveness of Doxy -PEP. The study suggests that the widest eligibility criteria led to the greatest 
reduction in STIs, while the simulations with the most restrictive e ligibility criteria (namely, users of HIV 
PrEP and people living with HIV/AIDS with a syphilis diagnosis) had the greatest benefit -cost ratio while 
limiting antimicrobial resistance in N. gonorrhoeae. 
Response: 
We sincerely thank Reviewer 2 for their time,  effort, and this comprehensive summary of our work. We 
are pleased that the summary accurately captures the core objectives, methodology, and key findings of our 
individual-based modelling study on Doxy-PEP eligibility strategies among GBMSM in Australia. 
Your concise overview correctly highlights our evaluation of seven different Doxy -PEP eligibility 
definitions based on HIV status, PrEP use, and STI history, and our assessment of their impacts on STI burden, 
antimicrobial resistance, and cost-effectiveness. We particularly appreciate you noting the main conclusions, 
such as the trade-off between the strategies. 
We have carefully considered all the comments and suggestions provided by Reviewer 2 in the full review 
and have addressed them in detail in our point-by-point responses and through revisions to the manuscript. In 
our point-by-point response below, the key points are formatted in bold. Any new or revised text that has been 
added to the manuscript or supplementary materials is quoted and highlighted in red for clarity. We found the 
feedback to be very constructive and believe it has helped us to improve the clarity and strength of our paper. 
Thank you once again for your thoughtful and accurate appraisal of our study. 
Comment 2 (Validity, Significance, Data and methodology): 
The study draws valid conclusions from the simulated data. However, some of the assumptions are 
questionable, the modeling approach would benefit from additional validation and sensitivity analyses, and 
the results, discussion, and conclusions should reflect the uncertainty in the parameters. I’m not sure how best 
to interpret the base case presented in Figures 1, for example, in which STI rates are projected to be stable. 
My understanding is that rates of STIs have been steadily increasing in Australia. Moreover, the authors report 
that they used 100 parameter sets, but uncertainty intervals are lacking from the figures and results. This makes 
it difficult to understand how to interpret the authors’ results and claims. 
The study addresses an important and timely question, by comparing different Doxy-PEP strategies along 
multiple policy -relevant dimensions, including incidence of multiple bacterial STIs, drug resistance in N. 
gonorrhoeae, and economic costs and benefits. Results from this study aim to inform the implementation of 
Doxy-PEP in Australia to help reduce bacterial STIs. However, questions about the underlying model, its 
parameterization, and the associated uncertainties make it challenging to interpret the manu script’s 
conclusions. 
The modeling approach is appropriate to answer the research question, the model parametrization is 
mostly grounded in the literature, and the categories of outcomes examined offer a comprehensive picture of 
both health and economic im plications of various eligibility definitions. However, the study would benefit

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 62 -->

from (1) additional validation of the calibration procedure, including calibration to additional targets, and (2) 
more extensive sensitivity analysis. 
Response: 
We sincerely thank the reviewer for their thorough and constructive initial assessment of our manuscript, 
acknowledging its important and timely research question, the appropriateness of the modelling approach, and 
the comprehensive scope of outcomes examined. We also greatly appreciate you highlighting key areas where 
further clarification, validation, and analysis would strengthen our study and the interpretation of its 
conclusions. 
We agree that robust model assumptions, comprehensive validation (including calibration to appropriate 
targets), extensive sensitivity analyses, and the clear presentation of uncertainty are all crucial elements for a 
rigorous modelling study. We have carefully considered your overarching feedback and have made significant 
revisions and additions throughout the manuscript and supplementary materials to address these points. 
1. Regarding base case STI rate projections and model calibration: 
We understand your concern about interpreting base case STI projections, particularly in light of observed 
rising STI trends in Australia. To address this, and your suggestion for calibration to additional targets, we 
have refined our model calibration. As will be detailed in our responses to your next comments on model 
calibration, our model now incorporates annual STI incidence trends from 2012-2022 as key calibration 
targets. These incidence data were sourced from national surveillance (ACCESS project) and calculated by 
our co-authors for distinct GBMSM subgroups (HIV-positive, HIV-negative PrEP users, HIV-negative PrEP 
non-users), with the methodology detailed in a published study (PMID: 39263009). This enhancement ensures 
our baseline projections more closely align with real -world epidemic dynamics before the introduction of 
Doxy-PEP. The new calibration results could be found in  the copy of Figure R15, and was also included in 
the Supplementary as Figure 16. 
 
Figure R15 (a copy for convenience). Outcomes of the model validation. The black points represent the 
real data points. The lines and shaded areas represent the mean and 95% CI of the model outputs.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 63 -->

2. Concerning the presentation of uncertainty: 
We acknowledge the importance of clearly reflecting parameter uncertainty in our results. Our findin gs 
are indeed derived from 100 distinct best-fitting parameter sets, and we typically report mean outcomes with 
95% Confidence Intervals (CIs). While these CIs are consistently provided in key summary tables (e.g., Table 
2), we opted not to display overlapping shaded CIs on all multi -scenario line graphs (like the Figure 1) 
primarily to maintain visual clarity and readability given the number of strategies compared. However, 
recognizing your point, we have ensured that uncertainty is more explicitly presented in other graphical 
forms where feasible, for instance, by incorporating error bars representing 95% CIs in newly added 
summary bar charts (e.g., for cumulative outcomes) and within our sensitivity analysis figures (e.g. , 
tornado plots).  We will elaborate on specific figures in response to any detailed comments on results 
presentation. Here we show the Figure 1 in the main text as an example: 
 
Figure 1. Projected impact of various doxy -PEP strategies on STI incidence, do xy-PEP prescriptions, 
coverage, and intervention efficiency among GBMSM (2024 -2034). Panels (A) to (D) illustrate the mean 
annual incidence rates (per 100 person-years) of gonorrhoea, chlamydia, syphilis, and all three STIs combined, 
respectively, across different doxy-PEP scenarios. Panel (E) shows the mean annual doxy -PEP prescriptions 
(in thousands). Panel (F) depicts the mean annual doxy-PEP coverage percentage among eligible individuals. 
Panel (G) presents the mean cumulative incident STIs (syphilis, c hlamydia, gonorrhoea; left y -axis, in 
thousands) and the mean cumulative doxy-PEP prescriptions (right y-axis, in thousands) over the 2025-2034 
period. Panel (H) displays the mean total infections averted (IA; left y -axis, in thousands) and the mean 
infections averted per doxy -PEP prescription (IA per prescription; right y -axis) over the 2025 -2034 period. 
All results shown are means (with or without 95% CI) calculated from simulations based on 100 parameter 
sets. The black dashed line in panel (A) refers to  the average incidence rate in 2020. The red dashed lines in 
panels (A) to (C) represent the 2030 target incidences, derived by reducing respective 2020 incidences to meet

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 64 -->

WHO’s reduction targets (90% for gonorrhoea, 50% for chlamydia, and 90% for syphilis ). Doxy -
PEP=doxycycline post-exposure prophylaxis. GBMSM=gay, bisexual, and other men who have sex with men. 
IA=infections averted. PrEP=pre -exposure prophylaxis. PY=person -years. STIs=sexually transmitted 
infections. WHO=World Health Organization. 
We affirm the significance of the research question and are committed to ensuring our conclusions are 
well-supported and clearly interpreted. The detailed point -by-point responses that follow will elaborate on 
specific model assumptions, parameterization c hoices, the refined calibration process (including new targets 
and validation against observed partnership structures where possible), and other specific queries you have 
raised. The question s about the additional sensitivity analyses  have been presented i n our responses to 
following comments 10 -15, which were specific to this topic.  We are confident that these comprehensive 
responses and the corresponding manuscript revisions will address your concerns. 
Comment 3 (Parameters and calibration): 
The model was  calibrated to positivity rates of gonorrhea, chlamydia, and syphilis tests, and the 
prevalence of decreased ceftriaxone susceptibility and tetracycline resistance in a sample of isolates from the 
Melbourne Sexual Health Centre (lines 161ff, supplement pages 20-22). The authors report calibration results 
by comparing fitted model outputs to the observed values of the calibration targets in Figure S9. However, the 
information provided is insufficient to assess how well the model reproduces the total number o f infections 
and diagnoses, which is important since one of the key outcome measures in the study is the number of 
infections that may be averted by Doxy-PEP. Specifically: …(left text shown in following comments) 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this important comment regarding the validation of our model against the scale 
of infections and diagnoses. To more directly address your concern about the model’s ability to reproduce 
infection volumes, we have significantly revi sed our primary calibration strategy. Our model is now 
calibrated against annual incidence rates for gonorrhoea, chlamydia, and syphilis (from 2012 -2022), 
which are stratified by key GBMSM subgroups (HIV -positive, PrEP-using, and PrEP non-using) and 
derived from national surveillance data (ACCESS project).  
As demonstrated in our new calibration results figure ( Figure R17, appended below for convenience), 
this revised approach ensures the model accurately reproduces the observed rates at which new infection s 
occur over time, which is crucial for robustly estimating the number of infections averted by doxy -PEP. The 
detailed estimation methodology for the target annual incidence rates used in our calibration has been 
published by our co-authors (PMID: 39263009). By calibrating to these dynamic incidence rates, we ensure 
that the model accurately reproduces the rate at which new infections are occurring within these populations 
over time. We believe this level of validation against dynamic incidence rates streng thens the model ’s 
predictive capabilities, and the model's goodness -of-fit compares favourably with other recently published 
individual-based models addressing STI dynamics. 
Furthermore, to ensure our model reflects real -world diagnostic intensity, we now incorporate empirical 
annual STI testing frequency data for our three key GBMSM subgroups, derived from the ACCESS system 
(supporting Figure R7). 
For the reviewer ’s convenience, copies of the aforementioned Figure R7  (detailing STI testing 
frequencies) and Figure R15 (showing the model calibration to incidence rates) are appended below this 
response.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 65 -->

time.

real data points. The lines and shaded areas represent the mean and 95% CI of the model outputs.

- How many isolates from Melbourne Health Centre were  tested for antimicrobial resistance and how

Response: 
We appreciate the reviewer’s question concerning the number of N. gonorrhoeae isolates tested for AMR 
at the Melbourne Sexual Health Centre (MSHC) and their representativeness for the broader gonococcal 
population in Australia, particularly for our study focusing on gay, bisexual, and other men who have sex with 
men (GBMSM). 
The Australian Gonococcal Surv eillance Programme (AGSP ,

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 66 -->

https://www1.health.gov.au/internet/main/publishing.nsf/Content/cda-pubs-annlrpt-gonoanrep.htm) provides 
national surveillance for N. gonorrhoeae  AMR and has been expanding its reach, with the number of 
gonococcal isolates tested across Australia generally showing an increasing trend. According to the latest 
available AGSP report of 2022, a total of 8,199 isolates were cultured nationally. Of these, 4,898 
(approximately 59.7%) underwent susceptibility testing for tetracycline, whic h we use as a proxy for 
doxycycline in our study. Victoria, the state where MSHC is located, contributed the largest number of these 
tetracycline-tested isolates, accounting for 2,428 (approximately 49.6% of the national total tested for 
tetracycline) in 2 022. The MSHC itself is recognized as Australia ’s largest public sexual health clinic 
(PMID: 40375254 ) and plays a significant role in this AMR surveillance.  The number of gonococcal 
isolates cultured and tested for AMR at MSHC has also increased over time . For 2022, the most recent year 
of AMR data utilized in our model calibration, MSHC performed antimicrobial susceptibility testing 
(including for ceftriaxone and tetracycline) on 1,488 gonococcal isolates. This means that in 2022, isolates 
from MSHC accou nted for approximately 30.4% (1,488 / 4,898) of all isolates tested for tetracycline 
susceptibility nationally under the AGSP, and approximately 61.3% (1,488 / 2,428) of those tested for 
tetracycline susceptibility in the state of Victoria. 
Given MSHC’s status as a major public sexual health service, its substantial contribution to both Victorian 
and national AMR surveillance data, and critically, its extensive service to the GBMSM population, we 
consider the AMR proportion data derived from MSHC isolates to be a strong and reasonably representative 
indicator of gonococcal antimicrobial susceptibility patterns among Australian GBMSM for the purposes of 
our model calibration. While national data aggregates isolates from diverse populations, the MSHC data 
provides more focused insight into the specific GBMSM group our model simulates. We believe this 
detailed context supports the use of MSHC AMR data as a key input for calibrating resistance trends relevant 
to our study population. 
Comment 5: 
- How well does the model capture the incidence and prevalence for chlamydia, gonorrhea, and syphilis 
in Australia? It would be important to include additional calibration targets that account for the total volume 
of infections and/or testing. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this question regarding how well our model captures STI incidence and 
prevalence in Australia, and the suggestion for additional calibration targets to account for the total volume of 
infections. 
Regarding the use of prevalence data, we opted not to use STI prevalence as a primary dynamic calibration 
target due to the challenges in obtaining continuous, annual, community -based STI prevalence survey 
data that is stratified by the key GBMSM subgroups (HIV-positive individuals, HIV-negative PrEP users, 
and HIV-negative PrEP non-users) over our entire calibration period (2012-2022). Such detailed longitudinal 
prevalence data for all three STIs across these specific subgroups is not readily available for the Australian 
context in a format suitable for dynamic model calibration. 
Consequently, as outlined in our response to your preceding comment concerning our calibration strategy 
(Comment 3), we chose to primarily calibrate and validate our model using annual STI incidence rates. 
Our refined calibration strategy, therefore, focuses on these annual incidence rates for gonorrhoea, chlamydia, 
and syphilis, covering the period 2012 -2022. These rates are stratified for the three key GBMSM subgroups 
(HIV-positive individuals, HIV-negative PrEP users, and HIV -negative PrEP non-users) and for the overall 
simulated population. The derivation of these target incidence rates from national surveillance data 
(ACCESS project) by our co -authors, including the detailed methodology, has been published.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 67 -->

Calibrating our model to these dynamic, subgroup-specific incidence rates ensures that the generation of new 
infections within our simulated population aligns with observed epidemiological trends. 
The outcomes of this comprehensive calibration against annual incidence are presented in Figure R15. 
This figure demonstrates the model’s strong capability to reproduce the observed annual incidence rates and 
their temporal patterns for all three STIs, especially for the overall population figures. We are confident that 
this validation against dynamic incidence data significantly strengthens the model ’s predictive capacity for 
estimating intervention impacts, including the number of infections averted. For additional context, the 
goodness-of-fit achieved and our model’s ability to replicate these complex temporal STI dynamics compare 
favourably with other sophisticated individual -based models in the field, including relevant work recently 
published in Nature Communications. 
By calibrating to these detailed annual incidence rate s (Figure R15) and incorporating realistic 
testing frequencies ( Figure R7), we believe our model now more effectively accounts for the volume of 
infections and provides a solid foundation for assessing the impact of doxy-PEP. 
For the reviewer ’s convenience, copies of the aforementioned Figure R7  (detailing STI testing 
frequencies) and Figure R15 (showing the model calibration to incidence rates) are appended below this 
response. 
 
Figure R7 (a copy for convenience). Simulation and calibration of the changing STI testing rates over 
time.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 68 -->

Figure R15 (a copy for convenience). Outcomes of the model validation. The black points represent the 
real data points. The lines and shaded areas represent the mean and 95% CI of the model outputs. 
Comment 6: 
- The authors claim their model ‘aligns well with actual epidemic trends’ (line 392), but the base case of 
the model seems to project unchanging incidence of all three STIs. Could the authors please point to data that 
support stable incidence for these STIs in Australia? 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this pertinent observation regarding our model’s base case projections in light 
of historically increasing STI trends in Australia. We agree that our baseline projections must realistically 
reflect these underlying epidemiological dynamics. 
As detailed in our response to your previous comment (Comment 5, concerning calibration targets), our 
model’s calibration strategy has been significantly revised. We now primarily calibrate against annual STI 
incidence rates for gonorrhoea, chlamydia, and syphilis (from 2012 -2022), stratified by key GBMSM 
subgroups (HIV-positive, HIV-negative PrEP users, HIV-negative PrEP non-users) and for the overall 
population, using estimates derived from national surveillance data (ACCESS project). This ensures that 
our model’s outputs during the historical calibration period align with observed increasing incidence trends.  
With this updated calibration, our revised base case projections for the future period (2023 -2034, before any 
Doxy-PEP intervention) present a more nuanced outlook. While the overall incidence for all three STIs is 
generally projected to continue an upward trend initially, our model suggests that the incidence may 
begin to stabilize and potentially show a slight decrease after 2030. 
Several dynamic factors incorporated into our revised model contribute to these projected future trends. 
One contributing factor is the declining proportion of individuals living with HIV over time  (as shown 
in Figure R6A), reflecting observed trends and demographic changes. Given that the HIV-positive population 
has historically had a higher burden of these STIs, a reduction in this population size can contribute to a 
moderation in overall STI incidence.  Furthermore, the model includes a  significant scale -up of PrEP 
coverage among HIV -negative GBMSM (Figure R6B), coupled with higher STI testing frequencies

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 69 -->

among PrEP users (Figure R7). Increased testing leads to earlier detection and treatment of STIs, which can 
reduce onward transmission and consequently temper the rise in incidence or even lead to declines for some 
infections. It is also plausible that after a period of sustained increase, the transmission dynamics for certain 
STIs within specific sexual networks may approach a form of natural equilibrium or “bottleneck”, especially 
when influenced by evolving prevention and testing behaviours like those associated with PrEP uptake. 
For the reviewer’s convenience, copy of the aforementioned Figure R6 (trends of HIV prevalence and 
PrEP coverage) was appended below this response. 
 
Figure R6 (a copy for convenience). Simulation and calibration of the changing HIV prevalence and 
PrEP coverage over time. Panel (A) shows the observed annual HIV prevalence among Australian GBMSM 
from the Gay Community Periodic Surveys (GCPS) for 2012 -2023 (blue points with 95% CIs) and the 
corresponding mean HIV average prevalence emerging from our model simulations (red points). The  model 
was initialized with 9.5% HIV prevalence in 2012, and the subsequent trend is driven by the model's dynamic 
population turnover rather than an imposed external trend. Panel (B) shows the observed annual PrEP coverage 
among HIV-negative GBMSM from th e GCPS for 2012 -2023 (blue points). The dashed line represents the 
logistic curve fitted to the solid data points, which is used to model the PrEP uptake trend in our simulation. 
The two hollow data points (for 2021-2022) were excluded from the curve fitting process as potential outliers 
likely influenced by the COVID-19 pandemic. The latest national data shown for both panels is from 2023. 
By calibrating to historical annual incidence data and incorporating these dynamic population and 
behavioural factors, our revised base case projections now provide a more realistic foundation against which 
the impact of doxy-PEP interventions can be assessed. 
Comment 7: 
- Given the complexity of sexual behavior that is simulated in the model, it would be helpful to compare

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 70 -->

the number of partnerships, the distribution of the numbers of regular and casual partners, or other measures 
of sexual behavior with those observed empirically, e.g., those reported by the authors in Figure S3. This 
would help validate the assumptions of sexual behavior, which may impact how infections are spread across 
the population and how well different eligibility criteria for Doxy-PEP alter those transmission patterns. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this important suggestion, and in response, we have conducted new analyses 
to explicitly validate our model ’s emergent sexual network structure against key empirical data.  
Ensuring that our simulated partnership network reflects real-world sexual behaviour is crucial for the validity 
of our STI transmission dynamics and intervention impact estimates. These new analyses, presented below, 
were performed by tracking key partnership metrics over the initial three years of the simulation. 
These new analyses confirm that after an initial burn-in period, the model successfully reproduces 
key empirically-derived network characteristics, including the distributions of annual casual p artners 
and partnership statuses (new Figures R15 and R16): 
(1) Annual number of casual partners (ANCP): Our model is parameterized using empirically -derived 
target ANCP distributions for our three key GBMSM subgroups. Our new analysis ( Figure R15) 
shows that after an initial stabilization period, the emergent ANCP distributions in the model align 
well with these empirical targets for each subgroup. 
(2) Distribution of partnership statuses: Our model also aims to reflect empirical proportions of GBMSM 
who engage in different types of partnerships (e.g., only regular partners, only casual partners, or both). 
Our new analysis ( Figure R16) tracked the simulated distribution of these partnership statuses and 
confirmed that, once stabilized, they closely matched the targ et distributions derived from empirical 
observations. 
By comparing these stabilized, model -generated metrics against empirical targets, we have 
validated that our model’s assumptions lead to realistic emergent network properties before the main 
analysis pe riod begins. As detailed in our response to Reviewer 1 (Minor Comment 28), our model 
incorporates a two -year burn -in period (one year for network stabilization, one year for STI distribution) 
before outcome recording starts in 2012. The new validation anal yses presented in Figures R15 and R16  
demonstrate that this period is sufficient for the network to reach a reasonably stable state. This provides 
confidence that our evaluation of doxy -PEP interventions is based on a partnership structure that adequately 
reflects key observed patterns in the Australian GBMSM population. 
For the reviewer ’s convenience, cop ies of the aforementioned Figures R16 and R17 were appended 
below this response. We also included the Figures R16 and R17 in the Supplementary as Figures 6 and 2, 
and added the text in corresponding sections as: 
“As the first two years of the model process were serve as a burn-in period for stabilization of partnership 
network and STI, we compare the model outcomes and the expected distribution of the partnership status in 
Supplementary Figure 2.” 
“As the first two years of the model process were serve as a burn-in period for stabilization of partnership 
network and STI, we compare the model outcomes and the expected distribution of the ANCP in 
Supplementary Figure 6.”

partners and the actual distribution of annual number of casual partners in the first three years of the 
model process.
