the actual distribution of partnership status. 
Comment 8: 
- The authors state that they calibrated the background probability of doxycycline resistance emergence

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 72 -->

based on data from 2015-2022 and cite Table S4 (section 3.2.2. should cite Table S2 not S4). However, those 
data are perplexing. From 2015 -18, the proportion with tetracycline AMR is steady around 8%, and then it 
jumps to around 20% from 2019-21, and then to 30% in 2022. Is it clear what’s driving this change from 2019-
22, why it only started in 2019, and whether this driver is expected to continue unabated? 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for their careful observations regarding the trends in tetracycline/doxycycline 
resistance in N. gonorrhoeae as shown in our  calibration data, for noting the citation error  (which has been 
corrected), and for querying the drivers of these trends. 
First, to address the perplexing segmented trend in resistance, we hypothesized that the observed 
acceleration from 2019 onwards is a ttributable to a change in Australian clinical guidelines for 
urethritis management around mid-2018. Prior to this, azithromycin was a common recommendation for 
non-gonococcal urethritis (NGU), particularly those caused by Mycoplasma genitalium  (MG) (PMID:  
26240201) and Chlamydia trachomatis (CT) (PMID: 26648627), and for urethritis more generally (PMID: 
31752720). However, due to increasing macrolide resistance in MG, the 2018 (and current) Australian STI 
Management Guidelines began recommending doxycyclin e-containing regimens for NGU (see: 
https://sti.guidelines.org.au/syndromes/urethritis-penile/). We posited that this increased use of doxycycline 
for treating NGU might have exerted greater bystander selection pressure on co -colonizing N. gonorrhoeae, 
potentially accelerating the emergence of doxycycline -resistant strains. This timing aligns with the observed 
inflection in the resistance data. Based on this hypothesis, our model now parameterizes the background rate 
of high -level tetracycline resistance (HL TetR) emergence as a piecewise function , with distinct rates 
calibrated for the periods before and after mid-2018. This revised approach provides a much better fit to the 
observed data, as demonstrated in our updated calibration results in Figure R15. Our model assumes this 
higher post-mid-2018 rate persists in future projections, reflecting current clinical practice. 
Second, to explore the uncertainty around this ongoing higher rate of resistance emergence, we 
conducted a one -way sensitivity analysis on the calibrated post -mid-2018 background resistance 
parameter (with results shown in Figures R8–R11). It is important to note that this parameter serves a dual 
role in our model, also acting as a proxy for the per -use probability of resistance developing from doxy -PEP 
itself. We varied this dual-purpose parameter from 0.5 times to 1.5 times its re-calibrated base-case estimate. 
Our findings indicate that varying this parameter had a generally modest impact on the overall reduction in 
new STIs and syphilis. However, as expected, it had a more discernible, albeit still moderate, influence on the 
projected increase in HL TetR gonorrhoea and on the benefit-cost ratios (BCRs). A higher assumed resistance 
emergence rate led to a moderately larger increase in HL TetR gonorrhoea and slightly lower (less favourable) 
BCRs. While ongoing AMR surveillance is cruc ial, these results suggest our primary conclusions regarding 
the comparative performance of the doxy-PEP strategies are reasonably robust to moderate variations in this 
key resistance parameter. 
Comment 9: 
- Table S5 reports the doxy -PEP efficacy for preve nting gonorrhea at 45%, with a range for sensitivity 
analysis from 13-66. However, is the success of doxy-PEP in preventing gonorrhea reflective of the prevalence 
of resistant strains, rather than an “intrinsic” likelihood of success? 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this insightful question regarding the 45% efficacy of doxy -PEP used for 
preventing gonorrhoea and how this relates to the prevalence of resistant strains versus an “intrinsic” efficacy. 
We agree entirely with the reviewer ’s premise: the observed 45% efficacy from meta -analyses is indeed a 
“blended,” real-world value reflecting performance in populations with mixed gonococcal susceptibility, not

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 73 -->

a purely “intrinsic” efficacy against perfectly susceptible strains. Our modelling approach is designed to reflect 
this reality by dichotomizing the problem: we assume 0% efficacy  against infections with high -level 
tetracycline resistance (HL TetR, defined as MIC > 8 mg/L), while applying the meta-analysed 45% efficacy 
to all other N. gonorrhoeae strains that do not meet this high-level resistance criterion. 
This approach is supported by recent clinical and pharmacokinetic evidence. The 45% efficacy applied 
to non-HL TetR strains is appropriately conservative because trials show imperfect pre vention even 
against strains considered “susceptible” by lower thresholds.  For instance, the DoxyPEP trial reported 
breakthrough infections even with strains susceptible at an MIC < 2 mg/L (PMID: 37018493). Furthermore, 
pharmacokinetic studies suggest that rectal doxycycline concentrations following a standard doxy-PEP 
dose may be insufficient to reliably inhibit strains with even intermediate resistance (PMID: 38428259). 
This evidence, combined with findings from different trials showing varied efficacy ba sed on the local 
prevalence of strains with different MICs (PMIDs: 37018493, 38797183) , supports our decision to use a 
meta-analysed average efficacy for all non -highly-resistant strains rather than assuming a higher, purely 
“intrinsic” value. 
Therefore, using the meta-analysed 45% efficacy represents a robust and pragmatic approach given the 
current data landscape. It provides a more stable and generalizable estimate than data from any single 
study, which could be heavily influenced by its specific local A MR prevalence. While we acknowledge 
that more granular, MIC -stratified efficacy data would enhance model precision, our approach —
distinguishing between complete ineffectiveness against high -level resistant strains and a partial, meta -
analysis-derived effectiveness against others—is a reasonable way to model doxy -PEP’s impact in a mixed -
resistance environment. The uncertainty around this parameter is further explored in our sensitivity analysis 
(range 13%–66%). 
Comment 10 (Sensitivity analyses): 
The study includes extensive sensitivity analysis of parameters related to Doxy -PEP, including varying 
the length of the prescription, uptake and adherence in the eligible population, efficacy, and cost parameters 
(see lines 220-227). However, the study did not conduct sensitivity analysis on key parameters of the natural 
history of each STI, sexual behavior, or those governing the emergence of resistance. Here are some examples 
of parameters that should be included in such a sensitivity analysis: 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for acknowledging the extensive sensitivity analyses already performed on 
parameters related to doxy-PEP implementation (such as prescription length, uptake, adherence, efficacy, and 
cost parameters). We also appreciate the valuable suggestions for performing additional sensitivity analyses 
to explore the uncertainties associated with key model assumptions regarding the natural history of each STI, 
sexual behaviour patterns, and parameters governing the emergence of antimicrobial resistance. 
In response to this, we have indeed conducted several new one-way sensitivity analyses addressing many 
of the specific parameters highlighted by the reviewer. Our approach has been to assess the impact of these 
parameter variations on four key outcomes that we use for evaluating and ranking the doxy-PEP strategies: (i) 
overall reduction in new STIs, (ii) reduction in new syphilis cases, (iii) change in the incidence of gonorrhoea 
with high-level tetracycline resistance (HL TetR), and (iv) benefit-cost ratios (BCRs). 
For clarity and to provide a consolidated overview, the main results of all one -way sensitivity 
analyses—including those initially planned and these new analyses performed based on your 
suggestions—are presented across a set of summary fi gures: Figures R8 -11. Each of these figures 
corresponds to one of the four key outcome metrics listed above. Within each figure, individual panels 
typically represent the different doxy-PEP strategies, and tornado plots illustrate how the respective outcome

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 74 -->

varies as each parameter is changed across its plausible range. 
We included the detailed description of the one-way sensitivity analysis in our supplementary as following 
text: 
“5.2 One-way sensitivity analyses 
To assess the robustness of our model’s projections and to identify key parameters influencing the 
epidemiological and economic outcomes of the evaluated doxy-PEP strategies, we conducted extensive one-
way sensitivity analyses. In these analyses, individual model parameters were varied one at a time across 
plausible ranges, while all other parameters were held at their base-case values (as detailed in supplementary 
Table 2, unless otherwise specified). The impact of these variations was assessed on four primary outcomes: 
the cumulative reduction in overall new STIs, the cumulative reduction in new syphilis cases, the cumulative 
change in new gonorrhoea infections with HL TetR, and the BCRs over the 10-year intervention period 
(2025-2034). The results of these one-way sensitivity analyses for each of the five doxy-PEP strategies are 
presented as tornado plots in Supplementary Figures 20–23. 
The key parameters varied in these sensitivity analyses, along with their tested ranges, included: 
(5) Doxy-PEP intervention parameters: 
F. Prescription duration: varied from 3 months (13 weeks) to 12 months (52 weeks), would also 
lead to the prescribed doxy-PEP range from 26 to 104 doses (base case: 6 months). 
G. Uptake rate among eligible individuals: 50% to 100%, (base case: 75%). 
H. Adherence to taking doxy-PEP post-exposure: 60% to 100%, (base case: 80%). 
I. Pathogen-specific efficacies of doxy-PEP: 
d) Gonorrhoea: 13% to 66% (base case: 45%). 
e) Chlamydia: 56% to 92% (base case: 81%). 
f) Syphilis: 64% to 86% (base case: 77%). 
J. Probability of HL TetR development in N. gonorrhoeae per doxy -PEP use: varied by ±50% 
around the base -case value (which is linked to the calibrated weekly post -mid-2018 
background spontaneous HL TetR development rate). 
(6) STI natural history and epidemiological parameters: 
A. Proportion of new gonorrhoea infections that are symptomatic: 15% to 45% (base case: 30%). 
B. Probability of ceftriaxone treatment failure for gonorrhoea with ceftriaxone DS: varied by ±50% 
around the calibrated base-case values. 
C. Background weekly rate of sponta neous HL TetR development in N. gonorrhoeae (post mid -
2018): varied by ±50% around its calibrated base-case value. 
D. Initial STI positive rates (prevalence at start of 2012) for key subgroups (HIV -positive, PrEP-
users): varied by ±50% around their calibrated base-case values. 
(7) Behavioural parameters: 
A. Condom use frequency among individuals reporting “sometimes” using condoms: varied from 
25% (lower protection) to 75% (higher protection) of sexual acts with condom use (base case: 
50%). 
B. Proportions of individuals in each subgroup who “always use condoms”: baseline proportions

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 75 -->

(from Supplementary Figure 8) varied by multiplying them by 0.5 and 1.5. 
(8) Economic parameters: 
Annual discount rate for costs and QALYs: 0% to 6% (base case: 3%).”

Comment 11: 
- For example, the reported values for the proportion of gonococcal infections that are symptomatic varies 
widely in the literature. The value of 0.3 in this s tudy seems reasonable but should be varied in sensitivity

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 79 -->

analyses, as symptom status impacts diagnosis rates and these in turn impact Doxy-PEP eligibility. 
Response: 
We agree with the reviewer that the proportion of gonococcal infections that are symptoma tic is a 
parameter with variability in reported values and that a sensitivity analysis is warranted due to its potential 
impact on diagnosis rates and, consequently, on the outcomes of doxy-PEP strategies (particularly those with 
eligibility criteria linked to STI diagnoses). 
Our base-case model assumes that 30% of new gonococcal infections are symptomatic. We conducted a 
one-way sensitivity analysis by varying this proportion to a lower value of 15% and a higher value of 
45%. The impact of these variations on our four key outcomes (overall reduction in new STIs, reduction in 
new syphilis cases, change in incidence of gonorrhoea with high-level tetracycline resistance (HL TetR), and 
benefit-cost ratios (BCRs)) for each doxy-PEP strategy is presented in Figures R8-R11. 
Varying the symptomatic proportion of gonorrhoea demonstrated a moderate impact on the 
projected overall STI reduction, syphilis reduction, and BCRs (as shown in Figures R8, R9, and R11, 
respectively). The general trend observed was that a higher proportion of symptomatic gonococcal infections 
(i.e., 45%) resulted in fewer total STIs averted by doxy-PEP strategies, fewer syphilis infections averted, and 
consequently, lower BCRs. This is likely because a higher sympt omatic rate leads to more gonorrhoea cases 
being diagnosed and treated through routine clinical pathways, thereby reducing the baseline pool of infections 
that doxy-PEP can additionally prevent or the overall transmission intensity that doxy-PEP modifies. 
Interestingly, the change in incidence of gonorrhoea with HL TetR (shown in Figure R10) appeared 
to be more sensitive to this parameter.  When the proportion of symptomatic gonococcal infections was 
assumed to be higher (45%), the additional increase in HL TetR gonorrhoea attributable to some doxy -PEP 
strategies was notably reduced, with the lower bound of the outcome range (represented by the extent of the 
tornado plot bar) approaching zero for certain strategies. This suggests that if a larger fraction of gonorrhoea 
infections naturally progress to symptomatic disease and are treated conventionally, the selective pressure 
exerted by doxy -PEP for resistance development might be lessened, potentially leading to little or no net 
additional increase in HL TetR gonorrhoea under those specific assumptions for some strategies. 
In summary, while the proportion of symptomatic gonococcal infections does influence the projected 
outcomes, particularly the dynamics of HL TetR gonorrhoea, the overall conclusions regarding  the potential 
for STIs to be averted and the general cost -effectiveness trends across the different doxy -PEP strategies 
remained broadly consistent within the tested range of this parameter. 
Comment 12: 
- Also, as the authors state (e.g., lines 191-192), the probability of doxycycline resistance emerging with 
the use of Doxy -PEP is poorly understood. To account for this uncertainty, it would be helpful to explore 
smaller or larger values than the 0.05% fixed in the analysis. 
Response: 
We agree with the reviewer that the probability of N. gonorrhoeae developing high-level resistance due 
to doxy-PEP use is an important parameter with inherent uncertainty, warranting a sensitivity analysis. First, 
we wish to clarify its parameterization in our recalibrated model, as the “0.05% fixed” value is from a previous 
version. 
Our model incorporates two distinct pathways for the emergence of high-level tetracycline resistance (HL 
TetR): (1) a spontaneous background rate, which was re-calibrated to 0.135% per week (95% CI: 0.068%–
0.193%) for the period from mid -2018 onwards, and (2) a separate per-doxy-PEP-use probability. Due to

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 80 -->

the lack of direct empirical data for this per -use probability, we set its base-case values to be equivalent to 
this calibrated weekly background rates. 
To account for the uncertainty specifically around the impact of the intervention, we then performed a 
one-way sensitivity analysis exclusively on the per -doxy-PEP-use probability of resistance emergence. 
We explored values from 0.5 to 1.5 times its base -case estimate s. Crucially, during this analysis, the 
spontaneous background resistance rate was not changed and remained fixed at its calibrated value. The 
impact of this variation on our four key outcomes is presented in Figures R8-R11. 
Our findings indicate that changing this doxy-PEP-induced resistance probability within the tested range 
had a generally modest impact on overall STI reductions and benefit -cost ratios. As expected, it had a more 
discernible, though still moderate, influence on the projected increase in HL TetR gonorrhoea ( Figure R10). 
The overall impact of this parameter's variation was less pronounced than that of other factors, such as condom 
use. Therefore, our primary conclusi ons appear reasonably robust to moderate uncertainty in this 
specific intervention-related resistance probability. 
Comment 13: 
- Similarly, a rate of 10% treatment failures for strains with reduced ceftriaxone susceptibility was defined 
through calibration. Only very few treatment failures to ceftriaxone have been reported in the literature, so it 
is unclear which MICs are associated with treatment failure and at which frequency. Sensitivity analyses 
should explore whether dynamics change if this the treatment failure proportion is higher or lower. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for their comment regarding the treatment failure (TF) rate for N. gonorrhoeae 
(NG) strains with ceftriaxone decreased susceptibility (DS) and for recommending a sensitivity analysis on 
this parameter. We would like to clarify that following our model re -calibration, the base-case probability of 
treatment failure for NG infections identified as having ceftriaxone DS is now re-calibrated to 7.6% (95% 
CI: 6.0%–9.8%). 
Regarding t he literature on ceftriaxone treatment failures, we acknowledge the reviewer ’s point that 
directly reported microbiological failures are rare. However, studies on post -treatment DNA persistence can 
offer some related insights, though DNA persistence does not directly equate to viable infection or treatment 
failure and can also be due to reinfection. For instance, a study (2014, PMID: 25371490) investigated the 
persistence of NG DNA following standard treatment. They found that while overall DNA persistence at day 
14 post-treatment was around 8% for both pharyngeal and rectal infections, for a small subgroup of isolates 
(n=10) with elevated MICs for both ceftriaxone (≥0.06 mg/L) and azithromycin (≥0.5 mg/L), DNA persistence 
was notably higher at 30%. This 30%  persistence in a specific, potentially harder -to-treat subgroup 
(though based on a small sample) is considerably higher than our model ’s calibrated TF rate of 7.6% 
for strains with ceftriaxone DS (where ceftriaxone DS is defined by ceftriaxone MIC >0.06 m g/L, without 
a concurrent azithromycin resistance criterion for the TF probability itself). This comparison suggests our 
calibrated TF rate is within a plausible range, potentially on the conservative side relative to specific high-risk 
scenarios reported in literature. 
To explore the impact of uncertainty in this TF rate, we conducted a one -way sensitivity analysis by 
varying our calibrated rates by 0.5 times and 1.5 times its base -case value s. The influence of these 
variations on our four key outcomes (overall reduction in new STIs, reduction in new syphilis cases, change 
in incidence of gonorrhoea with high-level tetracycline resistance (HL TetR), and benefit-cost ratios (BCRs)) 
is presented in Figures R8-R11. 
Our findings from this sensitivity analysis indicate that variations in the ceftriaxone TF rate had a

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 81 -->

generally modest impact on overall STI and syphilis reduction, but a more discernible influence on both HL 
TetR gonorrhoea dynamics and cost-effectiveness ( Figures R8 -R11). Specifically, a higher assumed 
probability of ceftriaxone TF led to a larger projected increase in HL TetR gonorrhoea attributable to doxy -
PEP interventions. A plausible mechanism for this is that more persistent infections with ceftriaxone DS (due 
to higher TF) create more opportunities for doxy-PEP, when taken for prophylaxis against other exposures, to 
select for acquired high -level tetracycline resistance. Conversely, a higher ceftriaxone TF rate also led to 
slightly larger (more favourable) BCRs for the doxy-PEP strategies, likely because an increased baseline STI 
burden and its associated management costs make the infections averted by doxy -PEP more economically 
valuable. This reveals a nuanced but moderate trade -off, where a higher ceftriaxone treatment failure 
rate can, to a certain extent, improve the relative cost -effectiveness of doxy -PEP while exacerbating 
specific AMR outcomes. 
In conclusion, while the assumed ceftriaxone treatment failure rate for strains  with ceftriaxone DS does 
exert some influence, particularly on AMR dynamics for gonorrhoea and on overall cost -effectiveness, the 
primary conclusions of our study regarding the comparative performance of the doxy -PEP strategies 
appeared reasonably robust to the tested variations in this parameter. 
Comment 14: 
- Another parameter that would benefit from revisiting and from sensitivity analysis is the time until 
spontaneous recovery, which was defined to be 33 weeks (see supplement). Recent evidence suggests thi s is 
far shorter (see Barbee, 2021 and Barbee, 2022, natural history studies for both rectal and pharyngeal 
gonorrhea). 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for their suggestion to revisit the parameter for the time until spontaneous recovery 
from asymptomatic gonorrhoea and for providing references to recent evidence, including the work by Barbee 
et al. (2021, 2022). 
To clarify our methodology, our model does not use a fixed 33-week duration but instead stochastically 
assigns each asymptomatic gonococcal infection a recovery time from a gamma distribution. This distribution 
is parameterized to have a mean of approximately 33 weeks, with a 95% CI spanning approximately 16.5 to 
55.2 weeks. Our choice of this distribution and its parameters was primarily informed by a systematic review 
by Chow et al. (2016, PMID: 26886136), which synthesized data on clearance times for untreated gonococcal 
infections at different anatomical sites.  This review reported distinct mean clearance times for  pharyngeal 
gonorrhoea (approxim ately 114–138 days, or ~16 -20 weeks)  and, importantly, for rectal gonorrhoea 
(mean approximately 346 days, or ~49 weeks) . Our model’s gamma distribution, with its 95% CI of 
16.5–55.2 weeks, was designed to be consistent wit h and encompass these different s ite-specific 
clearance durations. 
Furthermore, the recent evidence kindly provided by the reviewer, including the work by Barbee 
et al. (2021, 2022), is consistent with the parameters of our chosen distribution.  The study by Barbee et 
al. on pharyngeal gonorrhoea (2021, PMID: 33513222) estimated a median clearance of 16.3 weeks, which 
aligns well with the pharyngeal data in the Chow et al. review and falls within the lower end of our gamma 
distribution's 95% CI. More recently, the Barbee et al. study on rectal gonorrhoea (2022, PMID: 34355734) 
found a shorter median duration of 9 weeks, but importantly, also noted that some rectal infections persisted 
considerably longer (6-11 months, or approx. 26-48 weeks). Our gamma distribution, with its wide CI (16.5–
55.2 weeks) and its ability to accommodate a skewed distribution with a long tail, was specifically designed 
to capture this overall heterogeneity, encompassing the shorter pharyngeal and median rectal clearance times 
as well as the longer -persisting rectal infections identified in both the Chow et al. review and the work by

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 82 -->

Barbee et al. 
Because our model already incorporates this significant variability by sampling from this 
comprehensive distribution for each infection, a separate one -way sensitivity analysis on this parameter 
was not performed. This approach ensures that the natural heterogeneity in gonococcal clearance times is an 
intrinsic feature of our simulation. The resulting variation in individual reco very times naturally contributes 
to the overall uncertainty observed in our final simulated outcomes (e.g., the 95% confidence intervals around 
our main findings), thereby addressing the uncertainty associated with this parameter by design. 
Comment 15: 
- Assumptions around sexual behavior are known to play an important role in estimates of the impact of 
different interventions produced by individual-based models (see Althaus et al, 2012). As mentioned above, it 
would be helpful to understand how well the mo del reproduces empirically observed partnership structures, 
and in sensitivity analyses, explore how changes in the assumed sexual behavior alter the outcomes of different 
Doxy-PEP eligibility criteria. For example, if contacts and infections are more (less) concentrated in a subset 
of the population, do strategies with narrower (wider) eligibility criteria become more cost-effective? It would 
also be useful to conduct sensitivity analyses on sexual behavior parameters, such as the extent of condom use 
in the population. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for their insightful comments regarding the validation of our model ’s sexual 
behaviour assumptions and for suggesting further sensitivity analyses on these important parameters. 
First, to address the need for validation, we have conducted new analyses confirming that our 
simulated partnership network successfully reproduces key empirically observed structures after an 
initial burn-in period. As detailed in our response to your Comment 7, we confirmed that the emergent annual 
number of casual partners (ANCP) distributions and the proportions of individuals in different partnership 
configurations within our model align well with their empirically -derived target distributions. These new 
validation analyses (present ed as Figures R16 and R17 ) provide confidence that our model ’s assumptions 
lead to realistic emergent network properties. 
Second, as suggested, we performed additional one-way sensitivity analyses on key sexual behaviour 
parameters to explore their impact on strategy outcomes, with results presented in our summary 
tornado plots (Figures R8–R11). 
(1) Regarding the concentration of infections:  As a proxy to explore sensitivity to risk concentration, 
we varied the initial STI prevalence in the high -risk subgroups (HIV-positive and PrEP -using 
individuals), multiplying the calibrated base -case values by 0.5 and 1.5. We found that variations in 
these initial subgroup prevalences had a generally modest impact on overall STI and syphilis reduction. 
However, the influence was more pronounced on the projected increase in HL TetR gonorrhoea and 
on BCRs; specifically, a higher initial STI prevalence tended to be associated with a larger subsequent 
increase in HL TetR gonorrhoea attributable to doxy-PEP, and also with larger (more favourable) BCRs. 
(2) Regarding the extent of condom use: We performed a sensitivity analysis by varying the proportion 
of individuals who “always use condoms” within each of our three GBMSM subgroups, multiplying 
the baseline proportions by 0.5 and 1.5. This variation had a moderate impact on all four key outcomes. 
A higher proportion of “always use condoms ” (i.e., greater background protection) generally led to 
smaller absolute reductions in new STIs and syphilis from doxy -PEP, likely due to a lower bas eline 
incidence. Concurrently, this higher level of condom use was associated with a smaller increase in the 
incidence of HL TetR gonorrhoea and resulted in smaller (less favourable) BCRs, as the incremental

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 83 -->

benefit of doxy-PEP is reduced when baseline condom use is already high. 
These validation and sensitivity analyses contribute to a better understanding of our model ’s robustness 
and how variations in key sexual behaviour parameters might influence the projected impacts of doxy -PEP. 
The results have been incorporated into the revised manuscript and supplementary materials. 
Comment 16: 
The study would benefit from some improvements in the analytical approach, including (1) the statistical 
analysis and definition of outcomes and (2) the visualization and presentation of the results. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for their suggestions regarding potential improvements to our analytical approach, 
including aspects of statistical analysis, outcome definition, and the visualization and presentation of results. 
We have carefully considered these overarching points and will address them in detail within our responses to 
your specific comments that follow, where these particular aspects are elaborated upon. 
Comment 17: 
The sets of eligibility criteria that the authors compare are relevant and established in the literature and 
each strategy is assessed along several dimensions of outcome measures. Health impacts are assessed 
primarily by the number of averted infections. However, as strategies differ in the size of the eligible 
population, it would be helpful to include a measure of infections averted normalized by the number of 
prescriptions. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this constructive suggestion. We agree that including a measure of infections 
averted normalized by the number of prescriptions is very helpful for comparing strategies, especially since 
the size of the eligible population differs substantially across the various doxy-PEP eligibility criteria. 
We are pleased to confirm that we have incorpor ated this metric into our revised analyses and 
figures. Our updated Figure 1 now presents a comprehensive overview which includes not only annual STI 
incidence rates (Panels A -D) and cumulative new STIs with doxy -PEP prescriptions (Panel G), but also 
specifically details the cumulative STIs averted by each doxy -PEP strategy and the STIs averted normalized 
by the number of doxy-PEP prescriptions (Panel H). 
Observing panel H of the revised Figure 1, for instance, allows for nuanced interpretations: while the 
strategy of providing doxy -PEP to all PrEP-using attendees results in the largest cumulative number of new 
STIs averted over the 2025 -2034 period, the strategy targeting individuals diagnosed with syphilis 
demonstrates the highest number of new STIs averted per doxy-PEP prescription. This highlights the different 
dimensions of impact and efficiency for each strategy, as per your suggestion. 
We believe this addition provides a more complete picture for comparing the efficiency of the different 
doxy-PEP strategies. The revised Figure 1 and related text in the Results section were presented as follows: 
“Epidemiological impact and doxy-PEP utilization 
We illustrate the increasing STI incidence rates in the base case and the reductions observed after 
implementing doxy-PEP with various strategies (Fig. 1A-D). In the context of the WHO 2030 goals for STI 
reduction from 2020 levels (red dashed lines in Fig. 1A-C), modelling projects that by 2030, the five evaluated 
doxy-PEP strategies could reduce chlamydia incidence  by 14.6-83.8% and syphilis incidence by 1.7 -95.6%, 
compared with their respective 2020 levels. Notably, strategies targeting individuals with two STI diagnoses

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 84 -->

in the past 12 months or all PrEP-using GBMSM were projected to achieve the WHO’s chlamydia reduction 
target, with the latter also meeting the syphilis reduction target by 2030. However, while doxy -PEP initially 
reduced gonorrhoea incidence, projected 2030 levels under all strategies surpassed the 2020 baseline (black 
dashed line in Fig. 1A), typically rebounding within two to three years of implementation due to increasing 
doxycycline resistance; nevertheless, incidence remained below the projected base-case (no doxy-PEP) level. 
Annual doxy-PEP prescription volumes and population coverage vari ed substantially across strategies 
(Fig. 1E-F). Most scenarios showed a peak in prescriptions in the first year of implementation before 
stabilizing. This resulted in projected average annual coverage levels among eligible individuals ranging from 
a low of approximately 3.9% (95% CI: 3.1-4.6%) for the strategy targeting individuals with syphilis diagnosis 
to a high of 26.2% (95% CI: 26.1-26.3%) for the strategy targeting all PrEP-using attendees. 
Cumulatively ( Fig. 1G ), in the base case, we projected 51,798  (47,449–56,147) total incident STIs 
between 2025 and 2034, including 19,669 (95% CI: 16,809–22,529) gonorrhoea infections, 23,054 (19,863–
26,245) chlamydia infections, and 9,075 (7,307–10,842) syphilis infections. Across the five intervention 
strategies, the cumulative number of STIs averted ranged from 8,564 (95% CI: 7,686–9,442) for the strategy 
targeting all HIV-positive attendees to 28,096 (95% CI: 25,259–30,933) for the strategy targeting all PrEP -
using attendees, corresponding to overall STI reductio ns of 16.5% to 54.2% ( Fig. 1G and H). Specifically, 
the strategy targeting individuals with a syphilis diagnosis averted 11,589 (95% CI: 9,360–13,818) infections, 
a reduction of 22.4%. The epidemiological impacts required a wide range of intervention inten sity, from a 
cumulative 1,623 (1315 -1931) prescriptions for the syphilis diagnosis strategy to 11,429 (11,403-11,455) 
prescriptions for the PrEP -using attendees strategy. Consequently, intervention efficiency, measured as 
infections averted (IA) per prescription, was highest for the syphilis diagnosis strategy (7.1 IA per prescription; 
95% CI: 7.0–7.2) and lowest for the PrEP-using attendees strategy (2.5 IA per prescription; 95% CI: 2.4–2.5).”

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 85 -->

Figure 1. Projected impact of various doxy -PEP strategies on STI incidence, doxy -PEP 
prescriptions, coverage, and intervention efficiency among GBMSM (2024 -2034). Panels (A) to (D) 
illustrate the mean annual incidence rates (per 100 person -years) of gonorrhoea, chlamydia, syphilis, and all 
three STIs combined, respectively, across different doxy -PEP scenarios. Panel (E) shows the mean annual 
doxy-PEP prescriptions (in thousands). Panel (F) depicts the mean annual doxy -PEP coverage percentage 
among eligible individuals. Panel (G) presents the mean cumulative incident STIs (syphilis, chlamydia, 
gonorrhoea; left y -axis, in thousands) and the mean cumulative doxy -PEP pr escriptions (right y -axis, in 
thousands) over the 2025-2034 period. Panel (H) displays the mean total infections averted (IA; left y-axis, in 
thousands) and the mean infections averted per doxy-PEP prescription (IA per prescription; right y-axis) over 
the 2025-2034 period. All results shown are means (with or without 95% CI) calculated from simulations 
based on 100 parameter sets. The black dashed line in panel (A) refers to the average incidence rate in 2020. 
The red dashed lines in panels (A) to (C) represent the 2030 target incidences, derived by reducing respective 
2020 incidences to meet WHO ’s reduction targets (90% for gonorrhoea, 50% for chlamydia, and 90% for 
syphilis). Doxy-PEP=doxycycline post-exposure prophylaxis. GBMSM=gay, bisexual, and other me n who 
have sex with men. IA=infections averted. PrEP=pre-exposure prophylaxis. PY=person-years. STIs=sexually 
transmitted infections. WHO=World Health Organization. 
Comment 18: 
In lines 263ff, the authors describe the number of gonorrhea infections with re duced susceptibility to

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 86 -->

ceftriaxone and resistance to doxycycline. However, the number of infections alone might not be sufficient to 
evaluate changes in AMR, since the strategies produce different numbers of total infections. Including the 
proportion of infections with decreased susceptibility or resistance might help better understand the resistance 
implications of each strategy. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this insightful suggestion. We agree that evaluating changes in AMR solely by 
the absolute number of resistant infections might not provide a complete picture, especially since different 
intervention strategies result in varying overall numbers of gonorrhoea infections. Presenting the proportion 
of infections exhibiting resistance can indeed off er a clearer understanding of the relative resistance 
implications of each strategy. 
In our revised manuscript, we now utilize two figures, Figure 2 and a new Figure 3, to 
comprehensively illustrate the impact of various doxy-PEP strategies on the antimicrobial susceptibility 
patterns of N. gonorrhoeae. Figure 2, which is an evolution of the previous version, continues to present the 
annual incidence rates (Panels A -D) and cumulative new infections (Panel E) for gonorrhoea, stratified by 
four distinct antim icrobial susceptibility profiles: (A) “Susceptible to both ” (exhibiting neither ceftriaxone 
decreased susceptibility (DS) nor high -level tetracycline resistance (HL TetR)); (B) “Ceftriaxone DS only”; 
(C) “HL TetR only”; and (D) “Both” (concurrent ceftriaxone DS and HL TetR). Furthermore, Figure 2 now 
also explicitly quantifies the additional number of HL TetR  (including the “HL TetR only ” and “Both”) 
gonorrhoea infections projected for each doxy-PEP strategy relative to the base case (no doxy-PEP) over the 
10-year period, addressing the aspect of absolute numbers. Complementing this, the new Figure 3 directly 
addresses the reviewer’s recommendation by focusing on proportions; this figure illustrates the annual changes 
in the proportion of these four antimicr obial susceptibility profiles among all newly incident gonorrhoea 
infections, from 2024 (the year immediately preceding the 10-year intervention period) through 2034, for the 
base case and the five doxy-PEP intervention strategies. 
By presenting both the absolute incidence and cumulative numbers of infections by susceptibility profile 
(Figure 2) alongside the annual proportions of these profiles among new infections ( Figure 3), we aim to 
provide a multi-dimensional perspective on how each doxy-PEP strategy influences gonococcal AMR trends. 
A consistent finding emerging from both these figures is that the strategy providing doxy-PEP to PrEP-using 
attendees would lead to the largest projected number and the highest proportion of new gonorrhoea infections 
exhibiting HL TetR. Conversely, the strategy of providing doxy-PEP to individuals diagnosed with syphilis is 
associated with the smallest absolute number and the lowest proportion of new infections with HL TetR. 
We believe this dual approach to presenting AMR changes provides a more robust and nuanced 
understanding of the implications of each doxy-PEP strategy. The revised Figures 2 and 3 and related text in 
the Results section were presented as follows: 
“Impacts on antimicrobial profiles of gonorrhoea 
Although all doxy -PEP strategies reduced overall gonorrhoea incidence, we observed different 
antimicrobial susceptibility patterns among these infections ( Figs. 2 and 3 ). General ly, the incidence of 
gonorrhoea infections without HL TetR decreased under most doxy-PEP strategies compared to the base case 
(Fig. 2A and B), while broader strategies typically led to greater increases in strains with HL TetR ( Fig. 2C 
and D ). Notably, the  strategy targeting individuals with syphilis diagnosis presented a distinct pattern: it 
resulted in a slightly higher incidence of “Ceftriaxone DS only” strains but uniquely projected a decrease in 
“HL TetR only” strains below base case levels after 2030 (Fig. 2B and C). Furthermore, this syphilis-targeting 
strategy resulted in the smallest cumulative impact on gonorrhoea with HL TetR relative to the base case,

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 87 -->

leading to a net increase of 372 infections (95% CI: -234–979) over 10 years; in contrast, presc ribing doxy-
PEP to all PrEP -using attendees led to the largest cumulative increase of 3,732 additional gonorrhoea 
infections with HL TetR (95% CI: 2,827–4,637), an adverse impact more than tenfold greater in magnitude 
(Fig. 2E). 
Examining the annual propor tions of these antimicrobial susceptibility profiles among newly incident 
gonorrhoea infections revealed similar trends ( Fig. 3 ). In the base case, the proportion of new infections 
exhibiting HL TetR rose from 39.7% in 2024 to 68.3% by 2034. The strategy t argeting individuals with 
syphilis diagnosis showed the smallest proportional increase in HL TetR among doxy -PEP interventions, 
reaching 71.9% by 2034. Conversely, prescribing doxy-PEP to all PrEP-using attendees resulted in 98.8% of 
new gonorrhoea infections exhibiting HL TetR by 2034, implying doxy-PEP would then be effective against 
only 1.2% of incident gonorrhoea.” 
 
“Figure 2. Impact of doxy -PEP on incident gonorrhoea infections across different antibiotic 
susceptibility statuses among GBMSM. Panels (A)-(D) show mean annual gonorrhoea incidence rates (per

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 88 -->

100 person-years) under various scenarios, by antibiotic susceptibility status: (A) “Neither” (exhibiting neither 
ceftriaxone DS nor HL TetR); (B) “Ceftriaxone DS only”; (C) “HL TetR only”; and (D) “Both” (concurrent 
ceftriaxone DS and HL TetR). Panel (E) projects 10-year outcomes (2025-2034): the left y-axis presents mean 
cumulative total gonorrhoea infections (thousands), by these four susceptibility categories. The right y -axis 
displays the mean cumulative increase in gonorrhoea infections exhibiting HL TetR (sum of “HL TetR only” 
and “Both” categories, in thousands) compared to the base case, with 95% CI error bars. All results shown are 
means (with or without 95% CI) calculated from simula tions based on 100 parameter sets. GBMSM= gay, 
bisexual, and other men who have sex with men. STIs=sexually transmitted infections. Doxy -
PEP=doxycycline post-exposure prophylaxis. PrEP=pre-exposure prophylaxis. DS=decreased susceptibility. 
HL TetR=high-level tetracycline resistance (used as a proxy for doxycycline resistance).” 
 
“Figure 3. Annual proportions of incident gonorrhoea infections with different antibiotic susceptibility 
statuses across various scenarios, 2024 to 2034.  Each panel (A -F) corresponds to a specific scenario, 
illustrating the changing annual proportions of four N. gonorrhoeae antibiotic susceptibility statuses among 
incident infections over the period shown. The stacked areas represent infections characteriz ed by: neither 
ceftriaxone DS nor HL TetR; ceftriaxone DS only; HL TetR only; and both ceftriaxone DS and HL TetR. 
Percentage labels on the left of each panel denote these respective proportions for 2024 (the year immediately 
preceding the 10 -year intervention period), while labels on the right denote proportions for 2034 (the final 
year of the 10 -year intervention period). All results shown are means calculated from simulations based on 
100 parameter sets.DS=decreased susceptibility; HL TetR=high-level tetracycline resistance (used as a proxy 
for doxycycline resistance); Doxy -PEP=doxycycline post-exposure prophylaxis; STIs=sexually transmitted 
infections; PrEP=pre-exposure prophylaxis.”

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 89 -->

Comment 19: 
I would also recommend extending the analysis to explain wh ich characteristics of each strategy are 
driving the outcomes (e.g., reduced infections but rising resistance). For example, is it better to prescribe 
Doxy-PEP broadly or try different methods of targeting those most likely to a) take prescribed Doxy-PEP and 
b) be exposed to gonorrhea? Is there a discernible relationship between the number of individuals who are 
eligible for Doxy-PEP and the selection for resistance? For example, in lines 333ff, the authors write: “Our 
model outcomes suggest that doxy -PEP strategies targeting individuals diagnosed with syphilis should be 
prioritised over other strategies, as they provide the best balance between reducing STI incidence, minimising 
AMR development, and achieving strong economic returns on investment”. Is this because the strategy targets 
a small number of individuals or because it reduces syphilis transmission, specifically? Identifying the driving 
forces behind the strategy rankings may help draw more generalizable conclusions from the analysis beyond 
a recommendation for a single strategy. 
Finally, I would be interested to see more interpretation of multiple outcomes simultaneously. Currently, 
the outcomes are presented as individual analyses and strategy performance is not assessed along all outcome 
measures jointly (beyond a subset of outcomes presented in Table 2 and a couple sentences on ideal strategies 
in lines 299-304 but without much reference to data). The text would benefit from an exploration of pairs or 
sets of outcomes, such as the trade-off between averting infections and reducing antibiotic use. This could also 
be achieved visually in the form of a scatterplot of strategies with, say, the number of pills used and the 
number/percentage of infections averted. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for thes e constructive and detailed suggestions to extend our analysis. We agree 
that a deeper explanation of the characteristics driving the outcomes, a more explicit interpretation of multiple 
outcomes simultaneously, and the visualization of inherent trade -offs are crucial for drawing robust and 
generalizable conclusions. We have undertaken several new analyses and expanded our discussion  to 
address these points. These new analyses and the expanded framework provide a much richer, multi -faceted 
understanding of strategy performance. These findings and the new figures have been incorporated into 
the revised manuscript to provide the deeper interpretation you recommended. 
First, to explore the discernible relationship between the scale of doxy -PEP implementation and 
selection for resistance, we analysed the correlation between doxy -PEP coverage and the cumulative 
increase in resistant gonorrhoea. We plotted the projected average annual doxy-PEP coverage rate against 
the cumulative number of new gonorrhoea infections with high -level tetracycline resistance (HL TetR) for 
each intervention scenario over the 10 -year period. The results, presented in a new figure ( Figure R19), 
indicate a strong positive Spearman correlation between average annual doxy -PEP coverage and the 
cumulative number of new HL TetR gonorrhoea infections. This suggests that strategies resulting in higher 
overall population coverage with doxy -PEP are directly associated with a greater increase in these resistant 
infections, highlighting a key trade-off.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 90 -->

Figure R19. Relationship between average annual doxy-PEP coverage and the cumulative number 
of additional gonorrhoea infections with high -level tetracycline resistance (HL TetR) by doxy -PEP 
strategy (2025-2034). Each data point represents one of the five doxy-PEP intervention strategies. The x-axis 
shows the projected average annual doxy-PEP coverage (%) among eligible individuals for each strategy. The 
y-axis shows the cumulative number of additional new gonorrhoea infections with HL TetR over the 10-year 
period (2025-2034) compared to the base case (no doxy-PEP). A Spearman correlation analysis was performed 
to assess the association between these two variables across the strategies. 
We included the Figure R19 as the Supplementary Figure 17, and added one sentence t o report this 
finding in our manuscript as: 
“Exploratory analyses also found that higher doxy-PEP coverage was significantly positively correlated 
with more cumulative gonorrhoea infections with HL TetR across strategies (P < 0.001; Supplementary 
Fig. 17).” 
Second, to visually explore the trade -off between the epidemiological benefit (infections averted) 
and the level of antibiotic use (doxy -PEP prescriptions), we developed new scatterplots incorporating 
efficient frontiers. An efficient frontier identifies the set of strategies offering the maximum benefit for a 
given level of resource use. We created two such plots: one for cumulative total STIs averted versus 
prescriptions (Figure R20) and another for cumulative syphilis c ases averted versus prescriptions ( Figure 
R21). These analyses reveal that the strategy targeting individuals with a syphilis diagnosis consistently lies 
on the most efficient segment of the frontier in both plots, achieving substantial benefit for a relat ively low 
number of prescriptions. They clearly demonstrate that while broader strategies might avert more infections 
in absolute terms, this often comes at the cost of substantially increased and less efficient antibiotic distribution. 
We also included the Figures R20 and 21 as the Supplementary Figures 18 and 19.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 91 -->

Figure R20. Efficient frontier analysis of cumulative STIs averted versus cumulative doxy -PEP 
prescriptions across different strategies (2025-2034). Each data point represents one of the five d oxy-PEP 
intervention strategies, plotted by the total number of doxy -PEP prescriptions administered (x -axis) and the 
total number of new STIs (gonorrhoea, chlamydia, and syphilis combined) averted (y -axis) over the 10-year 
period (2025-2034), compared to the base case (no doxy-PEP). The solid line represents the efficient frontier, 
connecting strategies that offer the maximum number of STIs averted for a given number of prescriptions (or 
the minimum prescriptions for a given number of STIs averted). Strategies on the frontier are considered most 
efficient in terms of prescriptions per STI averted; the slope between points on the frontier indicates the 
incremental prescriptions required per additional STI averted. 
 
Figure R21. Efficient frontier analysis of cumulative syphilis infections averted versus cumulative 
doxy-PEP prescriptions across different strategies (2025-2034). Each data point represents one of the five 
doxy-PEP intervention strategies, plotted by the total number of doxy-PEP prescriptions administered (x-axis) 
and the total number of new syphilis infections averted (y -axis) over the 10 -year period (2025 -2034), 
compared to the base case (no doxy-PEP). The solid line represents the efficient frontier, connecting strategies 
that offer the maximum number of syphilis infections averted for a given number of prescriptions (or the 
minimum prescriptions for a given number of syphilis infections averted). Strategies on the frontier are 
considered most efficient in terms of prescriptions per syphilis infection averted; the slope between points on

the frontier indicates the incremental prescriptions required per additional syphilis infection averted. 
Finally, to provide the comprehensive joint assessment of strategy performance across multiple 
dimensions, we employed a structured multi -criteria evaluation framework.  This framework evaluates 
our five updated doxy -PEP strategies against four key outcome indicators: averted infections (overall STI 
reduction or syphilis-specific reduction), antimicrobial resistance (the increase in HL TetR gonorrhoea, aiming 
to minimize), and economic efficiency (the Benefit-Cost Ratio, BCR). We then analysed strategy performance 
under eleven distinct policy prioritization frameworks, which consider these indicators singly or i n various 
combinations. The results, visually summarizing the rank of each doxy -PEP strategy under these eleven 
frameworks, are presented in Figure R13 (a response to similar comments from Reviewer 1) . This multi -
criteria ranking, combined with insights from the new scatterplots, allows for a nuanced understanding of 
driving characteristics. For example, it confirms the “PrEP-using attendees ” strategy ranks highest for 
maximizing overall STI reduction but performs less well when minimizing HL TetR is prioritized. Conversely, 
the “one syphilis diagnosis ” strategy demonstrates a strong, balanced performance across frameworks 
weighting efficiency (BCR) and AMR mitigation, consistent with its highly efficient position on the frontiers. 
Its effectiveness thus stems from efficiently targeting high -transmission networks with a relatively modest 
number of prescriptions. This approach facilitates simultaneous outcome interpretation and allows for 
generalizable conclusions beyond reco mmending a single “best” strategy. For your convenience, we have 
appended a copy of the ranking framework figure (Figure R13) below this response. 
 
Figure R13 (a copy for convenience). Doxy-PEP strategy rankings across different policy prioritization 
frameworks. This figure illustrates the relative ranking (from Rank 1 [most favourable] to Rank 5 [least 
favourable]) of five distinct doxy-PEP implementation strategies across eleven policy prioritization 
frameworks. The strategies (defined in the figure legend and detailed in Table 1 of the main manuscript) were 
evaluated based on their performance against four key indicators: (1) maxima l reduction in overall Sexually 
Transmitted Infections (STI Reduc.); (2) maximal reduction in syphilis incidence (Syphilis Reduc.); (3) 
optimal outcomes concerning high-level tetracycline resistance in N. gonorrhoeae (HL TetR; i.e., minimizing 
its increase or maximizing its decrease); and (4) maximal BCR. The x-axis specifies each framework, showing 
rankings based on these indicators considered singly, or in combinations of two or three indicators. 
STIs=sexually transmitted infections . Reduc.=reductions.  HL TetR=high-level tetracycline resistance . 
BCR=benefit-cost ratio. PrEP=pre-exposure prophylaxis. 
We have added the methodology of this ranking frameworks as a separate section to our revised 
manuscript as follows: 
“Evaluation framework of doxy-PEP strategies 
To systematically compare and rank the five doxy -PEP strategies, we used a multi -criteria evaluation

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 93 -->

framework, assessing performance over 10 years (2025 -2034) against four key indicators: two for infection 
reduction—(1) reductions in overall STIs and ( 2) reductions in syphilis infection (highlighted due to its 
prioritization in the Australian consensus statement); one for AMR—(3) increased gonorrhoea infections with 
HL TetR, aiming to minimize this; and one for economic efficiency—(4) the BCR, aiming to maximize this. 
Strategies were ranked under eleven policy prioritization frameworks, considering these four indicators 
singly or in pairwise/three -way combinations (e.g., an infection reduction indicator with HL TetR impact 
and/or BCR). For combined -indicator frameworks, ranks (1=most to 5=least favourable) were based on 
summing individual indicator ranks (assuming equal weighting; lower sum = better performance). These 
rankings (Figure 4) aid decision-making by showing how strategy prioritization varies with policy goals.” 
We also included the ranking figure as Figure 4 in the manuscript, and added related text in our results 
section as follows: 
“Our multi-criteria evaluation framework showed that no single strategy was optimal across all eleven 
policy pri oritizations (Fig. 4). The strategy targeting all PrEP -using attendees ranked highest when 
maximizing overall STI and syphilis reduction was the sole priority. Conversely, the strategy targeting 
individuals with a syphilis diagnosis consistently achieved t he highest rankings in frameworks that 
emphasized minimizing the increase in HL TetR gonorrhoea, maximizing the BCR, and all the combined 
prioritizations frameworks.”
