Comment 21:

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 94 -->

- Lines 80-81. From my reading, those studies don ’t “underestimate the broader populat ion impact…of 
doxy-PEP strategies in reducing multiple STIs, ” because the studies don ’t evaluate multiple STIs. I suggest 
rephrasing to accurately reflect that these studies simply didn’t investigate the impact of doxy-PEP on multiple 
STIs. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for their careful reading and for pointing out the imprecision in our original 
phrasing on Lines 80-81 concerning previous modelling studies. We agree with the reviewer’s assessment that 
stating those studies “underestimated the broader po pulation impact…of Doxy-PEP strategies in reducing 
multiple STIs” was not the most accurate characterization, as their primary scope did not extend to evaluating 
multiple STIs simultaneously, which is a distinctive aspect of our current work. 
We would also like to note that the “Research in context” section, where the sentence in question 
was located, has been removed in our revised manuscript. This decision was made as this section is not a 
required component for articles in Nature Communications. 
Given your comment, we have also taken care to review other sections of the manuscript where our 
study is contextualized against previous research to ensure that the scope and findings of other studies are 
described accurately and appropriately. 
Comment 22: 
- It is unclear to me how many simulations were ultimately run to generate the data in the main analysis. 
In lines 167-170, it is indicated that 100 parameter combinations were used in the main analysis, but the text 
does not mention how often each param eter combination was run and the figures do not report uncertainty 
intervals. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for these questions regarding our simulation procedure and the presentation of 
uncertainty in our results. We provide the following clarifications. 
Regarding the number of simulation runs, our main analyses, as indicated in the manuscript (Lines 167 -
170 of the original draft), are based on 100 distinct parameter combinations, which represent the best -fitting 
parameter sets identified during calibration. For each of these 100 selected best-fitting parameter sets, we 
performed one simulation run.  A fixed universal random number seed (set to 123) was utilized for all 
simulations to ensure that the specific simulation run for any given parameter set is r eproducible. The use of 
100 different parameter sets allows us to capture the impact of parameter uncertainty on our model outcomes. 
The results presented in our manuscript, such as point estimates, are typically the mean values derived 
from the outputs of these 100 simulations (one run per distinct parameter set). The associated 95% Confidence 
Intervals (CIs) are calculated from the variation across these 100 runs, thereby primarily reflecting parameter 
uncertainty. We acknowledge the reviewer’s observation that not all figures in the original submission visually 
displayed these uncertainty intervals. While our main summary tables (e.g., Table 2) have consistently 
included 95% CIs, our initial multi -scenario line graphs did not include shaded CI regions pri marily to 
maintain visual clarity and avoid excessive overlap that could obscure the mean trends of the numerous 
strategies being compared. However, in response to feedback and to enhance the presentation of uncertainty, 
we have made revisions. Key summary tables continue to report means and 95% CIs, and furthermore, newly 
added or revised figures, such as summary bar charts for cumulative outcomes (e.g., revised Figure 1, panels 
G and H) and our new one-way sensitivity analysis summary figures (Supplementary Figures 20–23), now 
incorporate error bars representing these 95% CIs.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 95 -->

We also add this information to the Methods sections as follows: 
“Key model outputs derived from simulations using these 100 parameter sets are reported as mean values 
with 95% confidence intervals (CIs), based on one simulation run per parameter set.” 
Comment 23: 
- Lines 157-159 describe emergence of resistance but leave out the process by which resistance emerges 
with the use of Doxy -PEP. When first reading this, I thought doxy cycline resistance emerged independently 
of doxycycline use and this point was only clarified after reading the supplement. 
Response: 
We thank the Reviewer for highlighting this point of potential confusion in our initial description of 
resistance emergence. Actually, we introduced the doxycycline resistance emerged due to doxy -PEP in 
the Methods of our previous paper, but we agree that the original phrasing could have been clearer. 
Our intention was to introduce the model components in a staged manner within the main manuscript ’s 
Methods section. We first described the general gonorrhoea model , including background AMR 
development, in the “Model structure” subsection. The detailed mechanisms of the doxy -PEP intervention, 
including the specific process by which doxy-PEP use contributes to resistance, were then elaborated in 
the subsequent “Intervention scenarios and simulation” subsection of the Methods. We understand now 
that this structure inadvertently created an initial impression that resistance emerged only independently of 
doxy-PEP use. 
To address this and make the distinction clearer from the outset, we have revised the relevant sentence 
in the “Model structure” subsection of the Methods. The sentence now reads: 
“Ceftriaxone DS could develop during antimicrobial treatment, while a probability of spontaneous HL 
TetR development was included in each cycle to account for potential bystander effects contributing to rising 
resistance levels (independent of the influence of doxy-PEP).” 
By adding this parenthetical clarification, we signal to the reader early on that the mechanism being 
described is separate from the intervention ’s direct effect. The full details on how doxy -PEP use contributes 
to the emergence of HL TetR remain in the later “Intervention scenarios and simulation ” subsection of the 
Methods, as was originally intended. We believe this revision resolves the potential for misunderstanding, and 
we thank you for helping us improve the clarity of our manuscript. 
Comment 24: 
- Please clarify in the main text the rate of treatment failures of infections with decreased ceftriaxone 
susceptibility. In the supplement, it is stated that treatment failure occurs in just under 10% of cases and that 
this value was determined by model calibration. Additionally, is this consistent with empirical data? 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for their query regarding the rate of treatment failure (TF) for N. gonorrhoeae (NG) 
infections with ceftriaxone decreased susceptibility (DS), its clarification in the main text, and its empirical 
consistency. 
To enhance clarity, we have now explicitly stated the calibrated TF rate for NG infections with ceftriaxone 
DS within the “Model calibration” section of our main manuscript’s Methods. The “Model calibration” section 
now includes the sentence (the values were determined through model re-calibration process):  
“From these selected sets, key uncertain parameters were estimated; for example, the treatment failure 
rate for gonorrhoea infections with decreased ceftriaxone susceptibility was calibrated to 7.6% (95% CI:

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 96 -->

6.0%–9.8%).” 
Regarding consistency with empirical data, our calibrated 7.6% treatment failure rate for infections 
with ceftriaxone DS is broadly supported by recent evidence.  We acknowledge the reviewer’s point that 
directly reported failures are rare. However, a recent po oled analysis on MICs associated with ceftriaxone 
treatment failures (PMID: 39673499) reported that observed failures occurred at mean MICs of 0.09–0.14 
mg/L. Since these failure -associated MICs are indeed higher than our model ’s threshold for defining 
ceftriaxone DS (>0.06 mg/L), it supports the premise that infections in this category carry an increased risk of 
failure. Therefore, our calibrated 7.6% TF probability for this specific group of infections represents a 
plausible estimate of this increased risk. 
Furthermore, to account for uncertainty in this parameter, we conducted a one-way sensitivity analysis 
by varying TF probability of 7.6% (95% CI: 6.0%–9.8%) from 0.5 to 1.5 times its base -case value 
(results in Figures R8-R11). This analysis indicated a modest impact on overall STI and syphilis reduction, 
but a more discernible influence on HL TetR gonorrhoea dynamics and cost -effectiveness. Specifically, a 
higher TF rate led to a larger projected increase in HL TetR gonorrhoea ( as persistent infections create more 
opportunities for acquired resistance) and also resulted in slightly more favourable Benefit-Cost Ratios (BCRs) 
(as a higher baseline STI burden increases the economic value of averted infections). Overall, this analysis 
confirms that while the ceftriaxone TF rate is an influential parameter for certain outcomes, our study's 
main conclusions on the comparative ranking and trade -offs of the doxy -PEP strategies are robust to 
its variation. 
We believe this explicit statemen t in the main text, alongside the discussion of empirical data and the 
sensitivity analysis, addresses the reviewer's request for clarification and contextualization. 
Comment 25: 
- Line 207f: “Based on previous clinical trials, we assumed doxy -PEP was prescribed as two doses (400 
mg) per week, totalling 52 doses for the six -month prescription duration. ” I believe doxyPEP was not 
prescribed as two doses a week, but rather for use as needed. The average use was four doses a month in the 
Luetkemeyer et al. trial. Do the authors account for additional use of the remaining doses of Doxy -PEP after 
the 6 months have passed? Do you account for refills of Doxy -PEP if the prescribed pills are used up more 
quickly? 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for their pertinent questions regarding our assumptions for doxy-PEP prescription 
quantity, usage patterns, and the handling of remaining doses or refills. In response, we have revised both 
our manuscript text and our underlying model logic to clarify that prescriptions provide  a sufficient 
supply for event-driven use, and to explicitly allow for the use of leftover doses and for re -prescription 
if pills are exhausted and eligibility continues. The paragraphs below detail these enhancements. 
First, to address the query about the “two doses per week ” assumption, we have clarified in the 
manuscript that this was the basis for calculating the quantity of pills supplied in a standard six-month 
prescription, not a fixed prescription schedule. Our i ntention was to ensure a sufficient supply for 
individuals, while agreeing with the reviewer that actual doxy -PEP use is event -driven (“as needed ”), 
consistent with usage patterns in clinical trials. To make this clear in our manuscript, we have revised th e 
relevant sentences in the Methods section to read: 
“The six -month of doxy -PEP prescription duration was assumed to provide 52 administration events 
(200mg doxycycline each), a quantity calculated based on a potential usage of up to two such events weekly. 
Modelled as event -driven (post-condomless exposure) and adherence -dependent, an individual ’s course of

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 97 -->

doxy-PEP concluded once their prescribed doxycycline was exhausted, with re -prescription possible if 
eligibility criteria were subsequently met.” 
Second, our revised model logic now directly accounts for the use of leftover doses and the need for 
refills, as you astutely questioned. As reflected in the updated manuscript text quoted above, the phrase “an 
individual’s course of doxy -PEP concluded once their prescribed doxycycline was exhausted ” signifies that 
individuals use their initial supply until it is depleted, regardless of whether this occurs before or after a 
nominal 6-month window. Furthermore, the clause “with re-prescription possible if eligibi lity criteria were 
subsequently met” explicitly incorporates the possibility of refills if an individual exhausts their supply and 
continues to be eligible. 
These updates provide a more realistic simulation of real -world doxy -PEP usage patterns.  This 
revised logic improves the robustness of our simulation and directly addresses all aspects of your valuable 
query. We believe the manuscript is much clearer as a result. 
Comment 26: 
- How is Doxy-PEP use distributed among age groups? 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this question regarding the age distribution of individuals who use doxy -PEP 
under the different intervention strategies. In response, we conducted a new analysis (presented in Figure 
R22) which shows that doxy-PEP users tend to be from younger age groups across most strategies, with 
a notable exception for the strategy targeting all HIV-positive attendees. 
To generate these results, we tracked the age group of individuals at the time they initiated doxy -PEP 
during the simulated interven tion period (2025 -2034), averaging the results across our 100 best -fitting 
parameter sets. This analysis revealed that the age distribution of users varies by eligibility criteria. The general 
tendency for users to be from younger age groups (e.g., 25 -44 y ears) is particularly evident in strategies 
targeting individuals based on their STI diagnostic history or their use of PrEP. 
The exception to this pattern is the strategy targeting all “HIV positive attendees, ” under which 
fewer individuals from the youngest age groups (e.g., 15-24 years) are projected to initiate doxy-PEP. A 
reason for this difference is our model assumption that individuals entering the sexually active population (at 
age 15) are HIV-negative, resulting in a smaller pool of very young HIV-positive individuals eligible for this 
specific strategy. This analysis provides valuable insight into which age demographics are primarily reached 
by each of the proposed doxy-PEP implementation strategies.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 98 -->

Figure R22. Age distribution of individuals initiating doxy -PEP under different eligibility strategies 
(2025-2034). 
Comment 27: 
- Why did you decide to implement random mixing of casual partners (see supplement, algorithm 2)? 
Does assortative mixing (e.g., by preferred number of casual partnerships)  change the STI transmission 
patterns? 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for their question regarding the mixing patterns for casual partnerships in our 
model. While the final selection of a casual partner from the available pool is random, our model is 
designed with behavioural dynamics that result in an emergent form of assortative mixing by sexual 
activity level (i.e., by Annual Number of Casual Partners, or ANCP).  This approach is consistent with 
other individual -based models of STI transmission and allow s for realistic network structures to form 
organically. 
This assortativity is driven by the dynamic probability that an individual actively seeks a new casual

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 99 -->

partner. As detailed in the Supplementary Material, this probability is based on the difference b etween an 
individual’s target ANCP and the number of partners they have recently acquired. Individuals with a high 
target ANCP will seek new partners more frequently, meaning the pool of individuals actively seeking casual 
partners at any given time is dis proportionately composed of those with higher ANCPs. Therefore, when 
random pairing occurs from this active pool, individuals with high ANCPs have a much greater likelihood of 
partnering with other high-ANCP individuals. 
Consequently, this realistic, activity-based assortative mixing directly influences STI transmission 
patterns in our model, addressing your second question.  This emergent property allows infections to 
potentially concentrate and transmit more efficiently within these higher-activity networks. Because our model 
already incorporates this important degree of assortativity by design, we believe these concerns hav e been 
addressed. 
Comment 28: 
- Since modeling by sex act: Did you account for changes in sexual behavior if symptomatically infected 
with an STI? 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this question. In our current model, individuals who develop symptomatic 
infections are assumed to seek clinical services and receive treatment within a relatively short period 
(uniformly 1-2 weeks). Thus, this model does not currently explicitly incorporate a change in sexual 
activity levels during this brief pre-treatment symptomatic phase. 
We believe the impact of this simplification on overall long -term epidemic dynamics and intervention 
effects is likely limited. This is primarily because the symptomatic pre-treatment window is short, and a 
substantial proportion of overall STI transmission is driven by asymptomatic infections which persist 
for much longer periods. While explicitly modelling symptom-driven behavioural changes could be a future 
refinement, we do not expect this current assumption to substantially alter the ma in conclusions of our study 
regarding the comparative impact of the doxy-PEP strategies. 
Comment 29: 
- Is the condom use proportion applied to both regular and casual partnerships? Or is condom use 
more/less likely in one of the two partnership types? 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this question regarding our condom use assumptions. Yes, our model explicitly 
accounts for differential condom use, with distinct proportions applied based on both the partnership 
type and the characteristics of the individuals involved. 
Specifically, the proportions of individuals who “always use condoms” versus “sometimes use condoms” 
are stratified in two key ways: 
1. By Partnership Type: We apply different condom use distributions for regular partnerships versus 
casual partnerships. 
2. By GBMSM Subgroup: Within each partnership type, these distributions are further stratified for our 
three key populations: HIV -positive individuals, HIV -negative PrEP -using individuals, and HIV -
negative PrEP non-using individuals. 
These detailed, stratified condom use parameters, which are informed by empirical data, are presented in 
our Supplementary Figure 8. This approach allows the model to reflect the observed heterogeneity in

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 100 -->

condom use practices across different relationship contexts and GBMSM subgroups. 
Comment 30: 
- How do you handle co-infections? Can patients be co-infected with multiple STIs? Would testing for a 
symptomatic chlamydia infection also detect an asymptomatic gonorrhea infection? 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for these questions regarding the handling of co-infections and testing for multiple 
STIs within our model. 
In our model, the transmission dynamics for gonorrhoea, chlamydia, and syphilis are treated as 
independent processes. This means that an individual can indeed be co -infected with multiple STIs 
simultaneously, and the acquisition or progression of one STI does not directly influence the natural history 
of another within the current model structure. This allows for the simulation of concurrent infections, 
reflecting real-world scenarios. 
Regarding testing practices, when an individual in our model seeks clinical services —whether this is 
driven by symptoms of a particular STI or for routine asymptomatic screening —we assume that 
comprehensive testing for relevant STIs (gonorrhoea, chlamydia, and syphilis) occurs. Consequently, if 
an individual presents, for example, due to symptomatic chlamydia, the model assumes they would also be 
tested for other STIs like gonorrhoea and syphilis during that same clinical encounter. Any existing infections 
detected, whether symptomatic or asymptomatic, are then managed and treated according to the model's STI-
specific treatment pathways. This approach of simultaneous testing and subsequent treatment of 
identified infections du ring a clinical visit is intended to align with standard clinical practice for STI 
management in Australia. 
Comment 31: 
- My understanding is that any partner has at most one regular and one casual partner at any time point. 
Could you please clarify whether your model allows for greater numbers of simultaneous partnerships? 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for their question seeking clarification on the number of simultaneous partnerships 
allowed in our model. 
The reviewer’s understanding is correct: in our current model, individuals are limited to having at most 
one regular partner and, concurrently, at most one casual partner at any given time point. This means an 
individual can have a maximum of two ongoing partnerships simultaneously, one of each type. 
This limitation is implemented through the partnership formation algorithms described in our 
Supplementary Material (Algorithms 1-3). Specifically, individuals are maintained on separate availability 
lists for seeking regular partners (Ra list) and casual partners (Ca list). Once an individual forms a partnership 
of a specific type (e.g., a regular partnership), they are removed from th e availability list for that type of 
partnership (e.g., Ra list) for the duration of that ongoing relationship and are thus not available to form another 
concurrent partnership of the same type. 
We acknowledge that in reality, some individuals may engage in multiple concurrent regular or multiple 
concurrent casual partnerships. The assumption in our model to limit concurrency to one of each type is a 
simplification made to balance model complexity with the available data for parameterizing more intricate 
partnership dynamics. This approach is also consistent with methodologies used in some previous 
individual-based models of STI transmission (e.g., as seen in studies such as PMID: 34735428 and PMID: 
34894134).

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 101 -->

Exploring the impact of more complex multi -partnering scenarios, such as allowing for multiple 
concurrent casual or regular partners beyond this one -plus-one structure, could indeed be a valuable area for 
future research and model refinement. 
Comment 32: 
- Supplement, page 14 states: “As our study exclu ded the “no anal intercourse” scenario, we calculated 
the average proportions for the remaining choices and adjusted them to sum to 100% for each partnership 
type”. Why did you exclude non-anal sex acts? Does this alter the assumptions you make, for example, about 
the proportion of infections that are symptomatic? As the model does not explicitly account for anatomic sites 
of infection, I don’t understand why you would need to restrict sexual behavior by site. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for their careful reading and for highlighting the ambiguity in our original 
Supplementary Material. To be clear, our model does not exclude non -anal sex acts or restrict sexual 
behaviour by site; rather, it uses a general, non -site-specific definition of sexual acts for transmission.  
The confusion stemmed from our previous, misleading description of how we process survey data on condom 
use. We have now extensively revised Supplementary Material Section 1.3.4 (“Condom uses in sexual acts”) 
to clarify our current methodology. 
Our methodology relies on recent (2020–2024) Gay Community Periodic Surveys (GCPS) data, 
which uses clearer terminology and provides essential stratification not available in earlier surveys.  
Specifically, GCPS data  prior to 2020 sometimes used terms like “no anal intercourse ” for the category 
representing individuals not recently sexually active, which we acknowledge was confusing. The more recent 
2020–2024 data uses clearer terms (such as “No sex”) for this category and, crucially, provides the necessary 
condom use data for sexually active participants already stratified by HIV and PrEP status. 
To parameterize our model’s two condom use preferences (“always” vs. “sometimes”), we process 
the three main GCPS response categories from the 2020-2024 data as follows. Individuals from both the 
GCPS categories of “no recent sexual activity ” (i.e., “No sex”) and “always using a condom ” are grouped 
together and classified under our model’s “always use condoms” preference. The GCPS category “sometimes 
not using a condom ” directly informs our model ’s corresponding preference. The resulting derived average 
proportions are then applied as per the s tratification available in the 2020 -2024 GCPS data and detailed in 
Supplementary Figure 8. 
This clarification shows that our method for deriving condom use proportions does not restrict sexual 
behaviour by site or affect other unrelated model assumptions, such as the general proportion of infections 
that are symptomatic. We trust this detailed explanation resolves the reviewer ’s concerns.  This updated 
methodology, now clearly detailed in the supplement, resolves the initial ambiguity and confirms our 
assumptions do not alter other aspects of the model.  
“1.3.4 Condom uses in sexual acts 
In our model, sexual acts relevant for STI transmission are considered in a general manner, without 
explicit specification of anatomical site. To parameterize representative condom use distribution during such 
acts for Australian GBMSM, stratified by HIV and PrEP status, we primarily utilized data from the GCPS 
Melbourne reports. We focused on the 2020–2024 period as these more recent reports provided condom use 
data with the necessary stratification by HIV and PrEP status, which was not consistently available in earlier 
GCPS waves. The fact that this timeframe is also centrally located within our overall simulation period (2012–
2034) may incidentally help mitigate the impact of our model’s assumption that these condom use distributions

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 102 -->

remain constant over time. The GCPS data collected from participants reporting on their regular or casual 
partnerships categorized condom use within these specific partnership types into three ma in responses: (1) 
“reporting no recent sexual activity”, (2) “always using a condom during sexual acts”, and (3) “sometimes not 
using a condom during sexual acts ”. For our model, which categorizes individual preferences into either 
“always use condoms” or “sometimes use condoms,” we processed this survey data by grouping individuals 
from both the GCPS categories of “no recent sexual activity” and “always using a condom” into our model’s 
“always use condoms” classification. The proportions for “sometimes not using a condom” directly informed 
our model ’s corresponding category. These derived average proportions for “always use condoms ” and 
“sometimes use condoms” from the 2020-2024 GCPS data, further stratified by HIV -positive, HIV-negative 
PrEP-using, and HIV-negative PrEP non-using subgroups for both regular and casual partnerships, are detailed 
in Supplementary Figure 8. 
In this study, we assume that each individual has distinct and constant preferences for condom use during 
sexual act for regular partnerships and casual partnerships, respectively. Specifically, each individual 
maintains one preference for regular partnerships and a potentially different preference for casual partnerships. 
We categorize these preferences into two types: (1) Always use condoms, applying the protection of condom 
in 100% of sexual acts, and (2) Sometimes use condoms, applying the protection of condom in 50% of sexual 
acts as a midpoint value in the absence of more granular behavioural data. 
Given that each partnership involves two individuals, the combinations of their condom use preferences 
lead to three possible scenarios: (1) always-always, where both partners always use a condom, (2) sometimes-
sometimes, where both partners sometimes use a condom, and (3) always -sometimes, where one partner 
always uses a condom and the other sometimes does. For operational simplicity, the “always use condom ” 
feature is assigned only to partnerships in the “always-always” combination, while the “sometimes use 
condom” feature is applied  to partnerships in the “sometimes-sometimes” and “always-sometimes” 
combinations. To simulate condom use, we applied 90% of the protection efficacy once the condom was used 
by the pair of individuals in one sexual act. In detail, for the partnerships feat ured as “always use condoms”, 
we applied the protection efficacy of condom in 100% of sexual acts, and for those featured as “sometimes 
use condoms”, we applied the protection of condom in 50% of sexual acts. 
 
Figure 8. Summary of the stratified distribution of condom use based on data from GCPS 2020-2024.” 
Comment 33: 
- Impacts on incident STIs: The strategies are compared by the number of each STI averted under each 
strategy (lines 233ff and Figure 1). Unsurprisingly, the results indicate that strategies  with wider eligibility 
criteria lead to greater absolute reductions in STIs. In table 2, the authors introduce a new measure of STI

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 103 -->

reduction that is normalized for the number of Doxy-PEP prescriptions (new infections averted per doxy-PEP 
prescription). This is arguably the more interesting outcome, as it enables a fairer comparison of strategies, 
and I would recommend mentioning these results in the epidemiological impacts section, not only in the cost-
effectiveness analysis. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this excellent suggestion. We completely agree that the “new infections averted 
per doxy-PEP prescription” metric is a crucial outcome for a fair comparison of the strategies and that it should 
be highlighted within the main epidemiological results. 
In our revised manuscript, we have now prominently featured this efficiency metric in the 
“Epidemiological impact and doxy -PEP utilization” subsection of the Results.  Specifically, the number 
of infections averted per prescription is now visually presented for each strategy in Panel H of our updated 
Figure 1 and is also included in our main economic summary, Table 2. Furthermore, the main text describing 
the findings from Figure 1 now explicitly discusses this efficiency metric, highlighting key insights such as 
the strategy targeting individuals with a syphilis diagnosis demonstrating the highest number of infections 
averted per prescription, even while other broader strategies averted more infections in absolute terms. 
We believe this revised presentation better integrates this important efficiency measure into the broader 
discussion of epidemiological outcomes, as you rightly recommended.  The new Figure 1 and related text in 
the manuscript have been presented as follows: 
“Epidemiological impact and doxy-PEP utilization 
We illustrate the increasing STI incidence rates in the base case and the reductions observed after 
implementing doxy-PEP with various strategies (Fig. 1A-D). In the context of the WHO 2030 goals for STI 
reduction from 2020 levels (red dashed lines in Fig. 1A-C), modelling projects that by 2030, the five evaluated 
doxy-PEP strategies could reduce chlamydia incidence by 14.6 -83.8% and syphilis incidence by  1.7-95.6%, 
compared with their respective 2020 levels. Notably, strategies targeting individuals with two STI diagnoses 
in the past 12 months or all PrEP-using GBMSM were projected to achieve the WHO’s chlamydia reduction 
target, with the latter also meet ing the syphilis reduction target by 2030. However, while doxy -PEP initially 
reduced gonorrhoea incidence, projected 2030 levels under all strategies surpassed the 2020 baseline (black 
dashed line in Fig. 1A), typically rebounding within two to three years  of implementation due to increasing 
doxycycline resistance; nevertheless, incidence remained below the projected base-case (no doxy-PEP) level. 
Annual doxy-PEP prescription volumes and population coverage varied substantially across strategies 
(Fig. 1E-F). Most scenarios showed a peak in prescriptions in the first year of implementation before 
stabilizing. This resulted in projected average annual coverage levels among eligible individuals ranging from 
a low of approximately 3.9% (95% CI: 3.1-4.6%) for the strategy targeting individuals with syphilis diagnosis 
to a high of 26.2% (95% CI: 26.1-26.3%) for the strategy targeting all PrEP-using attendees. 
Cumulatively ( Fig. 1G ), in the base case, we projected 51,798 (47,449–56,147) total incident STIs 
between 2025 and 2034, including 19,669 (95% CI: 16,809–22,529) gonorrhoea infections, 23,054 (19,863–
26,245) chlamydia infections, and 9,075 (7,307–10,842) syphilis infections. Across the five intervention 
strategies, the cumulative number of STIs averted ranged from 8,564 (95% CI: 7,686–9,442) for the strategy 
targeting all HIV-positive attendees to 28,096 (95% CI: 25,259–30,933) for the strategy targeting all PrEP -
using attendees, corresponding to overall STI reductions of 16.5% to 54.2% ( Fig. 1G and H). Specifically, 
the strategy targeting individuals with a syphilis diagnosis averted 11,589 (95% CI: 9,360–13,818) infections,

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 104 -->

a reduction of 22.4%. The epidemiological impacts required a wide range of intervention intensity, from a 
cumulative 1,623 (1315 -1931) prescriptions for the syphilis diagnosis strategy to 11,429 (11,403-11,455) 
prescriptions for the PrEP -using attendees strategy. Consequently, intervention efficiency, measured as 
infections averted (IA) per prescription, was highest for the syphilis diagnosis strategy (7.1 IA per prescription; 
95% CI: 7.0–7.2) and lowest for the PrEP-using attendees strategy (2.5 IA per prescription; 95% CI: 2.4–2.5).” 
 
Figure 1. Projected impact of various doxy -PEP strategies on STI incidence, doxy -PEP 
prescriptions, covera ge, and intervention efficiency among GBMSM (2024 -2034). Panels (A) to (D) 
illustrate the mean annual incidence rates (per 100 person -years) of gonorrhoea, chlamydia, syphilis, and all 
three STIs combined, respectively, across different doxy -PEP scenarios. Panel (E) shows the mean annual 
doxy-PEP prescriptions (in thousands). Panel (F) depicts the mean annual doxy -PEP coverage percentage 
among eligible individuals. Panel (G) presents the mean cumulative incident STIs (syphilis, chlamydia,

2020 incidences to meet WHO ’s reduction targets (90% for gonorrhoea, 50% for chlamydia, and 90% for 
syphilis). Doxy-PEP=doxycycline post-exposure prophylaxis. GBMSM=gay, bisexual, and other me n who

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 105 -->

have sex with men. IA=infections averted. PrEP=pre-exposure prophylaxis. PY=person-years. STIs=sexually 
transmitted infections. WHO=World Health Organization. 
Comment 34: 
- The base scenario projects 27,801 STIs among 10k individuals in 10 years. i.e ., roughly 0.3 cases per 
person-year. How does that compare to observed/estimated incidence in Australia? 
- How are the projected infections distributed among the population and how does this distribution 
compare to real-world data? This level of validation is important since the study compares strategies that target 
specific groups. Especially since the strategy with widest Doxy -PEP use had the highest impact. What if 
infections are more concentrated in the real-world population than in the simulation? 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for these important questions on model validation. In our revised model, we now 
directly calibrate to observed annual Australian STI incidence rates to ensure our projections align with 
real-world data, and we have explored the impact of infection concentration through sensitivity analyses. 
The projection you noted (approx. 0.3 cases per person -year) was from a previous model version; the 
paragraphs below detail our updated approach. 
To address your first point on comparing our model’s incidence to Australian data, our primary 
calibration target is now the annual STI incidence from national surveillance (ACCESS project, 2012 -
2022). Specifically, we calibrate the model against annual incidence rates for gonorrhoea, chlamydia, and 
syphilis for both the overall population and stratified by three key GBMSM subgroups: HIV -positive, 
HIV-negative PrEP-using, and HIV-negative PrEP non-using individuals. This ensures that the generation 
of new infections within our simulated population aligns with observed epidemiological trends. The successful 
alignment of our model-simulated annual incidence rates with these Australian target data is presented in the 
new Figure R15. 
To address your second point on the distribution of infections, we conducted a sensitivity analysis 
to assess the impact of risk concentration. As a proxy for infections being more or less concentrated in high-
risk groups, we varied the initial STI prevalence within our HIV-positive and PrEP-user subgroups. As detailed 
in our response to your Comment 15 , this analysis showed a generally modest im pact on our four key 
intervention outcomes, suggesting our primary conclusions are reasonably robust to the level of initial 
infection concentration. (The detailed results can be seen in the tornado plots in Figures R8-R11 of our earlier 
response). 
We believe these comprehensive calibrations and sensitivity analyses ensure our model provides a robust 
basis for evaluating doxy -PEP strategies. For your convenience, we have appended a copy of our new 
calibration results (Figure R15) below this response.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 106 -->

Figure R15 (a copy for convenience). Outcomes of the model validation. The black points represent the 
real data points. The lines and shaded areas represent the mean and 95% CI of the model outputs. 
Comment 35: 
- Lines 233-251. I would recommend presenting results in order of effectiveness of strategies or including 
a summary table. It was a bit confusing having to rank the numbers while reading the text. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for their feedback on improving the clarity of our results presentation  when 
comparing doxy-PEP strategies. 
We thank the reviewer for their feedback on improving the clarity of our results presentation when 
comparing the doxy -PEP strategies. To address your suggestion for a clearer, multi -dimensional 
comparison, we have adopted a two-part approach: we have introduced a comprehensive multi-criteria 
ranking results Figure R13  (Figure 4 in the revised manuscript) to visually summarize their 
performance across different policy goals , and  consistently use d clear descriptive na mes for each 
strategy in the text. 
The Figure R13, which we also introduced in detail in our response to your Comment 19, directly 
serves the comparative purpose you recommended.  As this figure illustrates, the ranking of strategies is 
highly dependent on the specific public health goal being prioritized. This figure resolves addresses the need 
for a clear overview of strategy performance under various criteria  by visually ranking our five strategies 
across eleven distinct policy prioritization frameworks. It allows readers to easily see which strategy performs 
best according to different priorities (e.g., maximizing infection reduction versus balancing cost-effectiveness 
and AMR mitigation). For your convenience, we have appended a copy of Figure R13 below this response. 
To further enhance readability in the main text, we have also revised the text to consistently use descriptive 
names for each strategy (e.g., “the strategy targeting individuals with a syphilis diagnosis ”) rather than 
“Scenario X” numbering. We believe this combination—the new ranking figure for a high-level comparison 
and clearer descriptive names in the text —effectively resolves the previous difficulty in tracking and 
comparing strategy performance.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 107 -->

Figure R13 (a copy for convenience). Doxy-PEP strategy rankings across different policy prioritization 
frameworks. This figure illustrates the relative ranking (from Rank 1 [most favourable] to Rank 5 [least 
favourable]) of five distinct doxy-PEP implementation strategies across eleven policy  prioritization 
frameworks. The strategies (defined in the figure legend and detailed in Table 1 of the main manuscript) were 
evaluated based on their performance against four key indicators: (1) maximal reduction in overall Sexually 
Transmitted Infections  (STI Reduc.); (2) maximal reduction in syphilis incidence (Syphilis Reduc.); (3) 
optimal outcomes concerning high-level tetracycline resistance in N. gonorrhoeae (HL TetR; i.e., minimizing 
its increase or maximizing its decrease); and (4) maximal BCR. The x-axis specifies each framework, showing 
rankings based on these indicators considered singly, or in combinations of two or three indicators. 
STIs=sexually transmitted infections . Reduc.=reductions.  HL TetR=high -level tetracycline resistance . 
BCR=benefit-cost ratio. PrEP=pre-exposure prophylaxis. 
Comment 36: 
- Lines 256-258 (and also Line 321): I enjoyed seeing the performance of the strategies in the context of 
WHO targets, though these targets don ’t need to be achieved by a single intervention. Instead o f saying that 
“only the strategy prescribing doxy -PEP to all HIV+ or PrEP -using GBMSM (scenario 5) could achieve the 
WHO targets”, the authors could mention the percentage of the targeted reductions that can be achieved by 
Doxy-PEP alone. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for their excellent suggestion regarding how we frame the performance of doxy -
PEP strategies in the context of WHO targets. We agree that it is more informative to specify the extent of the 
targeted reductions that doxy-PEP alone might achieve, rather than only focusing on whether a single strategy 
meets the overall targets. We have revised the relevant paragraph in our manuscript to reflect this. The 
revised manuscript text now reads: 
“We illustrate the increasing STI incidence rates in the base case and the reductions observed after 
implementing doxy-PEP with various strategies (Fig. 1A-D). In the context of the WHO 2030 goals for STI 
reduction from 2020 levels (red dashed lines in Fig. 1A-C), modelling projects that by 2030, the five evaluated 
doxy-PEP strategies could reduce chlamydia incidence by 14.6 -83.8% and syphilis incidence by 1.7 -95.6%, 
compared with their respective 2020 levels. Notably, strategies targeting individuals with two STI diagnoses 
in the past 12 months or all PrEP-using GBMSM were projected to achieve the WHO’s chlamydia reduction 
target, with the latter also meeting the syphilis reduction target by 2030. However, while doxy -PEP initially 
reduced gonorrhoea incidence, projected 2030 levels under all strategies surpassed the 2020 baseline (black 
dashed line in Fig. 1A), typically rebounding within two to three years of implementation due to increasing

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 108 -->

doxycycline resistance; nevertheless, incidence remained below the projected base-case (no doxy-PEP) level.” 
We believe this revised presentation more accurately and clearly conveys the potential contribution and 
limitations of doxy-PEP alone in the context of the WHO targets, as suggested by the reviewer, and reflects 
our latest model findings. 
Comment 37: 
- In Figure 2, the ordering of strategies is inverted for panels A and B versus panels C and D. Could the 
authors elaborate on the tradeoff between selecting for doxycycline-sensitive or doxycycline-resistant strains? 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for their insightful comments regarding Figure 2, which raise two important points: 
one concerning its visual presentation and the other about the substantive trade -off between selecting for 
doxycycline-sensitive versus resistant strains of N. gonorrhoeae. To summarize our response, the apparent 
visual symmetry is an artifact of using adaptive y -axis scales for clarity, and regarding the trade -off, 
our model shows that while doxy-PEP does select for resistance, the absolute number of averted sensitive 
infections consistently outweighs the increase in resistant infections, resulting in a net benefit for overall 
gonorrhoea cases averted. 
Regarding the visual presentation, we opted for adaptive y -axis scales for each panel (A -D) to ensure 
optimal clarity for each specific susceptibility category.  This approach was chosen particularly for those 
categories where the absolute incidence rates were numerically smaller, as a unified scale might have obscured 
their trends. We acknowledge that this independent scaling could inadvertently create a visual impression that 
the reduction in doxycycline -sensitive strains is symmetrically mirrored by an increase in resistant strains. 
However, as explained below, this visual effect does not represent a dire ct 1:1 transfer of the infection 
burden. 
To quantitatively illustrate this trade -off, our results show that the number of averted sensitive 
infections is greater than the increase in resistant infections.  For example, comparing the strategy that 
targets all “PrEP-using attendees” to the base case over the 10 -year intervention period, our model projects 
that doxy-PEP would avert approximately 6,230 (95% CI: 5,847–6,651) new infections with doxycycline -
sensitive gonorrhoea. Simultaneously, the same strategy i s projected to result in a cumulative increase of 
approximately 3,731 (95% CI: 3,410–4,028) new infections with doxycycline -resistant gonorrhoea. This 
demonstrates that the reduction outweighs the increase, underpinning the overall decrease in total gonorrhoea 
cases seen in most strategies. 
The central implication of this trade -off is that while effective doxy -PEP use inherently exerts 
selection pressure, our model indicates it can still provide a net public health benefit for overall 
gonorrhoea control. A comprehensive assessment requires looking at both the absolute number of resistant 
infections (Figure 2) and their rising proportion among all new cases (our new Figure 3). The increase in the 
proportion of resistant strains is a critical consideration for  the long -term sustainability of doxy -PEP and 
underscores the necessity for targeted implementation and robust antimicrobial resistance surveillance. 
For the review’s convenience, the Figures 2 and 3 were attached below the response:

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 109 -->

“Figure 2. Impact of  doxy-PEP on incident gonorrhoea infections across different antibiotic 
susceptibility statuses among GBMSM. Panels (A)-(D) show mean annual gonorrhoea incidence rates (per 
100 person-years) under various scenarios, by antibiotic susceptibility status: (A) “Neither” (exhibiting neither 
ceftriaxone DS nor HL TetR); (B) “Ceftriaxone DS only”; (C) “HL TetR only”; and (D) “Both” (concurrent 
ceftriaxone DS and HL TetR). Panel (E) projects 10-year outcomes (2025-2034): the left y-axis presents mean 
cumulative total gonorrhoea infections (thousands), by these four susceptibility categories. The right y -axis 
displays the mean cumulative increase in gonorrhoea infections exhibiting HL TetR (sum of “HL TetR only” 
and “Both” categories, in thousands) compared to the base case, with 95% CI error bars. All results shown are 
means (with or without 95% CI) calculated from simulations based on 100 parameter sets. GBMSM= gay, 
bisexual, and other men who have sex with men. STIs=sexually transmitted infections. Doxy -
PEP=doxycycline post-exposure prophylaxis. PrEP=pre-exposure prophylaxis. DS=decreased susceptibility. 
HL TetR=high-level tetracycline resistance (used as a proxy for doxycycline resistance).”

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 110 -->

“Figure 3. Annual proportions of incident gonorrhoea infections with different antibiotic susceptibility 
statuses across various scenarios, 2024 to 2034.  Each panel (A -F) corresponds to a specific scenario, 
illustrating the changing annual proportions of four N. gonorrhoeae antibiotic susceptibility statuses among 
incident infections over the period shown. The stacked areas represent infections characterized by: neither 
ceftriaxone DS nor HL TetR; ceftriaxone DS only; HL TetR only; and both ceftriaxone DS and HL TetR. 
Percentage labels on the left of each panel denote these respective proportions for 2024 (the year immediately 
preceding the 10 -year intervention period), while labels on the right denote proportions for 2034 (the final 
year of the 10 -year intervention period). All results shown are means calculated from simulat ions based on 
100 parameter sets.DS=decreased susceptibility; HL TetR=high-level tetracycline resistance (used as a proxy 
for doxycycline resistance); Doxy -PEP=doxycycline post-exposure prophylaxis; STIs=sexually transmitted 
infections; PrEP=pre-exposure prophylaxis.” 
Comment 38: 
- Antibiotic use results: Lines 277ff: “In all strategies, the annual doxy-PEP prescriptions and doxycycline 
pills used by participants after condomless sexual acts increased rapidly in the first one to two years, then 
decreased slightly and stabilised.” Is this driven by the fact that STI diagnoses decline with the use of Doxy -
PEP and therefore fewer individuals are prescribed Doxy -PEP at the point of diagnosis? Would we expect 
patients to want to continue using Doxy -PEP if it is working for them? Could the author elaborate on which 
assumptions are driving the reduction in prescriptions and how those compare with empirically observed 
uptake patterns of Doxy-PEP? 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for these pertinent questions on our projected prescription dynamics. In summary, 
we can confirm the following: (1) The initial drop in prescriptions is primarily driven by the saturation 
of the ‘doxy-PEP naïve’ population, although declining STI diagnoses is a contributing factor for some 
strategies. (2) Our revised model explicitly allows for patients to continue doxy -PEP through re -
prescription if they continue to meet eligibility criteria. (3) A direct comparison with long -term, real-
world prescription trends is challenging as such empirical data are still emerging. The paragraphs below

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 111 -->

provide a more detailed explanation of these points. 
The initial peak-and-drop pattern in annual prescriptions is a general dynamic driven by the large-
scale initiation of doxy -PEP among a previously ‘doxy-PEP naïve ’ population. At the start of 
implementation, the entire eligible population is available to start the intervention, leading to a wave of first -
time prescriptions. In subsequent years, with a portion of the eligible population already on an active course 
of doxy-PEP, the pool of new initiators is smaller, causing the number of annual prescriptions to drop from its 
initial peak before it either stabilizes or follows other long-term trends. For strategies where eligibility is based 
on recent STI diagnoses, this decrease is compounded by the success of doxy -PEP in reducing the incidence 
of the STIs that trigger eligibility. 
To address your question about continued use for patients who find it effective, our revised model 
logic now explicitly allows for re-prescription. We have updated our manuscript’s Methods section to reflect 
this more realistic usage pattern, which addresses both the use of leftover doses beyond a nominal period and 
the need for refills. The text now clarifies that doxy-PEP use is event-driven and continues until the prescribed 
supply is exhausted, at which point re-prescription is possible. The specific sentence reads: 
“Modelled as event-driven (post-condomless exposure) and adherence-dependent, an individual’s course 
of doxy-PEP concluded once their prescribed doxycycline was exhausted, with re -prescription possible if 
eligibility criteria were subsequently met.” 
Regarding a comparison with empirically observed uptake patterns, a direct validation of long -
term prescription trends is currently limited by a lack of available data. While our model’s assumptions 
for initial uptake are informed by published acceptability data, long-term observational studies tracking annual 
prescription volumes for specific doxy -PEP programs are still e merging. Therefore, the dynamics projected 
by our model are a logical consequence of our evidence-based assumptions about initial uptake, re-prescription 
eligibility, population changes, and the impact of the intervention itself on diagnosis-linked eligibility criteria. 
Comment 39: 
- Sensitivity analyses (lines 305ff and Figures S12 -S43): The sensitivity analyses are a reproduction of 
the main text figures for variations in some parameters. Comparing the sensitivity analysis figures requires a 
lot of effort from the reader. The goal of these sensitivity analyses, as I understand it, is to convey to readers 
how much each outcome changes with variation in key parameters, and how much different strategies are 
impacted by that variation. This might be better ach ieved through a summary of the one -way sensitivity 
analyses, for example in a table or a heatmap of how a single outcome measure (e.g., cumulative infections) 
changes for each strategy with variation in the parameters of the sensitivity analysis (e.g., shorter prescription). 
- Why was sensitivity analysis of benefit -cost analysis restricted to a single strategy? See: “Focusing on 
the strategy with the highest BCR under initial parameter settings —providing doxy-PEP to HIV-positive or 
PrEP-using individuals diagnosed with syphilis (scenario 6)—we conducted a one-way sensitivity analysis on 
the BCRs (Figure S44).” (lines 310-312) 
Response: 
We thank the Reviewer for their constructive feedback regarding the presentation and scope of our 
sensitivity analyses. In response to your valuable suggestions, we have completely revised our sensitivity 
analysis approach. We now use a series of tornado plots to summarize the one-way sensitivity analyses 
for our four key outcomes, and have expanded the benefit-cost ratio (BCR) sensitivity analysis to cover 
all five of our intervention strategies. 
Our new presentation format uses tornado plots to clearly and efficiently summarize the results of 
extensive one-way sensitivity analyses (Figures R8 –R11, also the Supplementary Figures 20-23). This

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 112 -->

directly addresses your concern about the previous, cumbersome figures. These new figures visually 
summarize the impact of varying numerous individual parameters —including those related to doxy -PEP 
implementation, STI natural history, sexual behaviour, and economics —on four key outcome metrics: (i) 
overall STI reduction, (ii) syphilis reduction, (iii) change in HL TetR gonorrhoea incidence, and (iv) BCRs. 
This format allows for a clear and direct comparison of how sensitive each strateg y’s performance is to 
changes in different underlying parameters. 
Furthermore, this revised approach resolves the previous limitation of our benefit-cost analysis, as 
we now present the one -way sensitivity analysis for BCRs across all five doxy -PEP strategies. This 
directly addresses your second point about the previously restricted scope. Figure R11 specifically presents 
these comprehensive BCR sensitivity analyses, allowing for a systematic assessment of how parameter 
uncertainty affects the cost-effectiveness of every evaluated strategy, not just one. 
We believe this new presentation offers a much clearer and more systematic assessment of the impact of 
parameter uncertainty on the projected outcomes and cost -effectiveness of each strategy. For your  
convenience, we have appended copies of the new sensitivity analysis figures (Figures R8–R11) and the 
detailed description of the sensitivity analysis methodology from our revised Supplementary Material 
below this response. 
“5.2 One-way sensitivity analyses 
To assess the robustness of our model’s projections and to identify key parameters influencing the 
epidemiological and economic outcomes of the evaluated doxy-PEP strategies, we conducted extensive one-
way sensitivity analyses. In these analyses, individual model parameters were varied one at a time across 
plausible ranges, while all other parameters were held at their base-case values (as detailed in 
Supplementary Table 2, unless otherwise specified). The impact of these variations was assessed on four 
primary outcomes: the cumulative reduction in overall new STIs, the cumulative reduction in new syphilis 
cases, the cumulative change in new gonorrhoea infections with HL TetR, and the BCRs over the 10-year 
intervention period (2025-2034). The results of these one-way sensitivity analyses for each of the five doxy-
PEP strategies are presented as tornado plots in Supplementary Figures 20–23. 
The key parameters varied in these sensitivity analyses, along with their tested ranges, included: 
(9) Doxy-PEP intervention parameters: 
K. Prescription duration: varied from 3 months (13 weeks) to 12 months (52 weeks), would also 
lead to the prescribed doxy-PEP range from 26 to 104 doses (base case: 6 months). 
L. Uptake rate among eligible individuals: 50% to 100%, (base case: 75%). 
M. Adherence to taking doxy-PEP post-exposure: 60% to 100%, (base case: 80%). 
N. Pathogen-specific efficacies of doxy-PEP: 
g) Gonorrhoea: 13% to 66% (base case: 45%). 
h) Chlamydia: 56% to 92% (base case: 81%). 
i) Syphilis: 64% to 86% (base case: 77%). 
O. Probability of HL TetR development in N. gonorrhoeae per doxy -PEP use: varied by ±50% 
around the base -case value (which is linked to the calibrated weekly post -mid-2018 
background spontaneous HL TetR development rate). 
(10) STI natural history and epidemiological parameters: 
A. Proportion of new gonorrhoea infections that are symptomatic: 15% to 45% (base case: 30%).

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 113 -->

B. Probability of ceftriaxone treatment failure for gonorrhoea with ceftriaxone DS: varied by ±50% 
around the calibrated base-case values. 
C. Background weekly rate of spontaneous HL TetR development in N. gonorrhoeae (post mid -
2018): varied by ±50% around its calibrated base-case value. 
D. Initial STI positive rates (prevalence at start of 2012) for key subgroups (HIV -positive, PrEP-
users): varied by ±50% around their calibrated base-case values. 
(11) Behavioural parameters: 
A. Condom use frequency among individuals reporting “sometimes” using condoms: varied from 
25% (lower protection) to 75% (higher protection) of sexual acts with condom use (base case: 
50%). 
B. Proportions of individuals in each subgroup who “always use condoms”: baseline proportions 
(from Supplementary Figure 8) varied by multiplying them by 0.5 and 1.5. 
(12) Economic parameters: 
Annual discount rate for costs and QALYs: 0% to 6% (base case: 3%).” 
For the reviewer’s convenience, copies of the aforementioned Figures R8-11 were appended below this 
response. In the subsequent responses, we will address each of the specific parameters you suggested for 
sensitivity analysis point by point, detailing the variations tested and summarizing their impact by referring to 
these consolidated Figures R8-R11.

model parameters in the figure panels and not having explicit ranges defined on the y -axis, their plausible

Comment 40: 
Figure 1:

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 118 -->

- Brighter colors would improve legibility. 
- As mentioned above, an outcome measure that normalizes for the number of Doxy -PEP prescriptions 
would help place these results in context. Could the authors include such a measure in Figure 1 (e.g., the new 
infections averted per Doxy-PEP prescription)? 
Response: 
We thank the Reviewer for their constructive suggestions regarding Figure 1. In response to the feedback 
on legibility, we have revised the colour palette used for the lines in Figure 1 to enhance visual clarity.  
Furthermore, to aid readers in consistently tracking the performance of individual intervention strategies 
across different visualizations, we have implemented uniform markers for each specific scenario, ensuring 
these markers are consistent across Figure 1, Figure 2 (detailing gonorrhoea AMR impacts), and the new 
Figure 4 (presenting the multi-criteria strategy rankings). 
Regarding the inclusion of an outcome measure normalized by the number of doxy-PEP prescriptions, we 
appreciate the reviewer reiterating this important point. As indicated in our response to your earlier comment 
on this matter (Comment 17/33, concerning the fair comparison of strategies), we fully agree with its utility. 
Accordingly, we have incorporated this metric into the revised Figur e 1. Specifically, Panel H of Figure 
1 now presents both the cumulative total STIs averted and the important efficiency measure of new infections 
averted per doxy -PEP prescription for each of the evaluated strategies, allowing for a more contextualized 
comparison of their impacts.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 119 -->

“Figure 1. Projected impact of various doxy -PEP strategies on STI incidence, doxy -PEP 
prescriptions, coverage, and intervention efficiency among GBMSM (2024 -2034). Panels (A) to (D) 
illustrate the mean annual incidence rates (per 100 person -years) of gonorrhoea, chlamydia, syphilis, and all 
three STIs combined, respectively, across different doxy -PEP scenarios. Panel (E) shows the mean annual 
doxy-PEP prescriptions (in thousands). Panel (F) depicts the mean annual doxy -PEP coverage percentage 
among eligible individuals. Panel (G) presents the mean cumulative incident STIs (syphilis, chlamydia, 
gonorrhoea; left y -axis, in thousands) and the mean cumulative doxy -PEP pr escriptions (right y -axis, in 
thousands) over the 2025-2034 period. Panel (H) displays the mean total infections averted (IA; left y-axis, in 
thousands) and the mean infections averted per doxy-PEP prescription (IA per prescription; right y-axis) over 
the 2025-2034 period. All results shown are means (with or without 95% CI) calculated from simulations 
based on 100 parameter sets. The black dashed line in panel (A) refers to the average incidence rate in 2020. 
The red dashed lines in panels (A) to (C) represent the 2030 target incidences, derived by reducing respective 
2020 incidences to meet WHO ’s reduction targets (90% for gonorrhoea, 50% for chlamydia, and 90% for 
syphilis). Doxy-PEP=doxycycline post-exposure prophylaxis. GBMSM=gay, bisexual, and other me n who 
have sex with men. IA=infections averted. PrEP=pre-exposure prophylaxis. PY=person-years. STIs=sexually 
transmitted infections. WHO=World Health Organization.” 
Comment 41: 
Figure 2: 
- As mentioned above, Figure 2 could be improved by representing res istance proportions rather than 
absolute numbers. For example, by changing the y-axis in panels A-D and/or adding the resistance proportions 
to the stacked bar plot in panel E. Another option would be to replace panels A -D with Figure S10 from the 
supplement. 
- Please use consistent acronyms for the strains, e.g., either ceftriaxone DS or CRO DS. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for their thoughtful suggestions on how to best represent the antimicrobial 
resistance (AMR) profiles in Figure 2 . In response,  we have adopted a two-figure approach to 
comprehensively present AMR dynamics: Figure 2 now focuses on absolute numbers and incidence 
rates, while a new Figure 3 is dedicated to showing the proportions, as you suggested. We have also 
revised the manuscript to consistently use the acronym ‘ceftriaxone DS’. 
Our two-figure approach allows us to clearly present both the absolute scale of resistant infections 
and their changing proportional burden without overloading a single figure. As noted in our response to 
your earlier Comment 18, the revised Figure 2 is retained to focus on the absolute epidemiological impact, 
presenting annual incidence rates and the cumulative number of new gonorrhoea infections, stratified by the 
four key antimicrobial susceptibility pro files. Complementing this, and to directly address your 
recommendation, the new Figure 3  is now dedicated entirely to illustrating the annual changes in the 
proportion of these four susceptibility profiles among all newly incident gonorrhoea infections. 
Furthermore, regarding terminology, we have ensured the acronym for ceftriaxone susceptibility is 
used consistently throughout the manuscript.  We have reviewed the text, figures, and tables and can 
confirm that the term for decreased susceptibility to ceft riaxone is now uniformly referred to as “ceftriaxone 
DS.” We believe this combination of a new figure for proportions and consistent terminology effectively 
addresses both of your valuable suggestions.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 120 -->

“Figure 2. Impact of doxy -PEP on incident gonorrhoea  infections across different antibiotic 
susceptibility statuses among GBMSM. Panels (A)-(D) show mean annual gonorrhoea incidence rates (per 
100 person-years) under various scenarios, by antibiotic susceptibility status: (A) “Neither” (exhibiting neither 
ceftriaxone DS nor HL TetR); (B) “Ceftriaxone DS only”; (C) “HL TetR only”; and (D) “Both” (concurrent 
ceftriaxone DS and HL TetR). Panel (E) projects 10-year outcomes (2025-2034): the left y-axis presents mean 
cumulative total gonorrhoea infections (thousa nds), by these four susceptibility categories. The right y -axis 
displays the mean cumulative increase in gonorrhoea infections exhibiting HL TetR (sum of “HL TetR only” 
and “Both” categories, in thousands) compared to the base case, with 95% CI error bars. All results shown are 
means (with or without 95% CI) calculated from simulations based on 100 parameter sets. GBMSM= gay, 
bisexual, and other men who have sex with men. STIs=sexually transmitted infections. Doxy -
PEP=doxycycline post-exposure prophylaxis. PrEP=pre-exposure prophylaxis. DS=decreased susceptibility. 
HL TetR=high-level tetracycline resistance (used as a proxy for doxycycline resistance).”

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 121 -->

“Figure 3. Annual proportions of incident gonorrhoea infections with different antibiotic susceptibility 
statuses across various scenarios, 2024 to 2034.  Each panel (A -F) corresponds to a specific scenario, 
illustrating the changing annual proportions of four N. gonorrhoeae antibiotic susceptibility statuses among 
incident infections over the period shown. T he stacked areas represent infections characterized by: neither 
ceftriaxone DS nor HL TetR; ceftriaxone DS only; HL TetR only; and both ceftriaxone DS and HL TetR. 
Percentage labels on the left of each panel denote these respective proportions for 2024 (the year immediately 
preceding the 10 -year intervention period), while labels on the right denote proportions for 2034 (the final 
year of the 10 -year intervention period). All results shown are means calculated from simulations based on 
100 parameter sets.DS=decreased susceptibility; HL TetR=high-level tetracycline resistance (used as a proxy 
for doxycycline resistance); Doxy -PEP=doxycycline post-exposure prophylaxis; STIs=sexually transmitted 
infections; PrEP=pre-exposure prophylaxis.” 
Comment 42: 
- The paper is missing a discussion of the analysis relative to Traeger et al., 2023. What are the differences 
or similarities in estimates of antibiotic use or reduction in infections, in which strategies are optimal, etc. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for their valuable suggestion to include a discussion comparing our analysis with 
the findings of Traeger et al., 2023, particularly concerning estimates of antibiotic use, infection reduction, 
and optimal strategies.  We agree that such a comparison provides imp ortant context for our results. 
Accordingly, we have added the following discussion to the manuscript to address these points: 
“Our findings are consistent with previous evaluation on the efficiency of syphilis diagnosis -based 
targeting strategy, although our model projects a substantially higher number of infections averted per

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 122 -->

prescription for such strategies, which may stem from our modelling of further transmission effects and 
extended protection from full medication course use.” 
This added discussion highlights both consistencies (e.g., efficiency of STI history -based targeting) and 
differences (e.g., infections averted per prescription), while also offering potential reasons for these variations 
based on our model’s specific structure and outcome defin itions. We believe this addresses the reviewer ’s 
request for a comparative discussion. 
Comment 43: 
- If the goal is to examine strategies targeting syphilis reduction (as in the Australian consensus statement), 
did the authors consider strategies where partners of patients with syphilis are prescribed Doxy -PEP? (lines 
335ff) 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this insightful question regarding strategies targeting the partners of syphilis 
patients. Our current study did not explicitly model prescribing doxy-PEP to partners, primarily due to 
the significant number of additional complex assumptions that would be required for which robust 
empirical data are currently unavailable.  The paragraphs below elaborate on these complexities and our 
view on this approach as an avenue for future research. 
Implementing such a partner -focused strategy in our model would necessitate several key 
assumptions for which robust empirical data are limited.  These would include, for example, defining 
which types of partners (e.g. , casual, regular, or both) would be offered doxy -PEP, the proportion of index 
patients who would agree to or be able to facilitate partner notification for this purpose, and the subsequent 
uptake rates of doxy-PEP among these notified partners. 
Despite these current modelling challenges, we agree that prescribing doxy -PEP to partners of 
syphilis patients is a highly relevant and potentially impactful intervention that warrants careful 
evaluation in future work. Such a strategy has the potential to more d irectly and intensely disrupt syphilis 
transmission networks, aligning well with focused public health goals. We are very interested in exploring this 
approach, which would require further model development and dedicated efforts to gather or estimate the 
necessary parameters for these partner management pathways. 
Comment 44: 
- Lines 358ff: The recommendation to “maintain the current screening frequency” seems out of place and 
is not supported by any of the data in this study. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for highlighting that our previous statement regarding the recommendation to 
“maintain the current screening frequency” was out of place and not directly supported by the primary analyses 
presented in this study. We agree with this assessment. In our revised manuscript, we have removed that 
specific recommendation and have instead refined our discussion on the interplay between doxy -PEP 
and STI screening to better reflect the current study ’s scope and to align with existing guidance.  The 
updated discussion now emphasizes the importance of appropriate STI screening for individuals using doxy -
PEP, as underscored by the Australian consensus statement, and points to the need for future research in this 
area. The revised text in the Discussion section now reads: 
“Therefore, it may be more effective to incorporate doxy -PEP into a comprehensive sexual health 
program that includes other interventions such as vaccination for gonorrhoea and regular screening. However, 
decisions regarding screening frequency for  doxy-PEP users are complex; while frequent screening for

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 123 -->

asymptomatic gonorrhoea and chlamydia is generally recommended, it may also lead to increased antibiotic 
consumption and potentially heighten AMR. A post hoc analysis from the DoxyPEP randomised con trolled 
trial suggested that reducing STI screening for doxy -PEP users could decrease clinic visits but might delay 
the diagnosis of asymptomatic chlamydia infections. Indeed, the Australian consensus statement underscores 
the importance of appropriate STI screening for doxy-PEP users, and future modelling studies are needed to 
quantify the population impact of implementing doxy -PEP in conjunction with such potentially enhanced 
testing protocols, an approach not assessed in the current work.” 
Comment 45: 
- Lines 396ff: “Third, because of limited utility data, we applied lifetime QALY losses per STI rather than 
stage-specific losses. Incorporating stage -specific data in future studies could yield more precise QALY 
estimates.” Could the authors please expand on this limitation? It seems that with the individual-based model, 
it should be possible to incorporate disease stages. 
Response: 
We thank the Reviewer for their comment regarding our QALY calculations and for noting the capacity 
of individual-based models (IBMs) to incorporate stage-specific disease details. We agree that IBMs provide 
a powerful framework for such granularity. To clarify our primary rationale and reflect this directly in the 
manuscript, we have revised the relevant limitation statement, which now reads: “Third, primarily to simplify 
the quality-of-life assessment, we applied published lifetime QALY losses per STI instead of modelling stage-
specific losses ”. Our original phrasing in the limitation concerning “limited utility data ” was intende d to 
convey the scarcity of comprehensive, stage-specific utility values that are directly applicable to the Australian 
GBMSM context and align perfectly with all clinical stages and individual trajectories simulated for three 
distinct STIs within our dynamic model. 
While some international literature does provide estimates for stage -specific utility decrements, the 
process of adapting and integrating these into a fully individualized, dynamic QALY framework for thousands 
of agents across gonorrhoea, chlamy dia, and syphilis —each with multiple, potentially concurrent, and 
variably symptomatic stages —introduces considerable methodological complexities. These include 
standardizing diverse data sources, making assumptions for stages where data might be sparse or less directly 
applicable to the Australian setting, and robustly modelling the accumulation of QALY decrements based on 
individual-specific durations and experiences within each stage. Given these intricacies, and to maintain a 
clear focus on the primary epidemiological outcomes and the cost-effectiveness analysis driven by infections 
averted and direct medical costs, we opted for a more streamlined approach to the QALY estimation. Therefore, 
we utilized established, published lifetime QALY loss estimates per incident STI. This widely recognized 
method, while more aggregated, allowed us to incorporate the significant health-related quality of life impacts 
in a robust and more straightforward manner, ensuring this component of the economic evaluation remaine d 
tractable and comparable. 
We acknowledge that this approach simplifies the dynamic QALY experience. However, it provides a 
meaningful, albeit broader, measure of the quality -of-life implications. We continue to agree that refining 
QALY calculations with more granular, directly applicable stage -specific, and ideally Australian -
contextualized utility data is a valuable objective for future, more focused health economic modelling 
extensions. 
Minor Comment 1: 
Line 295: typo “avertin” instead of “averting”

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 124 -->

Response: 
Thank you for pointing out this typographical error. “Avertin” has been corrected to “averting” in this part 
of the text. We have also carefully checked the entire manuscript for any similar typographical errors. 
Minor Comment 2: 
The supplement would benefit from revision, as it is repetitive and hard to follow. For example, on pages 
16ff, the models are described in vague terms and then later the parameter values are introduced. It would be 
easier if the parameters were introduced together with the models (or if the model description did not reference 
vague parameters). For example, mixing based on seroconcordance is vaguely explained on page 4 (“The 
formation of regular partnerships adhered to their requirements for seroconcordance. ”) and then l ater 
explained again in more detail on page 8. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this valuable feedback on improving the clarity and structure of the 
Supplementary Material. In response to your suggestions, we have comprehensively revised the 
supplement: key methodological descriptions are now more closely integrated with their corresponding 
parameters, and preliminary mentions of complex concepts have been removed from the overview 
section to improve the logical flow. 
Specifically, to address your point about vague initial descriptions, we have enhanced the cross -
referencing throughout the document. This ensures a clear and easy-to-follow link between the description 
of a model component and its specific parameters, which are detailed in the parameter tab les. This approach 
aims to reduce any perception of initial vagueness by better connecting the conceptual descriptions with their 
quantitative inputs. 
Furthermore, following your example regarding seroconcordance, we have removed such 
preliminary mentions from the general overview.  The comprehensive explanation of the serosorting 
mechanism is now consolidated in the dedicated methodological section where it is first detailed (Section 
1.2.5). We have applied this principle throughout the supplement to ensure concepts are fully explained where 
they are most relevant, which reduces repetition. We believe these structural revisions have significantly 
improved the narrative flow and readability of the Supplementary Material, as you recommended. 
Minor Comment 3: 
Figure S5: What do you mean by “ratio of condomless sex”? 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for pointing out the need for clarification regarding the terminology used in relation 
to the figure previously designated as Figure S5. To clarify, the term “ratio of condomless sex” is no longer 
relevant as we have revised our methodology; we now use directly stratified empirical data to 
parameterize condom use, as detailed in Supplementary Section 1.3.4 and the new Supplementary 
Figure 8. 
Our current approach utilizes average data from recent Gay Community Periodic Surveys (GCPS) 
in Melbourne (2020–2024), which directly provide the necessary stratification not available in earlier 
data. This recent dataset was chosen because it contains condom use information (proportions of individuals

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 125 -->

“always using a condom” and “sometimes not using a condom”) already stratified by our three key GBMSM 
subgroups (HIV-positive, HIV-negative PrEP-using, and HIV-negative PrEP non-using) for both regular and 
casual partnerships. This more  direct data source makes the previous method of applying risk ratios to an 
overall baseline unnecessary. 
To parameterize our model, we process the raw GCPS survey categories to align with our model ’s 
two condom use preferences (“always” vs. “sometimes”). As detailed in Supplementary Section 1.3.4, 
individuals from both the GCPS categories of “reporting no recent sexual activity ” and “always using a 
condom” are grouped together into our model ’s “always use condoms ” classification. The GCPS category 
“sometimes not using a condom ” directly informs our model ’s corresponding preference. The resulting 
derived average proportions are then applied according to the stratification available in the 2020 -2024 data, 
as shown in Supplementary Figure 8 . This revised metho dology provides a more direct and transparent 
approach to parameterizing stratified condom use.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 126 -->
