I co-reviewed this manuscript with one of the reviewers who provided the listed reports. This is part of 
the Nature Communications initiative to facilitate training in peer review and to provide appropriate 
recognition for Early Career Researchers who co-review manuscripts. 
Response: 
We thank Reviewers 3 and 4 for their contributions to the peer-review process and for informing us about 
their co-review arrangement. We appreciate Nature Communications’ initiative to facilitate training in peer 
review and to provide appropriate recogniti on for Early Career Researchers who undertake this important 
work. Such programs are valuable for the scientific community. 
Comment 2 (from the reviewer 4): 
The full version of the code was not provided. I am not able to run the code as key files/parameters were 
not provided. 
Response: 
We thank reviewer 4 for bringing to our attention that the previously provided code was incomplete. In 
response to this, we have now uploaded the full version of our code used for the modelling simulations and 
for processing the outputs. The code of modelling and visualizing to reproduce all the tables and figures in the 
main text and Supplementary Information are publicly available on GitHub at https://github.com/Leo-
HaoL/Doxy_PEP_Model_AU. This revised submissio n includes all necessary files, parameters, and an 
explanatory document to facilitate the running of the code and replication of our analyses.

Minor comment 1: 
Please review the manuscript and Supplemental materials for missing/repeated definitions of acronyms. 
For example, PrEP was not defined in the Abstract section and GCPS was defined twice in the Supplemental 
materials. Please also check the definitions of ANCP, SCC, SSD, CI and ACCESS. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for pointing this out. We have carefully reviewed the manuscript and 
supplementary materials to ensure all acronyms (including PrEP, GCPS, ANCP, SCC, CI, and ACCESS) 
are defined correctly at their first use and that all duplicate definitions have been removed. 
(1) As suggested, we have now used the full name of PrEP in the abstract  as it only occurs once . The 
revised sentence reads: 
“This proportion was highest (97.4%) when targeting all HIV pre-exposure prophylaxis users, and lowest 
(81.2%) for the syphilis diagnosis strategy.” 
(2) As suggested, we have removed the duplicate definitions for GCPS, ANCP, and ACCESS in the 
supplementary materials to ensure they are only defined once at their first mention. 
(3) We have added definitions for other acronyms as needed. For example, SCC is now defined in Section 
1.2.5 (first use), and CI is now defined in Section 1.2.3 (first use) of the supplementary materials. 
(4) We have also reviewed all other acronyms. The duplicate definition for HL TetR has been removed, 
and we have confirmed that other key acronyms  (e.g., IA, BCR, DS, NG) are correctly defined at their first 
appearance and used consistently thereafter. 
Minor comment 2: 
Please review the manuscript and Supplemental materials for label consistency. For example, 
Supplementary Fig. 16 and Fig. 1 were us ed in the main text, but Figure 16 and Figure 1 were used in the 
figure headings. As another example, scenario 1 was used in line 325 but Scenario 3 was used in line 328. 
Please also check the use of Table 1/2 and Table S3/S4 in the Supplemental materials. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for their careful reading and for highlighting these inconsistencies. We have now 
performed a thorough review of the manuscript and supplementary materials to ensure all labels and 
references (including for figures, scenarios, tables, and sections) are consistent throughout. 
(1) Figure labelling: As suggested, we have standardized the naming of all figures. All figure references 
now use the full word “Figure”. Figures in the main text are referred to as “Figure X”, and figures in the 
supplementary materials are referred to as “Supplementary Figure X ”. The abbreviated form “Fig.” is no 
longer used. 
(2) Scenario labelling:  We have standardized the use of “scenario” to be lowercase throughout the 
manuscript and supplementary materials unless it appears at the beginning of a sentence 
(3) Table labelling: We have unified the naming convention for all tables. Tables in the main text are now 
consistently referred to as “Table X ”, and tables in the supplementary materials are r eferred to as 
“Supplementary Table X”. The format “Table SX” has been removed. 
(4) Other consistency checks: We have also performed a final check for other similar inconsistencies. 
This included standardising the capitalisation when referring to specific sections of the text (e.g., “Section” at

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 131 -->

9 
the start of a sentence and “section” within a sentence). No other labelling inconsistencies were identified. 
We are confident that these revisions have significantly improved the consistency and readability of the 
manuscript. 
Minor comment 3: 
In the abstract, it is worth noting the large proportion of new gonorrhea cases w ith HL TetR in the base 
case, followed by the differences between the base case and the intervention strategies. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this excellent suggestion. We agree that highlighting the high baseline 
proportion of HL TetR gonorrhoea provides important context for the intervention outcomes. We have revised 
the abstract to include this information. The revised sentences now read: 
“However, compared to the high 73.5% baseline projection for high-level tetracycline resistance by 2034, 
all strategies yielded higher proportions.  This proportion was highest (9 7.4%) when targeting all HIV pre -
exposure prophylaxis users, and lowest (81.2%) for the syphilis diagnosis strategy.” 
Minor comment 4: 
Line 136: It may be worth pointing out the projected incidence rate/proportion of gonorrhea resistant to 
both drugs under different strategies, which potentially affects the treatment of gonorrhea (PMID: 36364980). 
It is interesting to see the incidence of gonorrhea resistant to both drugs under Scenarios 3 and 4 was similar 
to the one under Scenario 6 which had the highest number of doxy-PEP prescriptions. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this valuable suggestion and for their insightful observation. We have addressed 
both points as follows: 
1. Reporting on dual-resistant gonorrhoea 
We agree that explicitly reporting on gonorrhoea with dual resistance is important given its clinical 
implications for treatment. We have now added specific text and quantifi cation to the “Impacts on 
antimicrobial profiles of gonorrhoea” section of the Results. The revised text now includes sentences such as: 
“Furthermore, the cumulative infections exhibiting both ceftriaxone DS and HL TetR also increased from 
2,169 (95% CI: 1 ,796-2,543) in the base case to a range of 2,318 (95% CI: 1 ,924-2,713) for the syphilis -
targeting strategy to 2,706 (95% CI: 2,250-3,163) for the strategy targeting PrEP users.” 
And for the proportions: 
“By 2034, the proportion of infections exhibiting both Ceftriaxone DS and HL TetR increased to a range 
of 19.8% (syphilis-targeting strategy) to 23.3% (targeting PrEP users).” 
2. Clarifying the relationship between the number of doxy-PEP prescriptions and dual resistance 
We thank you for this insightful observation regarding the similarity in the incidence of dual -resistant 
(exhibiting both ceftriaxone DS and HL TetR) gonorrhoea between certain strategies in our previous figure. 
Your comment prompted a rigorous re-evaluation of our simulation methodology, which has led to a clearer 
and more robust finding. 
In our initial simulations, we used a standard seeding approach that, while conventional, could lead to 
subtle stochastic biases when comparing scenar ios with different numbers of events (e.g., fewer infections).

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 132 -->

10 
This may have obscured the true underlying trend you noted. To ensure a perfectly fair comparison, we have 
implemented two key improvements: 
1) A deterministic random number generation system. Our primary improvement was to implement 
a method where the random number generated for a potential event depends deterministically on the 
agent’s unique ID, the type of event, and the simulation time step (using a hash function).  This 
guarantees that if the same potential event occurs for the same agent at the same time point across 
different scenarios, it receives the exact same random draws  (e.g., the same probability check for 
infection upon exposure). This approach effectively eliminates the bias caused by diverging pseudo-
random sequences and isolates the impact of the intervention strategy itself when comparing 
outcomes. 
2) Increased runs of simulation.  Alongside this core methodological improvement, to enhance the 
stability of our estimates during the cal ibration phase, we significantly increased the number of 
simulation runs used to evaluate each parameter set from one (in our previous analysis) to thirty, 
enhancing the stability of our estimates. 
The results from these refined simulations are presented i n the revised Figure 2D. While the absolute 
incidence rates between the high -uptake strategies remain close, the new results now reveal a clear and 
consistent dose-response relationship . The rank order of the annual incidence of infections with both 
ceftriaxone DS and HL TetR now perfectly aligns with the number of doxy -PEP prescriptions. Specifically, 
the strategy with the most prescriptions (targeting PrEP users) now unambiguously results in the highest 
incidence of dual-resistant infections, followed in descending order by the other strategies according to their 
prescription volumes. 
We are grateful for your comment, as it has led to a more robust analysis and a clearer presentation of this 
critical dose-response effect. 
Minor comment 5: 
Multi-criteria evaluation: The ranking for each single indicator makes sense but the ranking for 
combinations of indicators seems ambiguous (Figure 4). For example, to prioritize maximal STI reduction 
and minimal increase in NG with HL TetR, Scenarios 3 (two STI  in 6m), 4 (two STI in 12m) and 6 (PrEP) 
have the same score of 6 if summing individual indicator ranks with equal weight. However, Scenario 3 ranks 
higher than the other two in Figure 4. Similarly, to prioritize maximal STI reduction and maximal BCR, both 
Scenario 2 (syphilis) and 3 (two STI in 6m) have the lowest score of 5 but Scenario 2 ranks higher in Figure 
4. Supplementary Figures 18–19 seem to better depict the order of implementation between strategies: based 
on the efficiency, Scenario 2 should be prioritized, followed by Scenario 4 if aiming for further STI reduction, 
or Scenario 6 if aiming for further syphilis reduction. In addition, is it possible to implement the efficient 
frontier analysis to balance the reduction in STIs and the increase in NG with HL TetR/both resistances? 
Response: 
We are sincerely grateful to the reviewer for this exceptionally insightful and critical comment. We agree 
with the reviewer ’s assessment regarding the ambiguity  and potential inconsistencies in our previous 
ranking method. Your examples perfectly demonstrated the limitations of simply summing individual ranks, 
a method which we agree was insufficient. 
To address this, we have completely revised our ranking method ology, moving from simply summing 
ranks to a formal approach using composite scores derived from min -max normalized data , and, as 
requested, we have implemented new efficiency frontier analyses specifically balancing STI reduction

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 133 -->

11 
against increases in resistant gonorrhoea. 
For full transparency, here is the updated text from the Methods section (“Evaluation framework of doxy-
PEP strategies”): 
“For frameworks combining multiple indicators, we first normalized the mean outcome values for each 
indicator to a common [0, 1] scale using min-max normalization (calculated as [value - minimum value] / 
[maximum value - minimum value] across all strategies  for a given indicator). This normalization allows 
comparison and combination of indicators measured on different scales.  The adverse indicator, HL TetR 
increase, was reverse-scaled (using 1 - normalized value) to ensure a higher score is consistently more  
favourable. A composite score for each strategy was then calculated by summing the normalized values of the 
constituent indicators, assuming equal weighting. Based on these composite scores, strategies were 
subsequently ranked, with a higher composite sco re indicating better overall performance. These rankings 
(Figure 4) aid decision-making by showing how strategy prioritization varies with policy goals.” 
As this revised text explains, our new approach accounts for the magnitude of the performance 
differences between strategies, not just their ordinal position, thereby resolving the ambiguities the reviewer 
correctly identified. The rankings presented in the revised Figure 4 (attached as Figure R1) are now derived 
from this more defensible method. 
Furthermore, we thank you for the excellent suggestion to use efficiency frontier analysis. We agree this 
is a powerful way to visualize the trade -offs and have now performed two separate analyses balancing the 
number of infections averted against the number of additional resistant infections: 
1) First, we balanced the reduction in overall STIs against the increase in gonorrhoea with HL TetR 
(Supplementary Figure 20, attached as Figure R2). 
2) Second, recognizing the critical clinical importanc e of dual resistance, we conducted an additional 
analysis balancing STI reduction against the increase in gonorrhoea with both ceftriaxone DS and 
HL TetR (Supplementary Figure 21, attached as Figure R3). 
Your comments have been invaluable and have directly led to a significant improvement in the clarity and 
methodological rigor of our paper. We are confident that these revisions have substantially strengthened our 
analysis. 
 
Figure R1. Doxy-PEP strategy rankings across different policy prioritization frameworks. This figure illustrates 
the relative ranking (from Rank 1 [most favourable] to Rank 5 [least favourable]) of five distinct doxy -PEP

12 
implementation strategies across eleven policy prioritization frameworks. The strategies were evaluated based on  their 
performance against four key indicators: (1) maximal reduction in overall Sexually Transmitted Infections (STI Reduc.); 
(2) maximal reduction in syphilis incidence (Syphilis Reduc.); (3) optimal outcomes concerning high-level tetracycline 
resistance in N. gonorrhoeae (HL TetR; i.e., minimizing its increase); and (4) maximal Benefit -Cost Ratio (BCR). The 
x-axis specifies each framework, showing rankings based on these indicators considered singly or in combinations. For

transmitted infections. Reduc.=reductions. HL TetR=high -level tet racycline resistance. BCR=benefit -cost ratio. 
PrEP=pre-exposure prophylaxis.

Minor comment 9: 
Line 337: Did the authors mean gonorrhea without HL TetR? 
Response: 
Yes, the reviewer’s interpretation is correct. We thank the reviewer for this precise question, which helps 
us clarify a critical detail in our model ’s methodology: the 45% prevention efficacy for gonorrhoea is 
applied only to strains without high-level tetracycline resistance (HL TetR). 
To ensure this is perfectly clear for all readers, we have revised the sentence in the Methods section. The 
updated text now reads: 
“For prevention, when a participant engaged in condomless sex and used doxy-PEP within 72 hours, the 
probability of establishing an infection was reduced by 45% for gonorrhoea without HL TetR , 81% for 
chlamydia, and 77% for syphilis, based on pathogen-specific prevention efficacies from a meta-analysis.” 
Furthermore, to proactively address a potential concern about this parameter —a critical nuance that was 
also a key point of discussion during the previous review round  with another reviewer—we wish to provide 
the rationale for this modelling choice. The issue is whether the 45% efficacy from meta -analyses is a 
“blended” real-world value influenced by existing tetracycline resistance, rather than a purely “intrinsic” 
efficacy against susceptible strains. Our approach is summarized by the following points: 
1) The 45% efficacy is a “blended” real-world value: We agree with the premise that the 45% efficacy 
from meta -analyses is not an “intrinsic” efficacy against perfectly  susceptible strains. It reflects 
performance in populations with mixed gonococcal susceptibility. 
2) Our model dichotomizes this reality: Our model operationalizes this by assuming 0% efficacy  
against infections with HL TetR, while applying the meta -analysed 45% efficacy  to all other 
gonorrhoea strains. 
3) This approach is conservative and evidence-based: This is an appropriately conservative 
assumption. Recent clinical and pharmacokinetic evidence  (PMIDs: 37018493, 38428259)  shows 
imperfect prevention even against strains considered “susceptible” by lower thresholds, suggesting

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 137 -->

15 
that a higher, purely “intrinsic” value would be unjustifiably optimistic. 
4) Using meta-analysis is more robust: Relying on a meta-analysed average provides a more stable and 
generalizable estimate than data from a single study, which could be heavily influenced by its specific 
local AMR prevalence. The uncertainty around this parameter is further explored in our sensitivity 
analysis (range 13%–66%). 
We are grateful for your question, as it has allowed us to improve the precision of our Methods section 
and provide this important context for our modelling assumptions.

Minor comment 14: (Supplementary) 
Line 545: Please add the rationale for assuming that individuals become non-infectious immediately upon 
commencing treatment. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this important question, which allows us to clarify a key modelling assumption. 
As requested, we have now added a detailed rationale to the supplementary materials justifying the standard 
assumption of immediate non-infectiousness upon treatment, based on methodological (model time step), 
biological/pharmacological (rapid antibiotic action), and clinical/behavioural (abstinence counselling)

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 140 -->

18 
factors. 
“Our model assumes that individuals become non -infectious immediately upon commencing treatment. 
This is a standard simplifying assumption in infectious disease modelling, justified by a confluence of 
biological, clinical, and methodological factors, particularly in the context of our model’s structure. 
1) Methodological rationale and model timescale: Our individual-based model operates on a one-
week time step. The biological time to non-infectiousness following antibiotic treatment is typically 
24-72 hours. As this period is substantially shorter than our model’s one-week cycle, any remaining 
infectiousness within the first few days is not considered at the model ’s temporal resolution. This 
approach is consistent with other agent -based models of STI transmission (e.g., 
https://nccid.ca/publications/agent-based-modelling-of-chlamydia-trachomatis-transmission-in-a-
canadian-subpopulation/). 
2) Biological and pharmacological rationale: Recommended first-line antibiotic regimens act swiftly 
to neutralize pathogens long before the end of the first one-week model cycle. 
a) For syphilis, Canadian public health guidelines note that T. pallidum is rendered non-infectious 
within approximately 24 hours of treatment with benzathine penicillin G 
(https://www.canada.ca/en/public-health/services/infectious-diseases/sexual-health-sexually-
transmitted-infections/canadian-guidelines/syphilis/treatment-follow-up.html). 
b) For gonorrhoea, the recommended treatment, ceftriaxone, is a potent bactericidal agent. CDC 
guidelines imply rapid clearance, as a positive test -of-cure culture > 3 days after treatment 
completion indicates potential treatment failure, meaning non -infectiousness is expected well 
before this point (https://www.cdc.gov/std/treatment-guidelines/gonorrhea-adults.htm). 
c) For chlamydia, Australian guidelines for 7 -day doxycycline treatment note that a test -of-cure 
should not be performed within four weeks, as a positive result could reflect the detection of non-
viable DNA (https://sti.guidelines.org.au/sexually-transmissible-infections/chlamydia/). 
3) Clinical and behavioural rationale: The initiation of treatment is a multi -component intervention 
designed to immediately interrupt transmission. Standard clinical care, as reflected in Australian STI 
Management Guidelines, includes counselling patients to abstain from sexual contact for se ven 
days after treatment commences ( https://sti.guidelines.org.au/sexually-transmissible-
infections/gonorrhoea/). This behavioural directive acts in con cert with the medication to halt 
transmission at the source, an effect that our model captures by assuming immediate cessation of 
infectiousness. 
In summary, the combination of our model’s one-week time step, the rapid biological action of antibiotics, 
and the behavioural modifications inherent in clinical care makes the assumption of immediate cessation of 
infectiousness a robust and appropriate choice for this population-level model.” 
We have inserted this detailed rationale into the supplementary materia ls at the precise location you 
indicated. Furthermore, related to the model ’s temporal resolution discussed in this rationale, we have now 
also explicitly noted the limitation associated with the one-week time step within the manuscript: 
“First, our model ’s structure and temporal resolution may not capture all nuances of GBMSM sexual 
networks and behaviours.  For instance, operating on a one -week time step might obscure finer -grained 
dynamics related to very rapid transmission events or the precise timing of behavioural changes.” 
We are grateful for the opportunity to clarify this important aspect of our methodology.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 141 -->

19

Minor comment 22: (Supplementary) 
Line 727: It is worth mentioning that the infection statuses were initialized during a 52 -week burn-in 
period. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this excellent and precise point, which highlights a lack of clarity in our original 
description. 
The reviewer emphasized the need for a burn-in period after initializing infections. Our methodology did, 
in fact, include this crucial step, but our description in the main text was an oversimplifi cation that only 
mentioned the initial burn-in for the partnership network. 
To clarify our full, two-stage initialization process, we have revised the text in the supplementary

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 146 -->

24 
materials. The updated text is as follows: 
“As described in Algorithm 1, our simulation began with a two-stage initialization process. First, we ran 
the model for a 52-week (one-year) network burn-in period to allow the partnership network to reach a stable, 
dynamic equilibrium. Once this was achiev ed, we introduced the initial infections into the population. 
Following this, we ran the model for an additional 52-week infection burn-in period. This second burn-in 
allowed the newly introduced STIs to transmit and distribute naturally across their vario us disease stages 
before the main simulation period began at the start of 2012. The model then ran for 13 years, concluding at 
the end of 2024. Starting in 2025, we simulated the various doxy-PEP scenarios for ten years until the end of 
2034.” 
We are grateful for your suggestion, as it has allowed us to describe our methods with greater precision 
and transparency. 
Minor comment 23: (Supplementary) 
Line 809: It seems that κ should be κ_2. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for their meticulous reading and for identifying this inconsistency. 
The reviewer’s observation is correct. This was an oversight remaining from a previous revision where 
the parameter κ was split into two stages (κ_1 and κ_2), and the text at this location was not updated to reflect 
this change. 
We have now corrected the parameter to κ_2 at the specified line. Following this comment, we have 
also checked the entire manuscript and supplementary materials and can confirm that this was an isolated 
typographical error. We appreciate the help in ensuring the consistency of our text. 
Minor comment 24: (Supplementary) 
Figure 17: Why did the authors use the coverage of doxy-PEP as the x-axis instead of the number of doxy-
PEP prescriptions, given that the denominator of each coverage varied based on the  number of eligible 
individuals for each strategy? 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this important question for clarification. This is an excellent point that 
highlights an area where our terminology was not sufficiently defined and, as we discovered upon review, was 
described inconsistently. 
The reviewer’s concern is valid: if “coverage” were defined relative to the number of eligible individuals, 
the varying denominators would indeed make it a less direct measure for comparison than the number of 
prescriptions. However, in our study, “doxy-PEP coverage” refers to the proportion of the entire GBMSM 
population using doxy-PEP. Because the denominator (the total population) is constant across all scenarios, 
this measure of coverage is directly proportional to the total number of prescriptions administered. The dose-
response relationship shown in the figure is therefore robust. 
We acknowledge that our main text was confusing on this point, as it incorrectly referred to these values 
as “coverage levels among e ligible individuals. ” To prevent this ambiguity for all readers, we have now 
corrected the main text and added an explicit definition of “doxy-PEP coverage” at its first use in both 
the manuscript and the supplementary materials.

GBMSM using doxy-PEP) varied substantially across strategies... This resulted in projected average annual 
population coverage levels ranging from a low of approximately 2.8%... to a high of 26.1%...” 
The revised text in the supplementary now reads: 
“A strong positive correlation was found between higher average annual doxy-PEP coverage (defined as 
the proportion of all GBMSM using doxy-PEP)...” 
We appreciate the opportunity to make our manuscript more precise and thank the reviewer for helping 
us improve its clarity.

Comment 1 (Re Comment 3): 
We thank the authors for adopting our suggestion to calibrate to incidence, but we still have concerns 
about the calibration procedure. We were surprised to see the test positivity data dropped from the calibration 
targets. Why did the authors decide to calibrate only to incidence, not to both incidence and test positivity as 
in the prior version? These two data types together would allow calibration both to care seeking behavior 
(testing behavior) and transmission (incidence). 
We are perplexed by why the a uthors ran each simulation just once for each parameter combination. As 
the authors mention in the supplement, line 699, individual-based models are “highly stochastic”. That is why 
multiple simulation iterations are critical to reduce stochastic noise and  effectively compare the 10,000 
combinations of input parameters. 
The calibration process needs more description in the manuscript. In the supplement lines 703ff, the 
authors write: “We generated 10,000 unique parameter sets by sampling values for each unc ertain parameter 
from its predefined (typically uniform) distribution.” How were these parameter sets sampled? Did the authors 
sample the parameter ranges randomly? Using Latin Hypercube Sampling or a similar strategy could cover 
the parameter space more efficiently and would free up computational resources to run the simulation multiple 
times for each parameter combination. 
Response: 
We sincerely thank the reviewer for these critical and insightful comments on our calibration procedure. 
We agree completely that our previous approach had limitations that needed to be addressed. Your feedback 
has prompted us to implement a substantially more rigorous and robust calibration process, which we have 
updated throughout the manuscript and detail below. 
1. Re-incorporation of test positivity data 
Regarding the calibration targets, we agree that including both incidence and test positivity provides a 
more comprehensive calibration. We have now re-incorporated the test positivity data  from the same 
national surveillance system into our calibration targets. The updated calibration results, which now show a 
good fit to both data streams, are presented in the revised Figure R9. 
2. Multiple simulation runs and Latin Hypercube Sampling 
We agree entirely with your concerns regarding the single simulation run and the sampling method. Given 
the highly stochastic nature of our model, these were significant limitations. To address these core issues, we 
have improved our procedure: 
1) Sampling strategy: We adopted your excellent suggestion and replaced random sampling with Latin 
Hypercube Sampling (LHS) to generate the 10,000 parameter sets, ensuring more efficient parameter 
space coverage. 
2) Multiple simulation runs and parameter set selection:  
a) Calibration phase: We now run each of the 10,000 parameter sets 30 times, using a consistent set 
of 30 unique random seeds for every set. This multi-run approach is crucial for obtaining a reliable 
estimate of each parameter set ’s average performance, mitigating stochastic noise. Our choice of 
30 runs was guided by the heuristic from the Central Limit Theorem (PMID: 28367284; suggesting 
n≥30 for stable outputs) and computational feasibility. The goodness -of-fit is calculated by 
summing the sum of squared differenc es (SSD) from all 30 runs against the targets, yielding a

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 156 -->

34 
single aggregate SSD value per parameter set. This ensures selection is based on consistent 
performance, not a single “lucky” run. 
b) Selection and final projections: From this process, we selected the top 30 best-fitting parameter 
sets. Selecting multiple sets represents parameter uncertainty, a standard practice. Our approach is 
also consistent with the procedure in recent, highly relevant individual -based modelling work on 
doxy-PEP (e.g., see preprint at  https://doi.org/10.1101/2025.06.24.25330172), which utilized 
1,000 simulations for their base case analysis (though the specific breakdown between parameter 
sets and runs per set was not detailed). Final projections are based on 30 simulations performed 
for each of these 30 selected sets  (900 runs per scenario total), providing stable estimates 
incorporating both stochastic variability and parameter uncertainty. 
3. Deterministic Random Number Generation for Fair Comparisons 
Finally, your comments on managing stochasticity prompted us to strengthen our methodology for 
comparing the final intervention scenarios. To ensure fair comparisons, we have implemented a deterministic 
random number generation system. This method assigns a unique, repeatable random number to each agent 
for each specific event (based on a cryptographic hash function) . This guarantees that for any given 
parameter set, an individual receives the exact same random draws across all intervention scenarios, ensuring 
that observed differences are attributable solely to the intervention’s effect. 
We have thoroughly updated the Methods and Supplementary sections to detail this new , more 
rigorous calibration and simulation procedure. Revised text in the Methods and Supplementary w as listed as 
follows: 
1) Revised text in Methods:  
“Using Latin Hypercube Sampling, we generated 10,000 unique parameter sets by drawing values from 
predefined ranges ( Supplementary Table 2 ). Each set was run 30 times to ensure a robust performance 
evaluation, and the 30 sets minimizing the mean -squared differences between model outputs and calibration 
targets were selected for all subsequent analyses. From the se selected sets, key uncertain parameters were 
estimated; for example, the treatment failure rate for gonorrhoea infections with decreased ceftriaxone 
susceptibility was calibrated to 8.0% (95% CI: 6.2%–9.8%). All model outputs are reported as the mean and 
95% CI from simulations using these 30 selected sets, each run 30 times. Further details on all parameter 
values and model structure are available in the Supplementary Material.” 
2) Revised text in Supplementary:  
“… (2) We generated 10,000 unique paramet er sets using Latin Hypercube Sampling (LHS) to sample 
values for each uncertain parameter from its predefined distribution. (3) For each of these 10,000 parameter 
sets, we ran 30 model simulations from 2012 to 2023, using a consistent set of 30 unique ran dom seeds. We 
then calculated an aggregate goodness -of-fit value for each parameter set by summing the sum of squared 
differences (SSD) from each of the 30 runs between the model ’s outputs (annual STI incidence rates, annual 
STI test positivity, and annual NG AMR proportions) and the corresponding empirical calibration targets. (4) 
The 30 parameter sets that yielded the lowest aggregate SSD values were selected as the best -fitting sets. 
These 30 sets were then used for all subsequent analyses, with results typically presented as the mean and 95% 
confidence interval derived from 30 simulations for each of these 30 selected sets.”

Comment 2 (Re Comment 6): 
The explanations for stabilizing STI incidence raise further questions. In their response to reviewers, the 
authors write: “Given that the HIV-positive population has historically had a higher burden of these STIs, a 
reduction in this population size can c ontribute to a moderation in overall STI incidence. ” Do PLWH have 
higher STI prevalence because of HIV or because of behavior? What is causing the decline in HIV? If it is 
behavior, then we would expect STIs to decline as well, but if it’s PrEP or ART, then we wouldn’t expect that 
to impact STIs. 
The authors also write: “Furthermore, the model includes a significant scale-up of PrEP coverage among 
HIV-negative GBMSM (Figure R6B), coupled with higher STI testing frequencies among PrEP users (Figure 
R7). Increased testing leads to earlier detection and treatment of STIs, which can reduce onward transmission 
and consequently temper the rise in incidence or even lead to declines for some infections. It is also plausible 
that after a period of sustained increase,  the transmission dynamics for certain STIs within specific sexual 
networks may approach a form of natural equilibrium or “bottleneck”, especially when influenced by evolving 
prevention and testing behaviours like those associated with PrEP uptake.”  
It is unclear whether these are hypotheses the authors are proposing to explain a real -world stabilization 
of STI incidence or whether the authors observe these patterns in their model, i.e., increased STI testing and 
PrEP coverage. Test volume, transmission “bottlenecks”, and PrEP coverage could certainly be verified in the 
model outputs. These should then be compared to real-world data if used to justify their calibration fit. 
Response: 
We thank you for this insightful follow -up question. This is a critical point that allows us to clarify the

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 158 -->

36 
precise mechanisms within our model leading to STI incidence stabilization, distinguishing between data -
driven inputs and emergent model outcomes: the observed stabilization is an emergent property driven by 
calibrated behavioural parameters, population dynamics (consistent with observed data ), and the 
complex interplay with data-driven prevention scale-ups (PrEP coverage and testing frequency ), rather 
than external assumptions. 
We will address the two parts of your comment separately. 
1. The role of the PLWH population and drivers of HIV decline 
First, to directly answer your question about the PLWH population, the higher STI burden among PLWH 
in our model is explicitly driven by calibrated behavioural parameters, not the biological effects of HIV . 
As detailed in our supplementary materials, the PLWH subgroup is characterized by higher rates of partnership 
formation and lower condom use frequencies. 
Therefore, the stabilization of STI incidence is not driven by a simulated change in these underlying risk 
behaviours. Instead, it is an emergent phenomenon of several interconnected demographic and network 
dynamics that unfold over the simulation period: 
a) A demographic shift: The primary driver is a gradual decline in the proportion of the population living 
with HIV . Crucially, our model does not simulat e HIV transmission or the effects of ART . This 
decline is an outcome of population turnover (aging, mortality, emigration) that aligns with observed 
Australian data (Supplementary Figure 3A). As the proportion of PLWH decreases, the size of this 
key, high-STI-burden subgroup reduces. 
b) Network bottleneck effect: Our model incorporates serosorting preferences (detailed in 
Supplementary Section 1.2.5). As the PLWH population becomes smaller, forming new partnerships 
within their preferential network becomes subtly more difficult, reducing STI transmission efficiency 
specifically within this group. 
c) Prevalence feedback in new entrants: The STI prevalence assigned to new PLWH immigrants is 
based on th e current STI prevalence of the existing PLWH population. As STI incidence begins to 
stabilize, new entrants reinforce this trend. 
2. Clarifying model inputs vs. outputs: PrEP coverage and testing frequency 
Regarding your second point, we want to clarify that PrEP coverage and testing frequency are data-
driven inputs to ensure realism, not hypotheses or emergent outputs  requiring model verification in the 
way suggested. These inputs are based on observed real-world data: 
1) PrEP coverage: The scale-up is a direct input modelled using a logistic growth curve fitted to national 
data (Supplementary Figure 3B, attached as R10 ). Furthermore, our model realistically allocates 
new PrEP prescriptions preferentially to individuals with higher numbers of sexual partners, as detailed 
in Supplementary Section 1.2.4. 
2) STI testing frequency: Testing rates are data -driven inputs based on observed patterns 
(Supplementary Figure 11, attached as R14), assigned differently based on HIV/PrEP status. 
When these realistic inputs are combined, the STI incidence among PrEP users stabilizes as an emergent 
property, reflecting the complex interplay where prevention benefits are counterbalanced by targeting a 
population with higher underlying risk. 
In summary, the model does not assume STI stabilization; this trend emerges organically. We are grateful

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 159 -->

37 
for your questions, as they prompted us to add this detailed explanation to the supplementary materials Section 
2.3 “Calibration results”, improving transparency: 
“Our base case model projects a stabilization of future STI incidence among PrEP users and possible decline among 
population living with HIV (PLWH), which is not an imposed assumption but rather an emergent property of the model’s 
underlying data-driven inputs and calibrated dynamics. These trends could be explained by following information 
(1) The role of the PLWH population: The primary driver is a gradual, calibrated decline in the proportion of the 
PLWH. In our model, the higher STI burden among PLWH is explicitly driven by calibrated behavioural parameters 
(e.g., higher partnership rates, lower condom use), not the biological effects of HIV . The decline in the PLWH proportion 
is an outcome of population turnover (aging, mortality, emigration) that aligns with observed Australian data 
(Supplementary Figure 3); it is not the result of simulated  HIV transmission or ART effects. This demographic shift 
reduces the overall STI burden through several interconnected effects: 
a) A reduction in the size of this key, high-STI-burden subgroup. 
b) A “network bottleneck effect”, where the smaller PLWH population makes it more difficult to form new casual 
partnerships within their preferential (serosorting) network, subtly reducing transmission efficiency. 
c) A feedback loop in new entrants, where the STI prevalence assigned to new PLWH immigrants is based on the 
current, stabilizing prevalence of the existing PLWH population. 
(2) Data-driven inputs: PrEP Coverage and STI Testing: Other key factors, such as PrEP coverage and testing 
frequency, are data-driven inputs, not emergent outcomes. The scale -up of PrEP is based on a logistic growth curve 
fitted to national data (Supplementary Figure 3), with new prescriptions realistically targeted to individuals with higher 
partner numbers (Supplementary Section 1.2.4). Similarly, testing rates are based on observed patterns (Supplementary 
Figure 12). The model’s emergent outcome shows that despite these prevention scale -ups, STI incidence among PrEP 
users only stabilizes rather than strongly declining, reflecting the complex interplay between effective prevention tools 
and their targeted use in a population with higher underlying behavioural risk.”

Our comment has been addressed, assuming figures R16 and R17 include empirical data. This is not 
completely clear in the figures. Could the authors more clearly indicate which values in figures R16 and R17

(attached as Figures R16 and 17) to explicitly state which values are empirical and cite their data source 
(Gay community periodic survey).

Comment 4 (Re Comments 9-15 (Sensitivity Analysis), Primary comment (required)): 
We appreciate that the authors extend ed the sensitivity analyses to additional parameters. For each 
parameter, they considered a range between 0.5 and 1.5 times the value in the main analysis. However, this 
results in very different effective parameter ranges. For example, condom use is varied from 0.25 to 0.75 (0.5 
+/-50%, while the probability that tetracycline resistance emerges upon Doxy-PEP use varies from 0.000675 
to 0.002025 (0.00135 +/ - 50%). Given that both these parameters are technically bound by 0 and 1, very 
different ranges of the parameter space are considered. 
Furthermore, in the last version of the model, the resistance emergence parameter was calibrated to a 
value of 0.05, which is not even included in the range of values considered in the sensitivity analysis. Ranges 
for parameters such as the treatment failure rate are similarly narrow. The authors write in their response to 
reviewers: “For instance, a study (2014, PMID: 25371490) investigated the persistence of NG DNA following 
standard treatment. They found that while overall DNA persistence at day 14 post -treatment was around 8% 
for both pharyngeal and rectal infections, for a small subgroup of isolates (n=10) with elevated MICs for both 
ceftriaxone (≥0.06 mg/L) and azithromycin (≥0.5 mg/L), DNA persistence was notably higher at 30%. This

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 162 -->

40 
30% persistence in a specific, potentially harder -to-treat subgroup (though based on a small sample ) is 
considerably higher than our model ’s calibrated TF rate of 7.6% for strains with ceftriaxone DS (where 
ceftriaxone DS is defined by ceftriaxone MIC >0.06 mg/L, without a concurrent azithromycin resistance 
criterion for the TF probability itself). This  comparison suggests our calibrated TF rate is within a plausible 
range, potentially on the conservative side relative to specific high -risk scenarios reported in literature. ” 
However, the range of values they consider in the sensitivity analysis is 0.038 to 0.114 (0.076 +/ - 50%). If 
they base their value on the 2014 study, why not cover the full range of values in that study in the sensitivity 
analysis, including a 30% treatment failure rate? 
In the main text and in the reviewer response, the authors compa re the impact of variations in different 
parameters, which is misleading, given the varying ranges of parameter values. For example, in the main text, 
lines 243ff: “Our comprehensive one -way sensitivity analyses highlighted that the assumed condom use 
frequency among individuals who “sometimes” use condoms (varied 25%–75%; base case: 50%) was the 
most influential parameter across the four key outcomes.” Also in the main text, lines 170ff: “While the results 
were generally robust to variations in most parameters, the assumed condom use frequency among individuals 
who “sometimes” use condoms (varied 25%–75%) was the most influential factor on all four key outcomes.” 
However, our sense is that the current sensitivity analysis does not allow for this kind of com parison, for the 
reasons described above. 
Finally, the authors interpret the impact of changes in parameter values using qualitative terminology, 
such as describing effects as “modest”, “moderate”, or “substantial” or stating that results are “robust” to 
variation in parameters. For example, in their response to reviewers, the authors write: “Our findings indicate 
that changing this doxy -PEP-induced resistance probability within the tested range had a generally modest 
impact on overall STI reductions and be nefit-cost ratios. As expected, it had a more discernible, though still 
moderate, influence on the projected increase in HL TetR gonorrhoea (Figure R10). ” We recommend 
providing either definitions for these terms or, preferably, quantitative results, such as the difference in the 
number of infections averted. 
Response: 
We are extremely grateful to the reviewer for this insightful and methodologically rigorous critique. We 
agree with the reviewer ’s points regarding the significant limitations of our previous  sensitivity analysis 
approach and interpretation. Their critical feedback has prompted us to further improved our one -way 
sensitivity analysis methodology and reporting. 
Our response addresses your comments in three sections:  (1) detailing the new, justified ranges; (2) 
specifically addressing concerns about the resistance emergence and treatment failure parameters; and 
(3) describing the revised interpretation and reporting. 
1) Part 1: Justification for new parameter ranges 
We now use four distinct methods to set parameter ranges . We now use four distinct methods to set 
parameter ranges, as detailed in the revised supplementary text (which you have requested we provide). 
a) Literature-Based Ranges: For parameters where evidence exists, we now use the full plausible range 
from the literature. This includes: 
i. Pathogen-specific efficacies: (e.g., 13–66% for gonorrhoea, from 95% CIs of a meta-analysis). 
ii. Proportion of symptomatic gonorrhoea:  (0–90%, based on literature reporting low extra -
genital and high urethral symptomatic rates).

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 163 -->

41 
iii. Duration of asymptomatic gonorrhoea: (3–50 weeks, based on natural history studies). 
iv. Probability of treatment failure:  (0–30%, directly addressing the reviewer ’s point  (in the  
following parts) by using the 30% upper bound from PMID: 25371490). 
b) Logical/symmetric ranges: For parameters with a hard 100% cap, we used symmetric ranges around 
our base case (e.g., uptake of doxy-PEP: 50–100%; adherence of using doxy-PEP: 60–100%). 
c) Wide exploratory ranges: For the highly uncertain de novo resistance parameters (probability of HL 
TetR development per doxy -PEP use, and background spontaneous HL TetR development rate), we 
have replaced the flawed +/- 50% range with a much wider, more appropriate exploratory ran ge of 0 
to 10 times the base values. This wide range is justified because: 
i. There is significant uncertainty surrounding these parameters. 
ii. One previous modelling study, for instance, used an upper limit for the doxy -PEP-induced 
resistance risk that was lower than our calibrated initial values. 
iii. Therefore, exploring up to 10 times our base value ensures we cover a broad spectrum of 
possibilities, including potentially much higher rates than previously considered, allowing for a 
conservative assessment of the model’s sensitivity. 
d) Proportional ranges (for grouped parameters only): We retained the 0.5–1.5 multiplier exclusively 
for two specific parameter sets due to their unique characteristics: 
i. The six parameters representing the “Proportions of individuals who ‘always use condoms’” 
(stratified by 3 population subgroups and 2 partnership types). 
ii. The two parameters controlling the “Annual number of casual partners (ANCP)” for HIV-
positive individuals and PrEP users. 
iii. Justification: This proportional approach was necessary because the baseline values within each 
set are highly heterogeneous (e.g., condom use ranging from 6.1% to 42.1%). An absolute change 
would disproportionately affect these diverse baselines. The proportional change ensures a 
consistent test of sens itivity relative to each subgroup ’s distinct starting point, preserving their 
relative differences. 
We have now updated the “One-way sensitivity analyses” section in the supplementary materials to detail 
these new ranges and their justifications. The updated tornado figures were attached as Figures R10-13 (also 
a copy of them below). 
“The key parameters varied in these sensitivity analyses, along with their tested ranges and justifications, 
included: 
1) Doxy-PEP intervention parameters:  
i. Prescription duration: Informed by the follow-up periods and prescription schedules in key clinical trials 
(e.g., Postexposure Doxycycline to Prevent Bacterial Sexually Transmitted Infections [PMID: 37018493]; 
Doxycycline Prophylaxis to Prevent Sexually Transmitted Infections in Women [PMID: 38657280]; and 
ANRS 174 DOXYV AC [PMID: 37419100]), we varied the duration from 3 to 12 months, would also 
lead to the prescribed doxy-PEP range from 26 to 104 doses (base case: 6 months). 
ii. Uptake rate among eligible individuals: Varied from a  symmetric lower bound of 50% to a hard upper 
bound of 100% (base case: 75%). 
iii. Adherence to taking doxy -PEP post-exposure: Varied from a symmetric lower bound of 60% to a hard

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 164 -->

42 
upper bound of 100% (base case: 80%). 
iv. Pathogen-specific efficacies of doxy -PEP: P lausible ranges were derived directly from the 95% 
confidence intervals of a recent meta-analysis: 
a) Gonorrhoea: 13% to 66% (base case: 45%). 
b) Chlamydia: 56% to 92% (base case: 81%). 
c) Syphilis: 64% to 86% (base case: 77%). 
v. Probability of HL TetR development pe r doxy -PEP use: This is a highly uncertain parameter. One 
previous modelling study used an upper limit of 10-4 for per-user risk, which is lower than our calibrated 
per-dose base values (1.9‰; [95%CI: 1.1-2.9‰]). To ensure a wide and conservative exploration, we set 
the range from 0 (no induction) to 10 times the calibrated initial values. 
2) STI natural history and epidemiological parameters: 
i. Proportion of new gonorrhoea infections that are symptomatic: We used a broad range from 0% to 90% 
(base case: 30%). T his reflects significant uncertainty in the literature, which notes high symptomatic 
rates (>90%) for urethral infections (e.g., as noted in NCBI Bookshelf: NBK558903) but very low rates 
for pharyngeal/rectal infections. A multi-country study also reported symptomatic rates in men as low as 
0-17% (PMID: 22461668). 
ii. Probability of ceftriaxone treatment failure for gonorrhoea with Ceftriaxone DS: Varied from 0% to 30%. 
The upper bound is informed by clinical data (e.g., PMID: 25371490) which observed 30% DNA 
persistence in a specific subgroup of isolates with elevated MICs, suggesting a plausible upper limit for 
treatment failure in resistant strains. 
iii. Background weekly rate of spontaneous HL TetR development (beyond 2025): To allow for a reasonable 
comparison with its counterpart (Probability of HL TetR development per doxy-PEP use) and to explore 
a wide range, we also varied this rate from 0 to 10 times its calibrated initial values (1.9‰; [95%CI: 1.1-
2.9‰]]). 
iv. Duration of  untreated asymptomatic gonorrhoea: Based on a systematic review and natural history 
studies on the duration of pharyngeal and rectal infections (PMIDs: 26886136, 34355734, 33513222), 
we varied the mean duration until natural recovery from 3 to 50 weeks. 
3) Behavioural parameters: 
i. Condom use frequency among individuals reporting “sometimes” using condoms: To explore the full 
impact of this parameter, we varied it across a wide plausible range from 1% to 99% (base case: 50%). 
ii. Proportions of individuals who “always use condoms ”: This parameter consists of six distinct baseline 
values, one for each of three population subgroups (HIV-positive, PrEP-using, PrEP-non-using) and two 
partnership types (casual, regular). These baseline values vary significantly across  subgroups (ranging 
from 6.1% to 42.1%, Supplementary Table 1). Applying a uniform absolute change (e.g., ±10 percentage 
points) would disproportionately affect these different baselines. Therefore, we applied a proportional 
change, multiplying all six initial values by 0.5 and 1.5 (i.e., ±50%), to ensure a consistent and fair test 
of sensitivity relative to each subgroup’s distinct baseline. 
iii. Annual number of casual partners of HIV+ individuals and PrEP users: The base values for these

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 165 -->

43 
parameters are sam pled from distinct Gamma distributions (Gamma(1.1, 25) for HIV -positive, 
Gamma(0.4, 20) for PrEP users). These parameters reflect differences in sexual contacts concentration, 
and further affected the concentration of infections ( Supplementary Figure 26-28). As specific range 
data is lacking, we tested the model ’s sensitivity to this concentration by applying a ±50% proportional 
change (multipliers of 0.5 and 1.5) to the sampled values for all individuals in these groups. This variation 
explores sensitivity to both the con tribution of high-activity groups to overall incidence and the overall 
concentration of infections. As detailed in Supplementary Figures 24-26, increasing the ANCP in these 
groups increased their proportional contribution to new infectio ns, but paradoxically decreased overall 
infection concentration (i.e., lowered the Gini coefficient), suggesting a wider dissemination of STIs 
throughout the network. 
4) Economic parameters: 
Annual discount rate for costs and QALYs: Varied from 0% to 6% (base ca se: 3%), representing a standard 
range used in health economic evaluations.” 
2) Part 2: Addressing specific parameter range concerns 
We thank the reviewer for identifying these significant issues with the ranges for two key parameters in 
our previous sensitivity analysis. Our revised approach directly addresses these concerns: 
a) Resistance emergence parameter: The reviewer correctly identified an inconsistency in our previous 
revision: the sensitivity range used for the background spontaneous HL TetR development rate did not 
include the value 0.05, which was an estimate from our original manuscript submission. This 
discrepancy arose due to the parameter’s evolution during the review process: 
i. First revision change: Following initial reviewer feedback, we refined the model by splitting 
this single parameter into two piecewise rates: one calibrated for the period before mid-2018 
and one for beyond mid-2018. Our current calibrated rates for these t wo periods are 0.30‰ 
(95% CI: 0.03-0.57‰) and 1.9‰; [95%CI: 1.1-2.9‰], respectively. The original 0.05% (i.e., 
0.5‰) value became obsolete as the direct base case once the parameter was split. 
ii. Previous sensitivity analysis: In the last revision, our sensitivity analysis focused on the post-
mid-2018 rate but applied a narrow +/- 50% range. 
iii. Current sensitivity analysis: We still focus the sensitivity analysis on the post-mid-2018 rate. 
However, acknowledging the significant un certainty and the reviewer’s point, we now use a 
much wider and more appropriate range of 0 to 10 times its calibrated base value . This 
resolves the previous issue of the narrow range. 
b) Treatment Failure Rate: We agree the previous range did not cover the 30% from PMID: 25371490. 
Our revised sensitivity analysis sets the range for this parameter to 0% to 30%, fully encompassing 
the literature value. 
Part 3: Revised interpretation of sensitivity analysis 
We appreciate this critical feedback and have revised our approach to interpreting and reporting these 
results. Specifically, the presentation and interpretation of the one-way sensitivity analysis  findings in the 
manuscript have been updated as follows: 
a) In the results section, the text now states:

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 166 -->

44 
“…Varying parameters within their justified ranges did not change the direction of the main outcomes: 
all strategies consistently reduced overall STIs and syphilis infections while increasing HL TetR gonorrhoea 
infections compared to the base case. However, the BCR could fall below 1 under specific parameter variations. 
Across the four key outcome indicators, the condom use frequency among individuals who “sometimes” 
use condoms was associated with the largest range of uncertainty. Varying this parameter across its range (1% 
to 99%) substantially altered the projected impacts compared to the initial projections. The reduction in overall 
STIs ranged from being 99.5% lower (at 99% condom use) up to 168.8% higher (at 1% condom use), and 
similarly, the reduction i n syphilis infections ranged from 99.3% lower (at 99% condom use) up to 190.5% 
higher (at 1% condom use). Conversely, the increase in gonorrhoea infections with HL TetR was affected in 
the opposite direction, ranging from being 99.7% lower (at 99% condom u se) up to 436.9% higher (at 1% 
condom use) relative to the initial projection, reflecting the diminished intervention impact at high condom 
use levels. Critically, increasing the condom use frequency to 99% pushed the BCR below 1 across all 
evaluated strat egies, with the BCR dropping as low as 0.04 (under the strategy targeting PrEP users). 
Additionally, decreasing the annual number of casual partners for HIV -positive and PrEP-using individuals 
to 0.5 times the base values also resulted in BCRs below 1 for the strategies targeting HIV-positive attendees 
and PrEP-using attendees.” 
b) In the discussion section, the text now states: 
“Our comprehensive one-way sensitivity analyses indicated that varying parameters across their justified 
ranges did not alter the fun damental findings regarding the direction of doxy -PEP’s impact: all strategies 
reduced overall STIs and syphilis while increasing gonorrhoea with HL TetR relative to the base case. Notably, 
the background condom use frequency among individuals who “sometimes” use condoms emerged as a 
critical factor influencing the magnitude of these impacts. Our results demonstrated that in simulated settings 
with higher condom use (e.g., varying the frequency for “sometimes” users up to 99%), the potential infections 
averted by doxy-PEP were substantially lower, while the projected increase in HL TetR gonorrhoea was also 
mitigated. Critically, these settings with higher condom use also pushed the BCR below 1 across all evaluated 
strategies, reaching as low as 0.04 f or the strategy targeting PrEP users. Furthermore, in simulated settings 
with lower sexual activity (decreasing the number of casual partners  among HIV-positive and PrEP users), 
the BCR also fell below 1 for strategies targeting these groups. Together, the se findings underscore that 
impacts and cost-effectiveness projections are highly dependent on the underlying behavioural context and 
network activity levels. This suggests that deploying doxy -PEP might yield greater net benefits in settings 
characterized by lower condom use or higher sexual activity, although these conditions could also correspond 
to larger relative increases in gonorrhoea with HL TetR.” 
In conclusion, we are deeply grateful for the reviewer ’s critique, which prompted a necessary and 
significant improvement. We are confident the revised approach —using justified ranges and focusing on 
quantitative uncertainty—has substantially improved the rigor, transparency, and validity of our sensitivity 
analysis.

Comment 5 (Re Comments 9-15 (Sensitivity Analysis), Secondary comments): 
The authors did not include the parameter governing natural clearance of gonococcal infections  in the 
one-way sensitivity analysis, arguing that the recent estimates for the duration of infection from Lindley 
Barbee’s studies (median 9 week duration of rectal infections and 16 weeks for pharyngeal infections) fall 
within their gamma distribution ce ntered around 33 weeks (with 95% CI of 16.5 -55.2 weeks). They write: 
“Because our model already incorporates this significant variability by sampling from this comprehensive 
distribution for each infection, a separate one-way sensitivity analysis on this parameter was not performed.” 
However, the studies by Barbee showed that the *median* duration of infection is shorter for pharyngeal and

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 171 -->

49 
rectal infections, not that short infections can occur in the tail of the distribution of durations. Shifting the 
gamma distribution in their model to consider distributions centered on shorter median durations may impact 
the analysis. It is therefore perplexing that the authors did not include this parameter in sensitivity analyses. 
In the response to reviewers, the authors write: “Regarding the concentration of infections: As a proxy to 
explore sensitivity to risk concentration, we varied the initial STI prevalence in the high-risk subgroups (HIV-
positive and PrEP -using individuals), multiplying the calibrated base -case values by 0.5 and 1.5. We found 
that variations in these initial subgroup prevalences had a generally modest impact on overall STI and syphilis 
reduction.” The starting prevalence of STIs should not have much impact on how concentrated infections are 
in the population after the burn -in period. Instead, the authors might consider changing the heterogeneity in 
contact rates in the population. How concentrated infection are should also be directly measured, for example 
by counting the number of people infected and the number of infections or calculating gini coefficients. 
Distribution of infections should be examined by groups targeted in the eligibility criteria and compared with 
real-world data. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for these insightful comments reg arding sensitivity analysis, particularly 
concerning the natural clearance parameter and the assessment of infection concentration. We address both 
points below.  We have addressed these points by explicitly including the duration of untreated 
asymptomatic gonorrhoea in our revised one-way sensitivity analysis (varying it across the full literature-
informed range), and by adopting the reviewer’s suggestion to assess sensitivity to infection concentration 
by varying partnership heterogeneity (annual number of  casual partners) and directly measuring the 
impact using multiple metrics. 
1. Sensitivity analysis for natural clearance of gonorrhoea 
We agree with the reviewer that incorporating the findings from the studies of Barbee et al. regarding the 
duration of untreated gonorrhoea is important. We appreciate the opportunity to clarify and refine our approach 
based on this feedback. 
In the previous version, we used a fixed Gamma distribution. We have now revised our methodology. We 
treat the  duration of untreated asymptomatic gonorrhoea  as a calibrated parameter , rather than a 
distribution sampled for each individual. The predefined range for this calibration was informed by the studies 
mentioned (Barbee et al., Clin Infect Dis 2021 & 2022) and the systematic review previously cited (Chow et 
al., Sex Health 2016), collectively suggesting a range from approximately 3 to 50 weeks . Our best -fitting 
parameter sets yielded a calibrated duration of 26.0 (95% CI: 13.7-38.5) weeks. 
Furthermore, acknowledging the uncertainty and potential impact, we have now explicitly included this 
parameter in our revised one-way sensitivity analyses, varying its value across the full literature-informed 
range of 3 to 50 weeks. The results are presented in the updated tornado plots (Figures R12–15). We believe 
this revised approach provides a more robust assessment. 
2. Sensitivity to infection concentration 
We agree with the reviewer that varying the initial STI prevalence is not an effective way to test sensitivity 
to infection concentration dynamics. We also agree that varying partnership heterogeneity is a more direct 
approach and that measuring concentration is crucial. 
To address this, we have taken the following steps: 
1) Varying number of casual partnership: As detailed in our response to Comment 4 and included in

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 172 -->

50 
our revised one-way sensitivity analyses , we now explicitly test sensitivity to partnership 
heterogeneity by applying a ±50% proportional change (multipliers of 0.5 and 1.5) to the Annual 
Number of Casual Partners (ANCP) of individuals in the high-activity subgroups (PLWH and PrEP 
users). The results of how this variation impacts the four main outcomes are included in the tornado 
plots (Figures R10-13). 
2) Measuring infection concentration: To directly investigate how varying the ANCP affects infection 
concentration, as the reviewer suggested, we conducted additional analyses evaluating several metrics 
under the base case and the ±50% ANCP scenarios: 
i. The proportion of new STIs occurring within the PLWH and PrEP user subgroups (Figure 
R15). 
ii. The ratio of unique individuals acquiring an infection each year to the total number of new 
infections (Figure R16). 
iii. The Gini coefficient for the distribution of new STIs across the population, calculated using the 
relative mean difference method (Figure R17). 
3) Findings on concentration: These analyses revealed a nuanced picture. While increasing the ANCP 
in high -activity groups does, as expected, increase the proportion of new infections occurring 
within those  groups (Figure R1 5), it paradoxically appears to decrease overall infection 
concentration. This is indicated by an increase in the unique persons/infection ratio ( Figure R16) 
and a decrease in the Gini coefficient (Figure R17) when ANCP is multiplied by 1.5. 
4) Interpretation: A plausible explanation is that increasing the partnership rates of these core 
groups enhances both their risk of acquisition and their potential for onward transmission. The 
latter effect may lead to wider dissemination of infections  throughout the network, thus reducing 
overall concentration, even as a larger fraction originates within those groups. This interpretation 
aligns with our previous analysis (response to Reviewer 1, Comment 9 during the last revision) 
highlighting the import ance of multi -generational transmission chains initiated by individuals with 
higher transmission potential. 
We believe this approach—varying ANCP as a proxy for exploring scenarios with different proportions 
of infections occurring within high -activity groups and different levels of overall infection concentration —
directly addresses the reviewer’s points. We have now clarified this dual interpretation in the supplementary 
text describing our sensitivity analysis: 
“Annual number of casual partners of HIV+ individuals and PrEP users: The base values for these 
parameters are sampled from distinct Gamma distributions (Gamma(1.1, 25) for HIV -positive, Gamma(0.4, 
20) for PrEP users). These parameters reflect differences in sexual contacts concentration, and further affected 
the concentration of infections ( Supplementary Figure 26-28). As specific range data is lacking, we tested 
the model’s sensitivity to this concentration by applying a ±50% proportional change (multipliers of 0.5 and 
1.5) to the sampled values for all individuals in these groups. This variation explores sensitivity to both the 
contribution of high -activity groups to overall incidence and the overall concentration of infections. As 
detailed in Supplementary Figures 26-28, increasing the ANCP in these groups increased their proportional 
contribution to new infections, but paradoxically decreased overall in fection concentration (i.e., lowered the 
Gini coefficient), suggesting a wider dissemination of STIs throughout the network.” 
We also added the new Figures R16 -17 as Supplementary Figure 26 -28. We are grateful for these 
comments, which have led to significant improvements in our sensitivity analysis methodology and reporting.
