Comment 6 (Re Comments 19): 
The scatterplot of the number of tetracycline resistant infections for varying Doxy -PEP uptake levels is 
helpful. 
The authors do not explain how the efficient frontier was calculated. 
The “multi-criteria evaluation framework” is helpful. However, the four single-outcome measures should 
be separated both visually and in the interpretation from the multi -criteria outcome measures. How were the 
combinations determined? Some combinations of outcomes are missing. This should be explained more 
clearly in the text. 
In the response to reviewers, the authors write: “Its effectiveness thus stems from efficiently targeting

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 176 -->

54 
high-transmission networks with a relatively modest number of prescriptions.” Did they verify this statement 
quantitatively in their model? For example, which proportion of persons with high partner change rates receive 
a Doxy-PEP prescription under the syphilis strategy relative to other strategies? 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for these detailed and insightful comments regarding our evaluation framework 
and efficiency analyses. We have addressed each of your points below. 
1. Explanation of efficient frontier calculation 
We have now added a description of how the efficient frontier was calculated (using the Minimum Slope 
Algorithm) to the captions of all relevant figures (Supplementary Figures 17-21). The sentence added is: 
“The solid line represents the efficient frontier, calculated using a minimum slope algorithm starting from 
the origin, which connects strategies offering the maximum benefit for a given cost.” 
2. Clarification of the multi-criteria evaluation framework 
We appreciate the feedback on the clarity of the multi-criteria evaluation framework and have made two 
key improvements: 
a) Visual separation: As suggested, we have revised Figure 4 (attached as Figure R1, also a copy below) 
to visually distinguish the single -, dual-, and triple-indicator frameworks using different background 
colours. 
b) Rationale for indicator combinations: Regarding potential missing combinations, our rationale was 
to include at most one of the two infection reduction indicators (overall STI or syphilis) in any 
combined framework. We agree this logic was not explicit and have added the following phrase to the 
Methods section 
“...Strategies were ranked under eleven policy prioritization frameworks, considering these four indicators 
singly or in pairwise/three -way combinations ( with each combination including at most one of the two 
infection reduction indicators).” 
3. Quantitative verification of the syphilis strategy’s efficiency 
We are grateful to the reviewer for challenging us to quantitatively verify our previous statement that the 
syphilis strategy ’s effectiveness stems from “efficiently targeting high -transmission networks. ” This was 
indeed a hypothesis that required verification from our model’s outputs. 
To investigate this, we conducted a new analysis to compare the average Annual Number of Casual 
Partners (ANCP) of individuals newly prescribed doxy -PEP under each of the five strategies  (Figure 
R18). Our analysis shows that the average ANCP of new users un der the syphilis -targeting strategy is not 
significantly different from that of users under the other strategies. 
This finding indicates that our previous hypothesis (that efficiency was driven by specifically targeting 
individuals with higher partner numb ers) is not supported by our model data . The high efficiency of the 
syphilis strategy is therefore likely attributable to other factors. As we detailed extensively in our response to 
Reviewer 1, Comment 9 during the previous revision round , this efficiency might be  driven by the 
interruption of multi-generational transmission chains. 
Consequently, we have removed the unsubstantiated statement regarding targeting high-transmission 
networks from the manuscript. We thank the reviewer for ensur ing our conclusions are strictly and

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 177 -->

55 
quantitatively supported by our model’s data. 
 
Figure R1 (a copy for convenience) . Doxy -PEP strategy rankings across different policy prioritization 
frameworks. This figure illustrates the relative ranking (from Rank 1  [most favourable] to Rank 5 [least favourable]) 
of five distinct doxy -PEP implementation strategies across eleven policy prioritization frameworks. The strategies 
(defined in the figure legend and detailed in Table 1) were evaluated based on their perform ance against four key 
indicators: (1) maximal reduction in overall Sexually Transmitted Infections (STI Reduc.); (2) maximal reduction in 
syphilis incidence (Syphilis Reduc.); (3) optimal outcomes concerning high -level tetracycline resistance in N. 
gonorrhoeae (HL TetR; i.e., minimizing its increase); and (4) maximal Benefit-Cost Ratio (BCR). The x-axis specifies 
each framework, showing rankings based on these indicators considered singly or in combinations. For combined -
indicator frameworks, rankings are based on a composite score derived from the sum of min-max normalized indicator 
values, as detailed in the Methods. Doxy -PEP=doxycycline post -exposure prophylaxis. STIs=sexually transmitted 
infections. Reduc.=reductions. HL TetR=high-level tetracycline resistance. BCR=benefit-cost ratio. PrEP=pre-exposure 
prophylaxis. 
 
Figure R18. Comparison of the Average ANCP among individuals newly prescribed doxy-PEP each year, by 
scenario (2024-2034). This figure displays the projected mean ANCP for individuals initiating doxy -PEP in each year 
from 2024 to 2034, stratified by the five different implementation strategies. The y -axis represents the average ANCP 
value calculated across all new users starting doxy-PEP within a given year under a specific strategy. The lines compare 
the risk profile (as proxied by ANCP) of individuals newly entering each program over time. This analysis was 
conducted to investigate whether strategies like the one targeting individuals with a syphilis diagnosis specifically select

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 178 -->

56 
individuals with higher baseline partnership numbers at the point of initiation. The figure indicates that the mean ANCP 
of individuals newly prescribed doxy-PEP under the syphilis-targeting strategy is not substantially different from those 
initiating under other strategies. All results shown represent the mean outcomes across 30 simulations for each of the 30 
best-fitting parameter sets. ANCP=annual number of casual partners. PrEP=pre -exposure prophylaxis. STIs=sexually 
transmitted infections. Doxy-PEP=doxycycline post-exposure prophylaxis. 
Comment 7 (Re Comments 22): 
It is highly unusual to run only a single simulation run for each of 100 parameter combinations, given the 
stochastic noise associated with individual-based models. Please consider running more iterations for each 
parameter combination to better capture the range of possible scenarios, including variation in contact 
networks, STI transmission, emergence of resistance, etc. 
Also, the labels on figures need to be corrected to read “average” for all outcomes. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this critical comment. We agree that running a single simulation for each 
parameter set was a significant limitation given the stochastic nature of our individual-based model. Our entire 
analysis has been revised to incorporate multiple simulation runs, and all figure captions and labels now 
explicitly describe reported outcomes as the “mean” (with 95% CI). 
As we detailed in our response to Comment 1, our entire analysis has been revised to incorporate multiple 
simulation runs at every stage. We have made the following key changes: 
1. Multiple runs for calibration: We now run each of the 10,000 parameter sets 30 times. The best -
fitting parameter sets are selected based on their aggregate performance  across these 30 runs, 
ensuring our selection process is not biased by a single “lucky” simulation. 
2. Multiple runs for final projections: All results presented in the manuscript are now based on the 30 
best-fitting parameter sets, with each of these sets being run an additional 30 times (for a total of 
900 simulations per scenario). 
To ensure this is transparent, we have thoroughly updated our Methods section. For example, the text now 
includes: 
“Using Latin Hypercube Sampling, we generated 10,000 unique parameter sets by drawing values from 
predefined ranges (Supplementary Table 2). Each set was run 30 times to ensure a robust performance 
evaluation, and the 30 sets minimizing the mean -squared differences between model outputs and calibration 
targets were selected for all subsequent analyses.  (PMID: 28367284 , and 
https://doi.org/10.1101/2025.06.24.25330172). …All model outputs are reported as the mean and 95% CI 
from simulations using these 30 selected sets, each run 30 times.” 
Finally, to address your second point, we have reviewed all figure captions and labels. All reported 
outcomes are now explicitly described as the mean of the multiple simulation runs, and this has been 
consistently applied throughout the manuscript and supplementary materials as: 
“All results shown are means (with 95% CI) across 30 simulations for each of the 30 best-fitting parameter 
sets”. 
We are grateful for your feedback, which has been instrumental in substantially strengthening the 
methodological foundation and robustness of our study. 
Comment 8 (Re Comments 34):

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 179 -->

57 
Please see our response to the prior comments 9 -15 mentioning the sensitivity analysis for risk 
concentration. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for referring back to this important topic. We confirm that this point regarding the 
sensitivity analysis for risk concentration has been addressed comprehensively in our detailed response 
to Comment 5 of this review round.  That response details the revised sensitivity analyses we conducted 
(varying the Annual Number of Casual Partners for high -activity groups) and the new metrics we calculated 
(proportion of infections within groups, unique persons/infection ratio, Gini coefficient) to directly investigate 
the impact on infection concentration. We hope that response, along with the corresponding revisions and new 
supplementary figures, fully addresses the reviewer’s concerns. 
Comment 9 (Re Comments 37): 
We recommend that the authors elaborate on the trade-off between minimizing ceftriaxone resistance vs 
doxycycline resistance in the main text, i.e., the fact that strategies that optimize ceftriaxone resistance produce 
higher doxycycline resistance (and dual resistance). 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this important suggestion to elaborate specifically on the trade-off between 
minimizing ceftriaxone resistance versus doxycycline resistance in the main text. 
Our model demonstrated: all doxy-PEP strategies are projected to decrease the cumulative incidence of 
infections with Ceftriaxone DS while simultaneously increasing the cumulative incidence of infections with 
HL TetR, compared to the base case (panel E of Figure 2, attached as Figure R19). 
To directly visualize and quantify this specific trade -off, we have conducted a new efficiency frontier 
analysis, plo tting the cumulative increase in HL TetR infections against the cumulative decrease in 
Ceftriaxone DS infections over the 10-year period (attached as Figure R20). 
This analysis clearly illustrates the cost in terms of increased HL TetR for each unit reduction achieved in 
Ceftriaxone DS. The strategy targeting individuals with a syphilis diagnosis lies on the most efficient part of 
the frontier, representing the point of minimum HL TetR increase per unit of Ceftriaxone DS reduction. Even 
for this strategy, t he slope indicates that averting one infection with Ceftriaxone DS is associated with an 
increase of at least 18.7 infections with HL TetR. 
We have incorporated this new analysis and figure into the manuscript. The Results section now presents 
this efficie ncy frontier plot, and the Discussion section interprets this specific trade -off, highlighting the 
substantial increase in tetracycline resistance associated with even modest reductions in ceftriaxone resistance 
through doxy-PEP. 
a) The revised text in Results section is as follows: 
“The efficiency frontier analysis shows the strategy targeting individuals with a syphilis diagnosis 
achieved the greatest reduction in Ceftriaxone DS per increase in HL TetR, with a ratio of 
approximately 18.7 additional HL TetR infections for every one Ceftriaxone DS infection averted 
along this efficient segment. (Supplementary Figure 17).” 
b) The revised text in Discussion section is as follows: 
“Second, the “one syphilis diagnosis” strategy stands out for its relatively favourable AMR profile. It 
yielded the smallest increase in HL TetR gonorrhoea and was the most efficient in trading increased

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 180 -->

58 
HL TetR for reduced Ceftriaxone DS. However, even this optimal strategy highlighted a steep trade-
off: averting one Ceftriaxone DS  infection was associated with at least 18.7 additional HL TetR 
infections, revealing the substantial ecological cost of using doxy-PEP.” 
 
Figure R 19. Impact of doxy -PEP on incident gonorrhoea infections across different antibiotic susceptibility 
statuses among GBMSM. Panels (A)-(D) show mean annual gonorrhoea incidence rates (per 100 person-years) under 
various scenarios, by antibiotic suscept ibility status: (A) “Neither” (exhibiting neither Ceftriaxone DS nor HL TetR); 
(B) “Ceftriaxone DS only”; (C) “HL TetR only”; and (D) “Both” (concurrent Ceftriaxone DS and HL TetR). Panel (E) 
projects 10 -year outcomes (2025 -2034): the left y -axis presents mean cumulative total gonorrhoea infections 
(thousands), by these four susceptibility categories. The right y -axis displays the mean cumulative increase in 
gonorrhoea infections exhibiting HL TetR (sum of “HL TetR only” and “Both” categories, in thousands) compared to 
the base case, with 95% CI error bars. All results shown are means (with or without 95% CI) calculated from simulations 
based on 100 parameter sets. GBMSM= gay, bisexual, and other men who have sex with men. STIs=sexually transmitted 
infections. Doxy -PEP=doxycycline post -exposure prophylaxis. PrEP=pre -exposure prophylaxis. DS=decreased 
susceptibility. HL TetR=high -level tetracycline resistance (used as a proxy for doxycycline resistance).

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 181 -->

59 
 
Figure R20. Efficiency frontier analysis of decrease in gonorrhoea with Ceftriaxone DS versus the increase 
in gonorrhoea with HL TetR.  Each data point represents a strategy compared with the base case, plotted by total 
decrease in gonorrhoea with Ceftriaxone DS (x-axis) and the cumulative increase in gonorrhoea with HL TetR (y-axis) 
over 10 years relative to the base case. The solid line represents the efficient frontier, calculated using a minimum slope 
algorithm starting from the origin, which connects strategies offering the maximum benefit for a given cost.  
STIs=sexually transmitted infections. HL TetR=high -level tetracycline resistan ce (used as a proxy for doxycycline 
resistance). 
Comment 10 (Re Comments 42): 
The comment is partially addressed. There is now a sentence mentioning the Traeger et al. paper, but the 
discussion of the paper is very limited. 
Response: 
To address this, we have expanded the discussion within the Discussion section, specifically within the 
paragraph evaluating the prioritization of the syphilis diagnosis strategy. To maintain the narrative flow 
while highlighting key contrasts, we integrat ed the comparison at this point, focusing on two major 
differences between our findings and those of Traeger et al.: the specific STI history criteria identified 
as most efficient, and the estimated efficiency of doxy-PEP (infections averted per prescription/person-
year). 
The revised text in the Discussion section now includes the following sentences: 
“Our findings on the efficiency of syphilis diagnosis -based targeting strategy align with Traeger et al., 
although their analysis favoured prescribing based on multiple recent STIs rather than syphilis diagnosis alone. 
Furthermore, their study estimated preventing one STI required over one person -year of doxy -PEP 
prescription, whereas our dynamic model projects each prescription course averts over two STIs, lik ely 
because it captures indirect effects from interrupting transmission chains.” 
We hope this expanded comparison, highlighting both the conceptual alignment and the important 
quantitative differences stemming from our dynamic modelling approach, now fully addresses the reviewer’s 
comment.

We thank the reviewer for acknowledging this revision. 
Comment 12 (Re Comments 45): 
The comment has been addressed. The explanation that there are insufficient data on stage-specific utility

Response: 
We thank the reviewer for finding our explanation convincing and for the excellent suggestion to state the 
rationale directly in the text. 
As suggested, we have revised the relevant sentence in the Limitations section to explicitly mention the

“Third, due to the lack of available Australian-specific, stage-specific quality-of-life data for these STIs, 
we applied published lifetime QALY losses per infection to simplify the assessment. Inc orporating stage-
specific data, should it become available, could yield more precise QALY estimates in future studies.” 
We appreciate this guidance in making our methodological choices clearer.

stage and the other individual j was in an infectious stage, and they had scheduled sexual acts within this cycle

Response: 
We thank the reviewer for their continued feedback on the supplementary materials and for highlighting 
specific areas requiring greater clarity. 
Regarding the general comment about vague references, we have reviewed our citations. We identified 
and corrected one journal article reference that had formatting errors. We surmise the comment may 
also refer to the web URLs used for specific national demographic data. These data were sourced from the 
Australian Bureau of Statistics ’ (ABS) interactive “Data Explorer ” module (main site: 
https://www.abs.gov.au/), which requires specific filters to isolate the precise datasets we used. The URLs 
provided in our references are direct links generated by applying these exact filters within the ABS Data 
Explorer. While they contain specific query codes, they represent the most direct and replicable way for 
readers to access the exact data underpinning our demographic assumptions. We have ensured these are 
clearly cited as formal references. 
We understand the reviewer’s broader concern about the length and structure of the supplement. We will 
now address each of the specific points (1-7) raised to improve clarity and conciseness where possible. 
(1) Clarification of parameters: 
We agree with the reviewer that this sentence lacked the necessary specific values. To improve clarity, we 
have revised the text to explicitly state the calibrated value for the probability of resistance development (κ₂). 
The revised sentence in the supplementary materials now reads: 
“… For the second impact, we applied the probability κ₂ (0.19% [95%CI: 0.11-0.29%]) derived from 
model calibration to represent the likelihood of HL TetR development with each use of doxy-PEP.” 
We believe this revision provides the necessary detail for understanding the model parameters. 
(2) Clarification of “HIV positive rate”:

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 184 -->

62 
We thank the reviewer for identifying the ambiguity in the description of this parameter in Supplementary 
Table 3. 
The parameter refers to the HIV prevalence within the total simulated GBMSM population , not an 
incidence rate. Crucially, this is a time-varying characteristic of our simulated population, not a fixed input 
parameter. Our model was initialized with an HIV prevalence of 9.5% in 2012, and the subsequent trend 
emerges from the model ’s demographic dynamics (population turnover) and is cali brated against observed 
Australian data. 
To improve clarity, we have revised the description in Supplementary Table 3. The entry now reads: 
“HIV prevalence (among total population): Time-varying; calibrated to observed data (Initial value 9.5% 
in 2012, decreases over time, see Supplementary Figure 3 for details).” 
Supplementary Figure 3 clearly shows the comparison between the observed national HIV prevalence 
trend and our model’s simulated output, providing the full details of this dynamic parameter: 
“Figure 3. Simulation and calibration of the changing HIV prevalence and PrEP coverage over time.  
Panel (A) shows the observed annual HIV prevalence among Australian GBMSM from the GCPS for 2012 -
2023 (blue points) and the corresponding mean HIV average prevalence emerging from our model simulations 
(red points). The model was initialized with 9.5% HIV prevalence in 2012, and the subsequent trend is driven 
by the model’s dynamic population turnover rather than an imposed external trend…” 
We believe this revision clarifies the meaning and nature of this parameter. 
(3) Clarification of “repetitive” text: 
We thank the r eviewer for their careful reading and agree that there was redundancy. We have now 
removed the duplicated sentence  in Section 1.5 that reiterated the validation of the model ’s demographic 
engine. The remaining text clearly distinguishes between the validation evidence (now stated once) and the 
subsequent description of the initialization process for new individuals entering the simulation, ensuring both 
necessary points are made concisely. 
(4) Clarification of infectious stages: 
We thank the reviewer for  requesting clarification on which specific disease stages contribute to 
transmission. 
We believe this information is provided in the paragraph immediately preceding the text cited by the 
reviewer. In that paragraph, we explicitly define the infectious stages for each STI used in the model. 
For the reviewer’s convenience, the relevant text defining the infectious stages reads: 
“We defined the infectious stages of each disease as follows: (1) gonorrhoea: infectious stages, either 
symptomatic or asymptomatic; (2) chlamydia: infectious stages, either symptomatic or asymptomatic; and (3) 
syphilis: primary syphilis (symptomatic or asymptomatic), first early latent syphilis, secondary syphilis 
(symptomatic or asymptomatic), second early latent syphilis, and late latent syphilis.” 
These are precisely the stages used to identify potentially transmitting individuals (individual j) in the 
partnerships considered for transmission risk calculation described in the subsequent paragraph. We hope this 
clarification resolves the reviewer’s concern. 
We thank the reviewer for requesting clarification on which specific disease stages contribute to 
transmission.

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 185 -->

63 
(5) Clarification of MIC threshold text: 
We thank the reviewer for pointing out that this sentence, while important, might seem misplaced and 
lacks details about the calibration process itself. 
The purpose of including the MIC threshold definitions (Ceftriaxone DS >0.06 mg/L; HL TetR >8 mg/L) 
in this section ( “Overview of gonorrhoea model ”) was primarily to define the fundamental categories of 
antimicrobial susceptibility  used within the model structure itself. These definitions are crucial for 
understanding the different gonorrhoea states simulated. 
However, we agree with the reviewer that this sentence does not belong to the calibration methodology 
description and could be confusing. To address this: 
1. We have retained the MIC definitions within the model overview section, as they are essential for 
defining the model states. 
2. We have removed any implication that this sentence describes the calibration process. 
3. We have ensured that the dedicated “Model calibration” section contain the full, detailed description 
of the calibration methods, data sources, parameters, and targets, as requested by the reviewer in their 
other comments. 
We believe this clarifies the purpose of the MIC definitions and ensures that the calibration process is 
described comprehensively in the appropriate sections. 
(6) Clarification of parameter descriptions: 
We thank the reviewer for identifying the redundancy in our description of the parameters governing the 
scheduling of sexual acts. 
The reviewer is correct that the parameters were described twice in close succession. To improve clarity 
and conciseness, we have removed the initial descriptive list of factors influencing the probability of a sexual 
act.

We thank the reviewer for their feedback regarding the overall clarity and structure of the Supplementary

Materials. While specific points for revision beyond those above were not detailed, we acknowledge the

a) Added a list of abbreviations: A comprehensive list of abbreviations used throughout the document

Response to the comments from Reviewer 1 
Summary: 
The authors have made extensive efforts to address my comments on the manuscript and the 
supplementary materials for the second time. Their responses are generally thorough, and the added 
explanations and edits have strengthened the overall quality of the work. Only a few minor issues remain to 
be addressed, primarily related to formatting. 
Response: 
We sincerely thank the reviewer for their positive assess ment of our revised manuscript and for 
acknowledging the efforts made in the previous round of revision. We are delighted to hear that the reviewer 
found our responses thorough and that the edits have strengthened the overall quality of the work. We have 
carefully addressed the remaining minor issues regarding formatting and inconsistencies as detailed below. 
Key points in our response are formatted in bold, and any new or revised text added to the manuscript or 
supplementary materials in this revision round is quoted and highlighted in blue for clarity. 
Comment 1 (Re Minor Comment 1): 
The authors have reviewed the use of acronyms and have corrected most of them. However, there are a 
few issues remaining: (1) SCC was only defined in Supplementary Figure 4, b ut not in the text of the 
supplementary materials; (2) CI was not defined in Section 1.2.3 of the supplementary materials as the authors 
mentioned; (3) Full terms were used after acronyms were defined (e.g., infections averted instead of IA was 
used in the  Discussion section, and Neisseria gonorrhoeae was used in the Supplementary materials). In 
addition, is it appropriate to use confidence interval, which is commonly used to estimate an unknown 
statistical parameter, to represent the uncertainties of simulated results (PMID: 35255860)? 
Response: 
We sincerely thank the reviewer for the rigorous check and for raising the important discussion regarding 
statistical terminology. We have addressed these points comprehensively: 
1. Statistical terminology (confidence interval vs. uncertainty interval):  
We have carefully considered the reviewer ’s comment and the recommended reference (PMID: 
35255860). We note that the referenced study employed Bayesian inference methods, for which “credible 
intervals (CrI)” are the appropriate metric. Given that our study utilizes a stochastic individual -based model 
with Latin Hypercube Sampling rather than a strict Bayesian framework, CrI may not be the most applicable 
term.  
Instead, we have adopted the term “uncertainty interval (UI) ”, which is widely accepted in 
mathematical modelling studies to describe ranges derived from simulation outputs and parameter uncertainty 
(e.g., see PMIDs: 34245682, 34596018, 27780211). 
Accordingly, we have globally replaced “95% confidence interval (CI)” with “95% uncertainty interval 
(UI)” throughout the main text and Supplementary Materials. We have ensured that UI is explicitly defined at 
its first occurrence in the main text (Results section) and in the Supplementary Materials (e.g., Supplementary 
Figure 15 caption). 
2. Acronym definitions and usage: 
(1) SCC: We acknowledge the reviewer ’s note. We have now explicitly added the acronym of

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 190 -->

6 
seroconcordance in the text of Supplementary Section 1.1.1 upon its first mention. 
(2) IA & NG: We have conducted a thorough proofread to ensure that IA (infections averted) and NG (N. 
gonorrhoeae) are defined at their first usage in both the Main Text and Supplementary Materials, and that the 
abbreviated forms are used consistently thereafter. 
Comment 2: 
Multiple revisions were not marked in red in the manuscript or the supplementary materials. For example, 
the second sentence that was added to the manuscript based on Minor Comment 4, and the revisions in 
Supplementary Table 3 based on Minor Comment 21. 
Response: 
We sincerely apologize for this oversight regarding the formatting of the revised documents. We fully 
understand the importance of clearly highlighting changes to facilitate the review process. 
In response to this comment, we have conducted a comprehensive line-by-line comparison between 
the initial and revised versions of both the Manuscript and the Supplementary Materials. 
(1) Restoring missing marks: We have ensured that all revisions from the previous round (R2) are now 
consistently marked in red. This includes the specific examples noted by the reviewer (e.g., the sentence added 
for Minor Comment 4 and updates to Supplementary Table 3) as well as all updates to numerical results and 
calibrated parameter values. 
(2) Highlighting new changes: To distinguish the changes made in this current round (R3)  from the 
previous ones, all new revisions made in response to this round of comments are marked in blue text. 
We believe this colour-coded approach will allow for a clear and efficient verification of all modifications. 
Comment 3 (Re Minor Comment 6): 
Based on the revised Figure 2, it is unclear whether the trade -off effect between the increased gonorrhea 
infections with HL TetR and the decreased infections with Ceftriaxone DS would sustain beyond 2034, since 
the incidence rates of infections with both resistances were much higher than those with only Ceftriaxone DS 
by 2034. In addition, the CIs for the decreased infections with Ceftriaxone DS seem comparable across all 
intervention strategies by 2034. It is important to emphasize the temporality of the results, or choose a different 
outcome for comparison. 
Response: 
We thank the reviewer for this insightful observation regarding the long-term dynamics of resistance. We 
agree that the rapid expansion of strains exhibiting concurrent resistances poses a significant challenge to the 
sustainability of the intervention’s benefits beyond the projected horizon. 
As the reviewer correctly noted, the incidence of infections with both Ceftriaxone DS and HL TetR is 
projected to rise significantly. Our analysis confirms that because doxy-PEP offers no protection against these 
strains, their growth eventually undermines the population-level reduction in Ceftriaxone DS. 
Therefore, we fully accept the reviewer’s suggestion to emphasize the temporality of the results. We have 
revised the Discussion section (specifically , in the paragraph discussing AMR trade -offs) to explicitly 
acknowledge that the observed benefits are likely confined to the 10 -year projection window due to this 
replacement dynamic. The added text is as follows: 
“...suggesting that doxy-PEP may select for HL TetR  in some strains already exhibiting Ceftriaxone DS

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 191 -->

7 
rather than eliminating them. Crucially, our projections show that the incremental rise in infections exhibiting 
both Ceftriaxone DS and HL TetR gradually outpaces the reduction in infections with Ceftriaxone DS alone. 
Since doxy -PEP confers no protection against strains with such concurrent resistances, their expansion 
progressively negates the suppression of Ceftriaxone DS, implying that the observed trade -off benefits are 
strictly limited to the 10-year timeframe. Broader AMR concerns include...” 
Comment 4 (Re Minor Comment 11): 
In the revised Figure 3, “(D)” still slightly overlaps with “Two”. 
Response: 
We sincerely apologize that this formatting oversight persisted in the previous revision. We appreciate the 
reviewer’s keen attention to detail. 
We have now carefully re-adjusted the layout of Figure 3 to ensure clear spacing and zero overlap 
between the panel label “(D)” and the title. Furthermore, following this comment, we conducted a 
systematic check of all figure labels throughout the manuscript and proactively adjusted potential overlaps in 
other figures (e.g., Figure 2, Panel D) to ensure consistent clarity. Please refer to the corrected Figure 3 in the 
revised manuscript (also provided here as Figure R1 for ease of verification). 
 
Figure R1. Annual proportions of incident gonorrhoea infections with different antibiotic susceptibility statuses 
across various scenarios, 2024 to 2034. Each panel (A-F) corresponds to a specific scenario, illustrating the changing 
annual proportions of four NG antibiotic susceptibility statuses among incident infections over the period shown. The 
stacked areas represent infections characterized by: neither Ceftriaxone DS nor HL TetR; Ceftriaxone DS only; HL TetR 
only; and both Ceftriaxone DS and HL TetR. Percentage labels on the left of each panel denote these respective 
proportions for 2024 (the year immediately preceding the 10-year intervention period), while labels on the right denote

<!-- GEAR_PAGE: 192 -->

8 
proportions for 2034 (the final year of the 10 -year intervention period). All results shown are means across 30 
simulations for each of the 30 best-fitting parameter sets. DS=decreased susceptibility. HL TetR=high-level tetracycline 
resistance (used as a proxy for doxycycline resistance). Doxy -PEP=doxycycline post -exposure prophylaxis. 
STIs=sexually transmitted infections. PrEP=pre-exposure prophylaxis. 
Comment 5 (Re Minor Comment 1): 
Re Minor Comment 24: The authors clari fied that doxy-PEP coverage was defined as the proportion of 
all GBMSM using doxy-PEP, rather than as the proportion among eligible individuals. However, inconsistent 
descriptions as doxy -PEP coverage percentage among eligible individuals can still be foun d in the titles of 
Figure 1 and Supplementary Figure 16. 
Response: 
We sincerely thank the reviewer for their meticulous attention to detail and for catching this inconsistency. 
We apologize for the oversight in updating these figure captions in the previous revision. 
We have now corrected the captions for both Figure 1 and Supplementary Figure 16 to accurately 
reflect that the reported coverage is calculated as a proportion of the total GBMSM population, 
consistent with our definition. 
Revisions: 
1. Figure 1 legend (Panel F): 
“Panel (F) depicts the mean annual doxy-PEP coverage percentage among all GBMSM.” 
2. Supplementary Figure 16 legend: 
“The x-axis shows the projected average annual doxy-PEP coverage among all GBMSM.”
